                    |   |   +---------------+   |   |
                    |   |                       |   |
                    |   +-----------------------+   |
                    |                               |
                    +-------------------------------+

  0 m ------------- 100 m ------------------------- 300 m
  |                  |                               |
  MONUMENT       end of prohibited            end of regulated
  ITSELF         area                          area

  WHAT THE RULE IS IN EACH BAND:
  PROHIBITED AREA   construction and allied activity is generally NOT
                    permitted; the band exists to keep the monument's
                    immediate surroundings clear
  REGULATED AREA    construction, reconstruction, repair and renovation
                    may be permitted, but only with prior PERMISSION,
                    on the recommendation route laid down under the Act
  BEYOND            ordinary planning law applies; this statute does not
                    reach

  WHO DECIDES:
  COMPETENT AUTHORITY   receives and processes applications; enforces the
                        prohibition; applies the heritage bye-laws
  NATIONAL MONUMENTS    the statutory authority created by the 2010
  AUTHORITY             amending Act; oversees the making and adoption of
                        heritage bye-laws and the permission architecture
  HERITAGE BYE-LAWS     monument-specific rules on conservation,
                        preservation and heritage-sensitive development,
                        which convert a uniform distance into a rule fitted
                        to THIS monument

  THE FIREWALL THAT MUST NEVER BE CROSSED:
  +--------------------------------+  +------------------------------+
  | PROHIBITED / REGULATED AREA    |  | WORLD HERITAGE BUFFER ZONE   |
  | source: Indian STATUTE         |  | source: the NOMINATION and   |
  | measured: from the protected   |  |   MANAGEMENT documents under |
  |   limits, in metres            |  |   the 1972 Convention        |
  | decided: by the competent      |  | mapped: property by property |
  |   authority under the Act      |  | decided: through the         |
  | effect: a permission regime    |  |   nomination process         |
  |   enforceable in Indian law    |  | effect: an international      |
  |                                |  |   management expectation     |
  +--------------------------------+  +------------------------------+
     THEY ARE DIFFERENT BOUNDARIES, FROM DIFFERENT INSTRUMENTS,
     AND MAY NOT COINCIDE ON THE GROUND.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Two numbers - one hundred and two hundred - are among the most frequently asked facts in this entire subject, and they are also among the most frequently misremembered, because candidates store them as a single number rather than as **two bands with two different rules**. The confusion has a second source: the same candidates have read that World Heritage properties have "buffer zones", and they merge the two ideas into one vague sense that there is a protected ring around important monuments.

Untangling this requires three separate statements. What the bands are. What rule applies in each. And why the domestic bands and the international buffer are different objects that happen to look similar on a plan.

### 2. The two bands, stated exactly

Around a **centrally protected monument**, the statutory regime creates two zones:

- the **first 100 metres** constitute a **prohibited area**, within which construction and allied activity are generally not permitted; and
- the **next 200 metres** beyond that - that is, from 100 metres to 300 metres - constitute a **regulated area**, within which construction, reconstruction, repair or renovation may be permitted, but only with prior permission under the statutory framework.

Three details prevent the standard errors.

**The 200 metres is additional, not total.** The regulated area is the *next* 200 metres. A candidate who reads "100 metres prohibited, 200 metres regulated" as meaning the regime ends at 200 metres has halved the reach of the statute.

**The prohibited band is not absolute in every conceivable case.** The general rule is that construction is not permitted; the statutory framework provides for limited exceptions of a public-works and repair character, subject to the prescribed approval route. An answer should say "generally prohibited, subject to the statutory framework" rather than "absolutely prohibited in all circumstances".

**The bands are extendable.** One widely used printed account states the position in a compressed form: the 1958 Act prohibits construction within a 100-metre boundary of a protected monument, and **this radius of 100-metre protection can be increased by government**. The bands are therefore a default, not a constitutional constant.

**A discrepancy recorded rather than resolved.** That compressed formulation states only the 100-metre prohibition and its extendability, and does not carry the further 200-metre regulated band or the permission architecture that goes with it. The fuller two-band account given here follows the statutory framework as it stands after the 2010 amending Act. The compression is recorded so that a reader who meets it is not surprised, and the two-band formulation is used in this session because it is the one the examination tests.

### 3. Who decides, and through what instrument

The zoning rule is only half the regime. The other half is the machinery that applies it.

**The competent authority** is the officer through whom the regime operates on the ground. Applications relating to construction, reconstruction, repair or renovation in the regulated area are made to and processed by this authority; the prohibition in the inner band is enforced through it; and it applies the monument-specific rules described below.

**The National Monuments Authority** is the statutory body created by the **Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains (Amendment and Validation) Act, 2010**. Its existence is the clearest single illustration of what that amending Act was for: the 1958 statute had a distance rule but no dedicated authority to administer it, and the 2010 Act supplied one. Its work centres on the framing and adoption of heritage bye-laws and on the permission architecture in the regulated area.

**Heritage bye-laws** are the element that converts a blunt instrument into a usable one. A uniform hundred-metre circle drawn around every monument in India is obviously crude: the appropriate treatment of the surroundings of a hilltop fort, a stepwell in a dense town, a riverside ghat and a rock shelter cannot be the same. Heritage bye-laws are monument-specific rules on conservation, preservation and heritage-sensitive development in and around the monument, framed with expert input and with the archaeological authority's involvement. They are where the regime stops being a distance and starts being a plan.

**One point of institutional interest, recorded as such.** The bye-law framing process is designed to draw on heritage expertise from outside government as well as inside, and the principal non-governmental heritage trust discussed in Lesson 9 is among the bodies associated with that consultative work. This is a useful detail because it is one of the few places where a non-governmental heritage body has a defined relationship to a statutory process.

### 4. Why the zone is the natural technique for immovable heritage

It is worth making explicit why the law reaches into the surroundings at all, rather than confining itself to the monument's own walls. The reason is that almost every threat to immovable fabric arrives **through its surroundings**:

- **vibration** from heavy construction and traffic transmits through ground into masonry;
- **altered drainage** caused by new building changes where water goes, and water is the principal agent of decay in most Indian monuments;
- **encroachment** occupies the space in which conservation work and visitor movement must happen;
- **visual setting** - the reason a fort looks like a fort - is destroyed by construction long before any stone is touched; and
- **fire and load risk** from adjoining structures reaches across a boundary that does not exist in physics.

A statute that protected only the fabric and not its setting would therefore protect nothing durable. That argument is the reason the regime is spatial, and it is a far better answer than reciting the two numbers.

### 5. Comparative treatment: the domestic zone against the World Heritage buffer

| | Prohibited and regulated areas | World Heritage buffer zone |
|---|---|---|
| Source | Indian statute - AMASR Act, 1958 as amended in 2010 | the 1972 Convention's nomination and management process |
| How it is fixed | a stated distance from the protected limits, extendable by Government | mapped individually for each property in the nomination dossier |
| Who applies it | competent authority, under the statutory authority's framework | the State Party, through the management arrangements it has undertaken |
| Legal effect | a permission regime enforceable in Indian law, with offences | an international expectation that the State Party manages the surroundings; enforcement, if any, is through domestic law |
| Applies to | every centrally protected monument, whether or not it is inscribed | only inscribed properties |
| Can they coincide? | they may overlap geographically, but they are separately determined and need not have the same boundary |

The last row is the examinable content. A World Heritage property in India may simultaneously have a statutory prohibited and regulated area under Indian law and a buffer zone under its nomination documents, and **the two boundaries are separately determined**. Substituting one for the other is a substantive error, not a simplification: it attributes the legal force of a domestic statute to an international management document, or denies a monument its statutory protection because it has no inscription.

### 6. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** A uniform hundred-metre prohibition is a bad rule. It is too restrictive in a dense historic city, where it sterilises land and imposes hardship on people who have lived there for generations, and too permissive for a large landscape monument whose setting extends for kilometres. Rigid distances are the wrong tool.

**Reply.** The objection identifies a real defect, and the statutory answer to it is the heritage bye-law: the uniform distance is a **default** that applies until monument-specific rules replace it with something fitted to the actual site. Three qualifications keep the reply honest. First, the default has a virtue the objection undervalues - it is **immediately operative and hard to argue with**, which matters in a country with a very large protected estate and limited administrative capacity; a regime that required a bespoke plan before any protection applied would protect nothing for years. Second, the objection's hardship point is genuine and is the reason the regulated band is a **permission** regime rather than a second prohibition: activity is contemplated, subject to control. Third, the defect the objection should press hardest is not rigidity but **coverage and enforcement** - a boundary that is not mapped, published and enforced on the ground has the same practical effect as no boundary at all, and that is a capacity problem rather than a drafting problem. The defensible position is: distances are a crude but necessary default, bye-laws are the intended correction, and the binding constraint is the capacity to map, publish and enforce.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies the **two bands with their two rules** - the single most asked pair of numbers in the topic - and the crucial detail that the 200 metres is **additional**. It supplies the **competent authority, the National Monuments Authority and heritage bye-laws** as three named elements of the machinery, which converts a numerical answer into an institutional one. It supplies the **why-a-zone argument** in section 4, which is the analytical content. And it supplies the **buffer-zone firewall**, which is a recurring trap and a guaranteed mark where it appears.

### Traps introduced here

- Reading the regulated area as ending at 200 metres from the monument rather than at 300.
- Describing the prohibited area as absolute without the statutory qualification.
- Forgetting that the bands may be increased by Government.
- Equating the statutory bands with a World Heritage buffer zone.
- Attributing the zoning regime to the 1958 Act alone, with no mention of the 2010 amending Act, the authority or the bye-laws.
- Reciting the numbers without the reason - vibration, drainage, encroachment, setting, fire and load.
- Assuming a non-inscribed monument has no protected surroundings.

### Revision notes

1. Around a centrally protected monument: the **first 100 metres** are the **prohibited area**; the **next 200 metres** - to **300 metres** - are the **regulated area**.
2. In the **prohibited area**, construction and allied activity are **generally not permitted**, subject to the statutory framework.
3. In the **regulated area**, construction, reconstruction, repair and renovation may be permitted **with prior permission** under the statutory route.
4. A widely used printed account records only that the 1958 Act **prohibits construction within 100 metres** and that the **radius can be increased by Government**; the fuller two-band account follows the statutory framework as amended in 2010, and the difference is recorded.
5. The **competent authority** processes applications and enforces the regime on the ground.
6. The **National Monuments Authority** is the statutory body created by the **AMASR (Amendment and Validation) Act, 2010**.
7. **Heritage bye-laws** are monument-specific rules on conservation, preservation and heritage-sensitive development; they convert a uniform distance into a fitted plan.
8. The regime is spatial because the threats are spatial: **vibration, altered drainage, encroachment, loss of setting, fire and load**.
9. **Statutory zone and World Heritage buffer are different objects**: different source, different method of fixing, different legal effect, and they need not coincide.
10. The statutory bands apply to **every centrally protected monument**, inscribed or not; a buffer zone applies only to an **inscribed property**.
11. The defect to press in an answer is **mapping, publication and enforcement capacity**, not the crudity of the distance.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"A boundary drawn on a map is only as good as the machinery that applies it." Discuss with reference to the protection of surroundings of centrally protected monuments in India. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The boundary is real and the machinery is the variable.

**The boundary.** Around a centrally protected monument the first **100 metres** form a prohibited area in which construction is generally not permitted, and the next **200 metres** form a regulated area in which construction, reconstruction, repair or renovation may be permitted only with prior permission. Government may extend the distance.

**Why a boundary at all.** Damage to immovable fabric arrives through its surroundings: vibration from construction, altered drainage, encroachment on working and circulation space, loss of visual setting, and fire and load risk from adjoining structures.

**The machinery.** The Amendment and Validation Act of 2010 supplied what the 1958 Act lacked: the **National Monuments Authority**, a **competent authority** to receive and decide applications, and **heritage bye-laws** that replace a uniform circle with rules fitted to the individual monument.

**The limit.** A zone that is not mapped, published and enforced produces the worst outcome of all - hardship for those inside it, and no protection for the monument. The distance is the default; the bye-law and the enforcement capacity are the substance.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the two bands stated exactly, with the regulated band correctly additional 3 + the mechanism-of-damage justification 2 + the three named elements of machinery 3 + the extendability of the distance 1 + the closing point that an unenforced zone imposes cost without benefit 1.** Writing "100 metres prohibited and 200 metres regulated" while implying the regime ends at 200 metres costs two marks, because the misreading is the exact thing being tested.

### Concept check

**Question:** A builder is told that a plot 250 metres from a centrally protected monument, which is also an inscribed World Heritage property, is "outside the UNESCO buffer zone and therefore free of restriction". Identify each error and state the correct position.

**Model answer:** There are two errors and one omission. The **first error** is the assumption that the World Heritage buffer zone is the operative restriction. It is not: the buffer zone arises from the property's nomination and management documents under the 1972 Convention, and whatever obligations it creates run to India as a State Party through its management arrangements. The restriction that binds a private builder is **Indian statute**. The **second error** follows from the first: a plot 250 metres from a centrally protected monument lies inside the **regulated area**, because the regulated area is the *next* 200 metres beyond the 100-metre prohibited area and therefore extends to 300 metres. Construction, reconstruction, repair or renovation there is not free; it requires prior permission through the competent authority under the statutory route, applying the heritage bye-laws framed for that monument. The **omission** is that the distance itself is not fixed for all time: Government may extend the protected radius, so a plot outside the default bands today may not be outside an extended band, and the correct step is to check the position for that specific monument rather than to reason from the general numbers. The correct position, stated in one sentence, is that the plot is inside the statutory regulated area, requires prior permission under Indian law, and its relationship to the property's buffer zone is a separate question that neither creates nor removes that requirement.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the international designation as the operative source of restriction and the domestic statute as background. In Indian law it is the other way round: the statute is what changes what a person may lawfully do, and the inscription changes India's obligations, not the builder's permissions.

   2. PROTECTION AND CONSERVATION preserve monuments, heritage sites
                                  and artefacts
   3. RESEARCH AND DOCUMENTATION  study and record sites, inscriptions

   4. RESTORATION AND MANAGEMENT  restore monuments and manage centrally
                                  protected heritage sites
   5. PUBLIC AWARENESS            publications, exhibitions and training
        |
        v
   THE ESTATE
   3,679 centrally protected monuments and archaeological sites
   (figure from the Survey's own live monuments page, accessed
    4 September 2026 - cite the figure WITH its access date)
        |
        v
   ADARSH SMARAK - a scheme of the Ministry of Culture under which the
   Survey identified 100 monuments for enhanced visitor amenities.
   Named examples include: prehistoric rock shelters of Bhimbetka
   (Madhya Pradesh); Group of monuments at Hampi (Karnataka); Sun
   Temple, Konark (Odisha); Elephanta Caves (Maharashtra); Shore
   Temple, Mahabalipuram (Tamil Nadu); Taj Mahal (Uttar Pradesh);
   Humayun's Tomb and Red Fort (Delhi); Hazarduari Palace and Adina
   Masjid (West Bengal).
   IT IS AN AMENITIES SCHEME. PROTECTION COMES FROM THE 1958 ACT.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

The Survey is so familiar a name that candidates skip it, and then cannot answer a question about it. The reason is that they hold it as a vague general agency for "old things" rather than as an institution with a founding date, a named first head, two governing statutes, a fixed list of functions, a defined estate and a set of specialised divisions. Every one of those is a potential item, and all of them together take ten minutes to learn.

There is also a structural point worth making at the outset, because it explains why this institution behaves as it does. The Survey is simultaneously a **research body** (it surveys, excavates and publishes), a **custodian** (it holds and maintains a very large monument estate), and a **regulator's arm** (it operates under statutes that create offences and permission regimes). Those three roles pull in different directions - research wants to open, custodianship wants to conserve, regulation wants to control - and most criticism of the institution is, at bottom, a complaint that one of the three roles has crowded out the others.

### 2. Origin and statutory basis

The position is stated compactly below and every clause is examinable. The Archaeological Survey of India is **directly under the aegis of the Ministry of Culture**, and it is the **foremost institution for archaeological research conducted across India**. Its focus is on the **preservation of the physical and tangible heritage** that has accumulated in India's ancient monuments and archaeological sites.

It was **established in 1861 under the leadership of Alexander Cunningham**, who is often considered the **father of Indian archaeology**, and who was **also appointed as the first Director-General** of the Survey.

It **functions under the Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains Act, 1958 and the Antiquities and Art Treasure Act, 1972** - the two statutes taught in Lessons 5 and 6. Note what that means structurally: the Survey is the institution through which **both** the spatial regime for immovable heritage and the transactional regime for movable heritage operate. It is the hinge between the two techniques, which is why an answer that separates the statutes must still bring them back together at this institution.

### 3. The five main functions, and why the list is more than a list

Five main functions are enumerated, and they are worth learning in this order because the order is a sequence of increasing commitment.

1. **Survey and excavation** - explore and dig archaeological sites.
2. **Protection and conservation** - preserve monuments, heritage sites and artefacts.
3. **Research and documentation** - study and record historical sites, inscriptions and findings.
4. **Restoration and management** - restore monuments and manage centrally protected heritage sites.
5. **Public awareness** - promote knowledge of India's cultural heritage through publications, exhibitions and training programmes.

Read them against Lesson 3's intervention ladder and something useful appears. Function 2 sits at the conservation rung; function 4 explicitly includes **restoration**, which is a higher rung and carries the interpretive burden analysed there. The same institution therefore performs both the stabilising and the restoring operations, which is precisely why the conservation-versus-restoration distinction is not an academic nicety in India: it is a distinction internal to a single agency's own mandate, and the choice between the two is made in-house.

Function 3 deserves separate emphasis for a different reason. **Documentation is a listed function of the Survey, not an administrative by-product.** That single fact is the institutional answer to the argument in Lesson 6 that inventory is the binding constraint on protecting movable heritage, and it is the answer to the dissociation risk identified in Lesson 3.

### 4. The specialised capacities

**The Central Archaeological Library.** Situated at the Survey's headquarters in New Delhi, with a collection of **more than 100,000 rare books and journals, plates and original drawings**. Two things make this worth remembering rather than skipping: it is itself a documentary-heritage collection of the kind discussed in Lesson 15, and the **original drawings** are frequently the only record of what a monument looked like before a nineteenth- or twentieth-century intervention - which makes them primary evidence for any restoration decision.

**The Underwater Archaeology Wing.** It is described as **one of the more specialised and one-of-a-kind divisions of the Survey**. This is the institutional answer to the underwater specialisation identified in Lesson 2: submerged sites need diving, survey and conservation capability that land archaeology does not possess, and a dedicated wing is how a national agency acquires it.

### 5. The estate, and the discipline that must travel with the number

The Survey's live monuments page states that it protects and maintains **3,679 centrally protected monuments and archaeological sites**, and that figure is recorded here as **accessed 4 September 2026**.

The discipline is absolute and should be carried into every answer. **This is a live figure, not a constant.** Monuments are declared and, occasionally, denotified; the number moves. A candidate who writes "the Survey protects 3,679 monuments" has stated as timeless something that is true only on a particular date. A candidate who writes "as recorded on the Survey's own monuments page, accessed in September 2026, the centrally protected estate comprises 3,679 monuments and archaeological sites" has done three things at once: given an exact figure, named the source, and demonstrated the citing discipline that the rest of the answer will be judged by.

The number also carries an analytical weight worth using. An estate of that size, held by a single agency that must also survey, excavate, research, document, restore, manage and run public programmes, is the concrete basis of the **capacity argument**: India's heritage architecture is institutionally rich and capacity-constrained, and the constraint is manpower, funding and enforcement reach rather than absence of law.

### 6. Adarsh Smarak, and the scheme-versus-statute firewall

The Survey **identified 100 monuments across India under the Adarsh Smarak scheme of the Ministry of Culture**, and the named examples include: the **prehistoric rock shelters of Bhimbetka** (Madhya Pradesh); the **Group of monuments at Hampi** (Karnataka); the **Sun Temple, Konark** (Odisha); the **Elephanta Caves** (Maharashtra); the **Shore Temple, Mahabalipuram** (Tamil Nadu); the **Taj Mahal** (Uttar Pradesh); **Humayun's Tomb** and the **Red Fort** (Delhi); and **Hazarduari Palace** and **Adina Masjid** (West Bengal).

The firewall must be stated every time this scheme is mentioned. **Adarsh Smarak is an amenities scheme.** Its object is enhanced visitor facilities at identified monuments. It is not a protection instrument, it creates no offence, and identification under it changes nothing about the monument's legal status. **Protection comes from the 1958 Act.** A candidate who writes that a monument "was protected under Adarsh Smarak" has described a scheme as a statute, which is the error Lesson 16 examines in full.

Three kinds of thing must never be described in one another's language:

- a **statute** creates protection, permissions and offences and can be enforced in a court;
- an **institution** carries a standing mandate and exists until it is wound up;
- a **scheme** is an executive programme that can be renamed, restructured, transferred between ministries or discontinued.

### 7. The colonial archaeologists: what the examination record requires and what this session can supply

The Survey's history is an examined subject in its own right, and the record is precise about which names appear. Alexander Cunningham is the founding figure and the first Director-General. **Sir Mortimer Wheeler** held the office of Director-General at the time of the Antiquities (Export Control) Act, 1947, as Lesson 6 records. Beyond those two, a directly owned Preliminary item from 2023 names **Alexander Rea, A. H. Longhurst, Robert Sewell, James Burgess and Walter Elliot** and asks what they were associated with.

**A declared gap, stated plainly.** No source held for this session carries an individual account of those five figures - their excavations, their regions, their publications or their offices. This session therefore records that the five names belong to the nineteenth- and early twentieth-century archaeological and antiquarian record in India and are routed to this topic by the examination ledger, and it supplies no biographical particular for any of them. The category-level reasoning that the item requires is the reasoning this lesson teaches: these are figures of the archaeological and epigraphic establishment of colonial India, the period in which the Survey was founded and its work organised, and they are not associated with the press, the church or the railways.

That last sentence is worth pausing on as a technique. When an item names several unfamiliar people and asks for a single category, the recoverable content is often **the category the names collectively belong to**, not the individual biographies. A candidate who knows that the Survey was founded in 1861, that its founding work was survey, excavation, documentation and epigraphy, and that this generated a body of named officers, can reason to the category without knowing any one of them.

### 8. Comparative treatment: what the Survey is, against what it is often assumed to be

| Assumption | Position |
|---|---|
| "The Survey owns the monuments" | It is the **custodian** under the 1958 Act. Custodianship is not ownership, and the 2010 amendment distinguishes **guardianship, purchase and lease** as three separate modes |
| "The Survey protects all Indian monuments" | It protects the **centrally protected** estate. State archaeology departments protect State-protected monuments, and very large numbers of significant structures are protected by neither |
| "The Survey grants permission for construction near monuments" | The permission architecture created in 2010 runs through the **competent authority** and the **statutory authority**, with heritage bye-laws; the Survey's involvement is real but it is not the sole decision-maker |
| "The Survey is a conservation agency" | It is a **research, custodial and regulatory** body at once, and the tension between those roles is the origin of most criticism of it |
| "Adarsh Smarak protects a hundred monuments" | Adarsh Smarak provides **visitor amenities** at identified monuments; protection is statutory |

### 9. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** Concentrating survey, excavation, research, conservation, restoration, management, public programming and a share of the regulatory function in one agency is a structural mistake. The body that excavates should not be the body that judges whether excavation was justified; the body that restores should not be the sole judge of whether restoration was appropriate; and the custodian of an enormous estate cannot also be a research institution of the first rank.

**Reply.** The structural criticism is serious and partly correct, and the correct part should be conceded: combining operational and evaluative roles does weaken independent scrutiny, and the creation of a separate statutory authority in 2010 for the permission architecture is itself an acknowledgement that not every function should sit in the same place. Three replies nonetheless stand. First, **integration has real benefits**: the officer who conserves a structure benefits from access to the excavation record, the original drawings in the library and the epigraphic material, and separating them can produce conservation that is technically competent and historically ignorant. Second, the criticism is often misdirected: the recurring failures reported about the Indian monument estate are overwhelmingly failures of **capacity** - manpower, trained conservators, funds, enforcement reach across 3,679 sites - rather than failures caused by role concentration, and a reorganisation that does not add capacity will not fix them. Third, the genuinely available reform is **procedural rather than structural**: documented decision-making, published conservation plans, external review of major restoration proposals and monument-specific bye-laws achieve much of what separation is meant to achieve without dismantling the integration that makes the work possible. The defensible position is that role concentration is a real weakness, capacity is the binding constraint, and transparency is the reform with the best ratio of benefit to disruption.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies the **highest-density set of citable particulars in the session**: 1861, Cunningham as father of Indian archaeology and first Director-General, the Ministry of Culture, the two governing statutes, the five functions, the library with its holdings, the Underwater Archaeology Wing, the Adarsh Smarak hundred with named examples, and the dated estate figure. It supplies the **scheme-versus-statute firewall**, which recurs throughout the topic. It supplies the **capacity argument** in its concrete form. And it supplies the **category-reasoning technique** for items that name unfamiliar archaeologists.

### Traps introduced here

- Saying the Survey owns monuments rather than holding them as custodian.
- Assuming the centrally protected estate is the whole of India's protected heritage.
- Describing Adarsh Smarak as a protection instrument.
- Giving the estate figure without its access date.
- Naming only the 1958 Act and forgetting that the Survey also functions under the 1972 antiquities statute.
- Attributing the Survey to a ministry other than Culture.
- Inventing biographical detail for colonial archaeologists whose records are not held.

### Revision notes

1. The **Archaeological Survey of India** is directly under the **Ministry of Culture** and is the foremost institution for archaeological research in India, focused on **physical and tangible heritage**.
2. Established **1861** under **Alexander Cunningham**, often considered the **father of Indian archaeology** and appointed its **first Director-General**.
3. It functions under the **Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains Act, 1958** and the **Antiquities and Art Treasure Act, 1972** - the hinge between the spatial and transactional regimes.
4. Headquarters **New Delhi**, with the **Central Archaeological Library** holding **more than 100,000 rare books and journals, plates and original drawings**.
5. It maintains an **Underwater Archaeology Wing**, one of its specialised and one-of-a-kind divisions.
6. **Five main functions:** survey and excavation; protection and conservation; research and documentation; restoration and management; public awareness.
7. **Documentation is a listed function**, not a by-product - the institutional answer to the inventory and dissociation problems.
8. **Adarsh Smarak**: a **Ministry of Culture scheme** under which the Survey identified **100 monuments** for **enhanced visitor amenities**. Named examples: **Bhimbetka, Hampi, Konark, Elephanta, the Shore Temple at Mahabalipuram, the Taj Mahal, Humayun's Tomb, the Red Fort, Hazarduari Palace and Adina Masjid**.
9. Adarsh Smarak is an **amenities** scheme; **protection comes from the 1958 Act**.
10. The Survey's live monuments page records **3,679 centrally protected monuments and archaeological sites**, **accessed 4 September 2026**. Always cite the figure **with** its access date.
11. **Sir Mortimer Wheeler** was Director-General at the time of the **Antiquities (Export Control) Act, 1947**.
12. **Alexander Rea, A. H. Longhurst, Robert Sewell, James Burgess and Walter Elliot** belong to the colonial archaeological and antiquarian record; **no individual particular for them is supplied or invented here**.
13. **Statute, institution and scheme** are three distinct kinds of thing; the Survey is an institution operating under statutes and running schemes.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"What measures has the Government of India taken for the protection and preservation of historic monuments, and what are the responsibilities of the Archaeological Survey of India in this regard?" Answer with reference to the statutory, institutional and scheme layers. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The measures fall into three distinct kinds and must not be described in one another's language.

**Constitutional.** Article 49 obliges the State to protect monuments, places and objects of artistic or historic interest, and requires that a monument declared of national importance by or under a law of Parliament be saved from spoliation, disfigurement, destruction, removal, disposal or export. Article 51A(f) lays a corresponding duty on every citizen.

**Statutory.** The chain runs from the Indian Treasure Trove Act, 1878 and the Ancient Monuments Preservation Act, 1904 through the Declaration of National Importance Act, 1951 to the Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains Act, 1958, which repealed the first two and Section 126 of the States Reorganization Act, 1956. The Amendment and Validation Act, 2010 added the power to declare monuments of ancient and medieval times of national importance, empowered the Director-General to assume guardianship, purchase or lease, and built the prohibited-and-regulated-area permission architecture with its authority, competent authority and heritage bye-laws. Movable heritage is governed separately by the Antiquities and Art Treasures Act, 1972.

**Institutional and scheme.** The Survey, established in **1861** under Alexander Cunningham and functioning under the 1958 and 1972 Acts, is custodian of the centrally protected estate - **3,679** monuments and sites on its own page, accessed September 2026. Its five responsibilities are survey and excavation; protection and conservation; research and documentation; restoration and management; and public awareness. Above these sit executive schemes: **Adarsh Smarak**, providing amenities at **100** identified monuments, and participation and funding mechanisms.

The architecture is complete on paper; its constraint is conservation manpower, funds and enforcement reach.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the three-kinds framing stated before any content 3 + Article 49 with its six verbs 3 + the statutory chain with the 1958 repeals and the 2010 additions 5 + the Survey's date, founder, statutes and five functions 4 + the dated estate figure cited with its access date 2 + Adarsh Smarak correctly identified as an amenities scheme 2 + the capacity conclusion 1.** Describing Adarsh Smarak as a protection measure costs three marks, because it collapses the three-kinds framing the answer opened with.

### Concept check

**Question:** An article states that "the Archaeological Survey of India owns and protects every ancient monument in the country, and has upgraded a hundred of them to protected status under a national scheme." Correct every clause.

**Model answer:** Four corrections are needed. **"Owns"** is wrong: the Survey is the **custodian** of monuments under the Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains Act, 1958, and the 2010 amending Act separately distinguishes **guardianship, purchase and lease** as three different modes by which responsibility is taken - which only makes sense if custodianship is not ownership. **"Every ancient monument in the country"** is wrong: the Survey holds the **centrally protected** estate, recorded on its own monuments page as **3,679** monuments and archaeological sites when accessed in September 2026; State archaeology departments protect State-protected monuments, and a very large number of significant structures are protected by neither regime. **"Upgraded a hundred of them to protected status"** is wrong twice over. The hundred monuments were **identified under the Adarsh Smarak scheme of the Ministry of Culture for enhanced visitor amenities** - a facilities programme, not a protective declaration; and protected status is conferred by **statutory declaration** under the 1958 Act, not by inclusion in a scheme. **"Under a national scheme"**, finally, conceals the firewall that matters: a **statute** creates protection and offences, an **institution** carries a standing mandate, and a **scheme** is an executive programme that can be renamed, moved between ministries or discontinued. An accurate sentence would read: the Survey is custodian of 3,679 centrally protected monuments and archaeological sites under the 1958 Act, as recorded on its own page in September 2026, and a hundred of those monuments have been identified under the Ministry of Culture's Adarsh Smarak scheme for enhanced visitor amenities.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that appearing in a government scheme's list changes an asset's legal status. Scheme membership is an administrative choice about where effort and money go; legal status comes from a declaration under a statute, and the two can move entirely independently of each other.

## Lesson 9 - The institutional map: who else acts, and what each one is precisely for

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Candidates meet these bodies as a list of acronyms and store them as "various cultural organisations". That storage guarantees two failures. In Prelims, a matching item pairing a body with its mandate cannot be answered. In Mains, the answer says "various cultural bodies exist to promote heritage", which is a sentence that earns nothing because it could have been written without reading anything.

The map above is therefore arranged not alphabetically and not by ministry, but by **the job each body does**. Six jobs: document and record; regulate and permit; research and study; teach and train; collect and display; project and publish. A body's job is what determines whether it is the right body to name in a given answer, and it is the only organising principle that makes the list learnable.

### 2. The documentation and record bodies

**The National Archives of India** has the longest and most examinable institutional history in this lesson. Its predecessor, the **Imperial Records Department**, was **founded in 1891 in Calcutta under Prof. G. W. Forrest**, and was one of the earliest British institutions serving as an archive of Indian administrative records. It was **moved to New Delhi in 1911** and **renamed the National Archives of India after Independence**, with a Director appointed to head it. The **archives were opened to the public for research in 1939**. A **Conservation Research Laboratory was added in 1940** for the express purpose of conserving and preserving important documents on Indian history. **Regional centres were opened at Jaipur, Bhubaneswar and Puducherry.**

Its stated objectives, as the source records them from the Ministry's memorandum, are four: to help preserve India's **documentary cultural heritage** for future generations and provide greater access to archival holdings; to build a **large repository** of documents and manage them scientifically; to create and nurture **closer relations between archival institutions and archivists** nationally and internationally; and to develop a **scientific temper** among archivists, custodians and users to ensure proper care and management.

**Abhilekh-Patal** is its online portal for access to reference media and digitised collections. The source gives the etymology and the acronym together, and both are examinable: *Abhilekh* means the records of ancient times and *Patal* means a platform, board or surface, while **PATAL** is also an acronym for **Portal for Access To Archives & Learning**.

**The National Mission on Monuments and Antiquities** is an agency under the Ministry of Culture, **launched in 2007**, aimed at **studying, documenting and preserving the cultural heritage of India**. Its existence is the systemic answer to the inventory problem identified in Lesson 6: a dedicated national documentation effort for monuments and antiquities.

**The National Mission for Manuscripts** carries the corresponding function for manuscripts - documentation, conservation and digitisation. Its role in this topic is as the destination for any question about manuscript heritage that is not an archival-records question.

### 3. The research and study bodies

**The Anthropological Survey of India** is, under the Ministry of Culture, the **apex Indian government organisation for anthropological studies and field research in physical and cultural anthropology**. It was **founded in 1945 in Varanasi** and **shifted to the Indian Museum in Calcutta in 1948**; it is **headquartered in Kolkata** with branches at **Port Blair, Shillong, Dehra Dun, Udaipur, Nagpur and Mysore**. It studies the socio-cultural and biological aspects of Indian people, focusing on culture, social institutions and ethnic affinities, and its flagship publication, the **Journal of the Anthropological Survey of India**, has been published **since 1952**.

**The Asiatic Society** is described by the source as the **oldest centre of learning and research in Asia**. It was **established in 1784 during Company rule** at the initiative of its founding president, **Sir William Jones**, to encourage oriental studies. Its successive names are a favourite item: founded as the **Asiatic Society**, renamed the **Asiatic Society of Bengal (1832-1935)**, then the **Royal Asiatic Society of Bengal (1936-1951)**, and finally the **Asiatic Society** again from **1952**. In **1984 it was declared an Institution of National Importance by an Act of the Parliament of India**. It houses a library and museum with **more than 117,000 books, 79,000 journals**, maps, microfiche, microfilm, paintings and **2,500 photographs**. One object connects it to the ancient-history syllabus: the **Bairat Minor Rock Edict of Ashoka, also known as the Bhabru Edict**, was initially housed at the Society's museum and was later moved to the **Indian Museum, Kolkata**.

**The Indira Gandhi National Centre for the Arts** and the **Indian Council of Historical Research** appear in the standard printed account as chapter sidebars whose body text is not recoverable in the copy consulted. What is recorded here is therefore confined to their function: the former is an autonomous centre under the Ministry of Culture for research, academic work and dissemination across the arts - visual, performing, written and oral; the latter is an autonomous body for historical research. **No founding year, office-holder, programme name or budget is asserted for either**, and that restraint is declared rather than concealed.

### 4. Training, collection and projection

**The Centre for Cultural Resources and Training** was instituted by the Ministry of Culture **for linking education with culture**, and was **established in 1979 at the behest of Dr. Kapila Vatsyayan and Smt. Kamaladevi Chattopadhyay**. It is **headquartered in New Delhi with centres at Udaipur, Hyderabad and Guwahati**. Its functions are to organise workshops giving teachers practical training in crafts for the school curriculum and to organise art activities across classical dance, drama and music; to create a curriculum on Indian art and culture for schools; and to create a resource library of scripts, digital photographs and audio and visual recordings as cultural educational aids, aimed at encouraging and reviving the arts and crafts of rural India. It runs extension services for students in government, government-aided and non-formal schools, and has implemented the **Cultural Talent Search Scholarship Scheme since 1982 for ages 10 to 14**, promoting music, dance, drama, painting, sculpture, crafts and literary activities.

**The National Gallery of Modern Art** is the premier art gallery under the Ministry of Culture, with its main museum at **Jaipur House, New Delhi**, and holds works by artists including Thomas Daniell, Abanindranath Tagore, Rabindranath Tagore, Gaganendranath Tagore, Nandalal Bose, Jamini Roy and Amrita Sher-Gil. **A boundary and a declared gap together:** museum administration as a subject - collection policy, accession, deaccession, museum governance and the national museum system - is **not carried by any source held for this session**, and nothing about it is asserted here. What this session owns about museums is their place in the institutional map and their relationship to the illicit-trafficking machinery discussed in Lesson 6, where the international museum body's published classifications of at-risk object categories operate through museum acquisition practice.

**The Indian Council for Cultural Relations** was **founded in 1950 by Maulana Abul Kalam Azad** and sits under the **Ministry of External Affairs** - the one major body in this map that is not under Culture, which is itself an examinable point. It promotes Indian culture and international cultural exchange, funds programmes in the visual and performing arts with international appeal, and honours foreign nationals with awards including the **Bharat Mitra Samman**.

**All India Radio**, officially **Akashvani since 1957**, is the national public radio broadcaster under **Prasar Bharati**, Ministry of Information and Broadcasting, with the motto **Bahujan Hitaya: Bahujan Sukhaya**. It reaches almost **99.19 per cent** of the population and broadcasts in **23 languages and 179 dialects**, with objectives defined under the **Prasar Bharati Act, amended in 1990**. The **Directorate of Film Festivals** was **set up in 1973** to organise national and international film festivals, and has additional charge of the National Film Awards functions.

### 5. Outside government: the heritage trust

**The Indian National Trust for Art and Cultural Heritage** is the principal non-governmental body in this field. It is recorded here at the level that is safe and useful: it is a **non-governmental organisation** devoted to the conservation of India's heritage, and it is associated with the consultative work through which **heritage bye-laws** under the statutory regime are framed, as Lesson 7 records.

The analytical point is worth more than the acronym. A non-governmental trust can do three things that a government agency finds hard: it can act on **unprotected** heritage, which by definition falls outside the statutory estate; it can work at **local chapter level** across many towns at once; and it can advocate publicly in a way a department cannot. Those three capacities are the reason a heritage architecture that consisted only of government bodies would have a large hole in it, and naming the hole is a better analytical move than naming the trust.

### 6. Comparative treatment: routing a question to the right body

| The question is about | The body to name | Why not the obvious alternative |
|---|---|---|
| a centrally protected monument's conservation | the Archaeological Survey of India | the arts centre researches; it does not conserve monuments |
| permission to build near a protected monument | the competent authority under the statutory regime, with the National Monuments Authority and heritage bye-laws | the Survey is custodian, but the permission architecture is separate |
| documenting monuments and antiquities nationally | the National Mission on Monuments and Antiquities | the Survey documents its own estate; national documentation across categories is the mission's object |
| a manuscript's conservation and digitisation | the National Mission for Manuscripts | the archives hold public records, which are a different class of document |
| a government file or public record | the National Archives of India, under the Public Records Act, 1993 | manuscripts are not public records |
| a tribal community's social organisation | the Anthropological Survey of India | a cultural academy promotes an art form; it does not conduct anthropological field research |
| cultural education in schools | the Centre for Cultural Resources and Training | the academies promote the arts; they do not write school curricula |
| projecting Indian culture abroad | the Indian Council for Cultural Relations, under External Affairs | it is the one major body here outside the Culture ministry |
| heritage that is not statutorily protected | the non-governmental heritage trust and local bodies | the statutory machinery does not reach it at all |

### 7. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** This proliferation of bodies is itself the problem. A survey, a mission for monuments, a mission for manuscripts, an archives, a laboratory, an arts centre, a research council, a training centre, three academies and a trust produce overlapping mandates, divided budgets and nobody clearly answerable. India does not need mandate precision; it needs fewer institutions.

**Reply.** The observation about overlap is fair and the conclusion does not follow. Three points. First, the bodies exist because the **objects differ**: a monument, a manuscript, a public record, a living practice, a community's social organisation and a school curriculum genuinely require different expertise, and a single agency covering all of them would be expert in none. Second, the practical failures reported in this field are rarely attributed to the existence of too many specialised bodies; they are attributed to **capacity** within each, which consolidation does not create. Third - and this is the concession the objector deserves - the real coordination problem is not the number of bodies but the absence of a clear route for heritage that **falls between** them: an unprotected historic building in a growing town belongs to no statutory estate, no mission's documentation programme and no academy's mandate, and it is exactly there that loss occurs. The reform worth arguing for is therefore not merger but **coverage**: a defined route for unprotected significant heritage, which is where the non-governmental trust and local bodies currently substitute for a system.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies a **very large number of directly citable institutional particulars** - founding years, founders, headquarters, renaming sequences, portals, publications and schemes - which are the raw material of matching and single-fact items. It supplies the **six-job organisation** that makes the list learnable. It supplies the **routing table** in section 6, which is the single most useful device for avoiding the "various cultural bodies" sentence. And it supplies the **coverage argument** in section 7, which is a genuinely analytical closing move rather than a list.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing "various cultural organisations" instead of naming a body with its precise mandate.
- Placing the Indian Council for Cultural Relations under the Ministry of Culture; it is under External Affairs.
- Confusing the Imperial Records Department's founding year with the archives' move to New Delhi or its opening to research.
- Confusing the manuscripts mission with the archives; manuscripts and public records are different classes of document.
- Giving the Asiatic Society's renaming sequence out of order or omitting the 1984 declaration.
- Assuming the Anthropological Survey is a museum body.
- Asserting founding years or programmes for bodies whose detail is not held.

### Revision notes

1. **National Archives of India:** predecessor the **Imperial Records Department, 1891, Calcutta, under Prof. G. W. Forrest**; moved to **New Delhi 1911**; renamed after Independence; **opened to public research 1939**; **Conservation Research Laboratory added 1940**; regional centres **Jaipur, Bhubaneswar, Puducherry**.
2. Its portal is **Abhilekh-Patal**; *Abhilekh* means records of ancient times, *Patal* a platform; **PATAL = Portal for Access To Archives & Learning**.
3. **National Mission on Monuments and Antiquities: launched 2007**, Ministry of Culture; studying, documenting and preserving cultural heritage.
4. **National Mission for Manuscripts:** manuscript documentation, conservation and digitisation.
5. **Anthropological Survey of India: founded 1945 at Varanasi**, shifted to the **Indian Museum, Calcutta, 1948**; headquartered **Kolkata**; branches **Port Blair, Shillong, Dehra Dun, Udaipur, Nagpur, Mysore**; **Journal since 1952**.
6. **Asiatic Society: 1784**, founding president **Sir William Jones**; **Asiatic Society of Bengal 1832-1935**; **Royal Asiatic Society of Bengal 1936-1951**; **Asiatic Society from 1952**; **Institution of National Importance by Act of Parliament, 1984**; **117,000+ books, 79,000 journals, 2,500 photographs**.
7. The **Bairat Minor Rock Edict (Bhabru Edict)** of Ashoka moved from the Asiatic Society's museum to the **Indian Museum, Kolkata**.
8. **Centre for Cultural Resources and Training: 1979**, at the behest of **Dr. Kapila Vatsyayan and Smt. Kamaladevi Chattopadhyay**; New Delhi, with **Udaipur, Hyderabad, Guwahati**; **Cultural Talent Search Scholarship Scheme since 1982, ages 10-14**.
9. **Indian Council for Cultural Relations: 1950**, founded by **Maulana Abul Kalam Azad**, under the **Ministry of External Affairs**; **Bharat Mitra Samman**.
10. **National Gallery of Modern Art:** Ministry of Culture; main museum at **Jaipur House, New Delhi**.
11. **All India Radio / Akashvani since 1957**, under **Prasar Bharati**, Ministry of Information and Broadcasting; motto **Bahujan Hitaya: Bahujan Sukhaya**; **99.19 per cent** reach; **23 languages, 179 dialects**; **Prasar Bharati Act, amended 1990**. **Directorate of Film Festivals: 1973.**
12. **INTACH** is a **non-governmental** heritage organisation, associated with the consultative framing of **heritage bye-laws**.
13. **IGNCA** (arts research) and **ICHR** (historical research) are recorded by function only; **no founding year or programme is asserted for either here**.
14. The routing rule: **monument to the Survey, manuscript to the manuscripts mission, public record to the archives, community to the anthropological survey, school curriculum to the training centre, projection abroad to the external-relations council.**

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"India's heritage architecture is institutionally rich and structurally incomplete." Examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

Both halves of the statement are defensible, and the second is the more interesting.

**The richness is real and specific.** Documentation is carried by the **National Archives of India**, whose predecessor Imperial Records Department dates to **1891** and which added a **Conservation Research Laboratory in 1940**; by the **National Mission on Monuments and Antiquities**, launched in **2007**; and by the **National Mission for Manuscripts**. Regulation runs through the **National Monuments Authority** created by the 2010 amending Act. Research sits with the **Anthropological Survey of India** (**1945**) and the **Asiatic Society** (**1784**, declared an Institution of National Importance in **1984**). Training sits with the **Centre for Cultural Resources and Training** (**1979**). Projection abroad sits with the **Indian Council for Cultural Relations** (**1950**), under External Affairs rather than Culture. Custody of the monument estate sits with the **Archaeological Survey of India** (**1861**).

**The incompleteness is not a shortage of bodies.** It is a gap in **coverage**. Heritage that is neither centrally protected nor State protected - vernacular buildings, historic urban fabric, cultural landscapes, recent significant architecture - falls outside every statutory estate and outside every mission's documentation programme, and can be lawfully lost. Into that gap steps a **non-governmental trust**, doing at local chapter level what no statutory body is mandated to do.

The reform that follows is therefore not consolidation but a defined route for unprotected significant heritage, with documentation preceding protection rather than following it.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = at least six named bodies with their correct job and a dated particular each 6 + the external-relations council correctly placed under External Affairs 1 + the coverage gap identified as the structural incompleteness 4 + a named class of heritage that falls into the gap 2 + the reform stated as coverage rather than merger 2.** An answer that argues the incompleteness is "too many overlapping bodies" scores at most half, because it mistakes an administrative irritation for a structural hole.

### Concept check

**Question:** Four questions arrive on the same desk: a request to conserve a nineteenth-century government file; a request to digitise a palm-leaf manuscript collection in a monastery; a request to record the kinship system of a forest-dwelling community; and a request to stop the demolition of an unprotected 1930s cinema. Route each to the right body, and say which one has no clear route.

**Model answer:** The **nineteenth-century government file** goes to the **National Archives of India**. It is a public record within the meaning of the Public Records Act, 1993, which covers documents, files, manuscripts, microfilms and images relating to the Central Government, a Ministry or a Department, requires a Records Officer and a Record Room, and provides for appraisal every twenty-five years in consultation with the archives. The archives also hold the **Conservation Research Laboratory**, added in 1940 precisely for conserving and preserving historical documents. The **palm-leaf manuscript collection** goes to the **National Mission for Manuscripts**, whose object is manuscript documentation, conservation and digitisation; it does **not** go to the archives, because a monastery's manuscripts are not public records and the distinction between the two classes of document is a real one. The **kinship system of a forest-dwelling community** goes to the **Anthropological Survey of India**, the apex government organisation for anthropological study and field research in physical and cultural anthropology; it does not go to a cultural academy, whose mandate is the promotion of an art form rather than social-scientific field research. The **unprotected 1930s cinema has no clear route, and that is the point of the question.** It is not a centrally protected monument, so the 1958 Act and the Survey's custodianship do not reach it; it is unlikely to be an antiquity within the 1972 Act; it is neither a manuscript nor a public record; and no academy's mandate covers a building. The only bodies likely to act are the **local authority**, under whatever heritage provisions its own planning regime contains, and the **non-governmental heritage trust**, which can document, advocate and mobilise but cannot prohibit. That gap - significant recent fabric outside every statutory estate - is the structural hole in the architecture, and it is where the losses actually occur.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because India has many heritage institutions, every heritage problem has an institution attached to it. The institutions are organised around **categories**, and anything that falls outside the categories falls outside the system - which is why identifying the uncovered case is worth more in an answer than listing the covered ones.

## Lesson 10 - Three national academies, and why one would not have been enough

### Visual: three academies, three objects, three kinds of safeguarding act

  WHY THREE AND NOT ONE:
  a performance exists only while performed  -> safeguard by supporting

                                                 TRANSMISSION
  a text exists once written                 -> safeguard by TRANSLATION,
                                                 PUBLICATION and RECOGNITION
                                                 across languages
  a painting or sculpture exists as an object-> safeguard by EXHIBITION,
                                                 COLLECTION and STUDIO
                                                 FACILITIES
  THREE OBJECTS, THREE FAILURE MODES, THREE SAFEGUARDING TECHNIQUES.
