### 1. The learner's actual problem

The three academies are usually memorised as a triplet of years and subjects - 1952 performing arts, 1954 letters, 1954 art - and that is enough for a single matching item and nothing else. What it misses is the reason the triplet exists, and the reason is genuinely interesting: **the three objects fail in three different ways, so they need three different kinds of support.**

A dance tradition dies when the last practitioner stops teaching. A literary tradition in a minority language dies when nobody publishes, translates or reads it. A visual-art tradition weakens when artists have no space to work, no exhibition to show in and no collection preserving what came before. Practitioner support, publication and recognition, and studio-and-exhibition infrastructure are three different instruments, and an institution designed for one of them will do the other two badly. That is the argument the three academies embody, and it is a far better opening sentence than a list of years.

### 2. Sangeet Natak Akademi

**Set up in 1952**, it is **India's first national academy for the performing arts**, promoting the country's **music, dance and drama**. The source records explicitly that it **collaborates with international organisations such as UNESCO to safeguard the cultural heritage of India** - a detail worth holding, because it is the institutional link between this lesson and Lesson 14 on intangible heritage.

Its stated aims are four: to promote **research and performances** in music, dance and drama; to encourage **theatre centres, especially regional theatres**, and to document literature including handbooks and illustrated dictionaries; to **revive, promote and preserve folk dance, music and drama**; and to foster **cultural contact with the international community** in those fields.

**Constituent and established institutions.** The academy has **three constituent units, two of these being dance-teaching institutions: the Jawaharlal Nehru Manipur Dance Academy at Imphal and Kathak Kendra in Delhi**. It has established several institutions over the years, including the **Manipur Dance Academy at Imphal**, a **Sattriya Centre**, **Ravindra Rangshala**, and a **Centre for Kutiyattam at Thiruvananthapuram** - a national project in support of Koodiyattam, the age-old Sanskrit theatre of Kerala.

**A discrepancy recorded rather than resolved.** The source states that the academy has *three* constituent units while naming only two, and then separately lists a longer set of institutions it has established. The two statements are not obviously consistent - the third constituent unit is not identified - and the list of established institutions repeats the Manipur academy in a slightly different form. Both statements are printed here as the source gives them; neither is silently reconciled.

**Awards and honours.** The set is large and examinable: the **Sangeet Natak Akademi Award**, the highest national recognition given to practising artists, gurus and scholars; the **Sangeet Natak Akademi Fellowship (Ratna Sadsya)**, for distinguished individuals for their contribution in the fields of arts, music, dance and theatre; the **Ustad Bismillah Khan Yuva Puraskar** for young artists **below 40 years of age**; and the **Tagore Ratna** and **Tagore Puraskar**, conferred on the occasion of the commemoration of the **150th birth anniversary of Rabindranath Tagore**. It also bestows the **Samman for Theatre Practitioners**, the **Award for Folk and Tribal Arts**, the **Award for Puppetry Arts** and a **Certificate of Honour**.

Notice the structure of that award set, because it is an argument in disguise. There is a top honour, a lifetime fellowship, a **youth** award with an age cut-off, and separate awards for **folk and tribal** arts and for **puppetry**. Each of those is an answer to a specific failure mode: the youth award addresses transmission to the next generation; the folk and tribal award addresses forms that have no urban market; the puppetry award addresses a form documented as being under particular pressure. Awards, in this field, are not ceremony - they are **income, visibility and legitimacy** for practitioners, which is the only safeguarding technique available for a form that has no fabric.

**The National School of Drama.** It was **set up by the Sangeet Natak Akademi as one of its constituent units in 1959**, and **in 1975 it became an independent entity and an autonomous organisation under the Ministry of Culture**. It is described as one of the foremost theatre-training institutions in the world and the only one of its kind in India, situated in New Delhi. It has two performing wings, the **Repertory Company** and the **Theatre-in-Education Company (Sanskaar Rang Toli)**. **Bharat Rang Mahotsav**, the National Theatre Festival, **started in 1999**, is held during the **second week of January** in New Delhi and is acknowledged as the largest theatre festival of Asia dedicated solely to theatre, with a **Satellite Festival** in a designated showcase city.

The 1959-to-1975 sequence is the examinable particular: **created by the academy, later independent of it.**

### 3. Sahitya Akademi

The Government of India **established the National Academy of Letters, or the Sahitya Akademi, in 1954**, to promote literature and literary activities and to foster national unity. It is an **autonomous organisation** undertaking literary activities in **over 24 Indian languages**: apart from the **22 languages mentioned in the Constitution**, it recognises **two more - English and Rajasthani**.

That 22-plus-2 formula is the single most tested fact about this academy, and it must be stated in that form. "Twenty-four languages" alone invites the follow-up question; "the twenty-two languages of the Constitution plus English and Rajasthani" answers it in advance.

**Awards and fellowships.** The **Sahitya Akademi Award** is for outstanding literary works in the recognised languages; the source records that the **first award was given in 1955**, and that **Shree Makhanlal Chaturvedi** was the first awardee in Hindi literature, for his work **Him Tarangini**. The award comprises a cash prize and a plaque bearing the word *Sahitya* in Devanagari, **designed by the filmmaker Satyajit Ray**; the source also notes that on many occasions the award has been returned or declined as an act of protest. The **Bhasha Samman**, **instituted in 1996** and also given by this academy, honours writers who have made significant contributions to Indian languages **other than the 24 major languages** covered by the main award, as well as to **classical and medieval literature**. The **Dr. Ananda Coomaraswamy Fellowship** is for those who have worked in the field of ancient India, and the **Premchand Fellowship** for those who have contributed to literature.

The Bhasha Samman is analytically the most interesting of these. It exists precisely because the main award's twenty-four-language frame **excludes** most of India's languages, and the academy's own answer to that exclusion is a separate honour. An answer that notes this - that the recognition architecture itself creates a boundary and then needs an instrument to reach past it - is doing analysis rather than recitation.

**Other activity.** The academy organises national and regional **workshops and seminars**; provides **research and travel grants** to authors; publishes books and journals including the **Encyclopaedia of Indian Literature**; publishes two bimonthly literary journals, **Indian Literature** in English and **Samkaleen Bharatiya Sahitya** in Hindi; and organises literary gatherings including **Samvad, Meet the Author, Kathasandhi, Mulakat and Asmita**. The source records that in recent years the collective focus has shifted to the **preservation of oral and tribal literature**, on the reasoning that ancient traditions were orally transmitted and must be preserved for future generations.

That last point is the bridge to Lesson 14. **Oral literature is simultaneously literature and intangible heritage**, and an academy of letters turning towards it is the clearest institutional illustration that the tangible-intangible boundary is porous.

### 4. Lalit Kala Akademi

The **National Academy of Art, or Lalit Kala Akademi, was set up by the Government of India in 1954** with the sole objective of **promoting fine arts in India**. It is an **autonomous body funded by the Ministry of Culture**. Although it deals with national and international art, its focus is on the **promotion and preservation of Indian art**. Its **main centre is in Delhi**, with **regional centres at Chennai, Kolkata, Lucknow, Shimla, Shillong and Bhubaneswar**.

Its activities are exhibition, collection and facility: it organises national and international exhibitions and events such as the **National Exhibition of Art** - described as its **most prestigious annual event** - and the **International Triennale India**; it preserves India's visual-art traditions; and it maintains centres with a **permanent collection of modern and contemporary Indian art**. It has set up the **Garhi Studios** as an institutional facility for seminars, artist workshops, lecture demonstrations and exhibitions. **In 1968 it started the Triennale-India exhibitions**, an ambitious programme to enter the international art circuit, with a host of countries invited to participate in New Delhi at **regular intervals of three to four years**; the **Rashtriya Kala Mela** became a regular complementary feature of the Triennale. The **Lalit Kala Akademi Award is the highest award given by the Government of India for visual arts**.

### 5. Comparative treatment: three academies against three failure modes

| | Sangeet Natak Akademi | Sahitya Akademi | Lalit Kala Akademi |
|---|---|---|---|
| Year | 1952 | 1954 | 1954 |
| Object | music, dance, drama | literature in over 24 languages | fine arts |
| How the tradition fails | the last practitioner stops teaching; no audience; no income | nobody writes, publishes, translates or reads in the language | no studio, no exhibition, no collection, no market |
| The instrument used | awards and fellowships to practitioners; teaching institutions; documentation; regional theatre support | awards, fellowships, grants, translation, publication, journals, encyclopaedia, literary gatherings | exhibitions, triennale, permanent collection, studios, regional centres |
| The reach-past-the-boundary device | awards specifically for **folk and tribal arts** and for **puppetry**; a **youth** award below 40 | the **Bhasha Samman** for languages outside the 24 and for classical and medieval literature | regional centres outside Delhi; the studio facility for working artists |
| Link to international safeguarding | collaborates with UNESCO to safeguard cultural heritage | oral and tribal literature focus overlaps with living heritage | - |

The row on reach-past-the-boundary devices is the one worth learning, because every recognition system creates an inside and an outside, and the interesting institutional question is always what it does about the outside.

### 6. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** Awards and festivals are not safeguarding. A practitioner who receives a national award once in a lifetime still has no income for the other forty years, and an annual festival in Delhi does nothing for a form that is dying in a village. The academies are a prestige system mistaken for a conservation system.

**Reply.** The criticism lands and must be partly accepted, but it misstates what the instrument is for. Three replies. First, in a field with **no fabric**, recognition is not decorative: it is one of the few levers that changes a practitioner's **income, students and standing** simultaneously, and the source's own award architecture - a youth award below 40, separate awards for folk, tribal and puppetry forms - shows the design is aimed at transmission rather than at ceremony. Second, the academies do more than award: they run **teaching institutions**, establish **centres for particular forms**, support **regional theatre**, document, publish and translate, and those are transmission activities rather than prestige activities. Third, the concession the objector deserves is the important one: none of these instruments reaches **livelihood at scale**, and an answer should say so plainly. The honest formulation is that recognition and teaching institutions address **visibility and transmission**, that neither addresses **income at scale**, and that a safeguarding argument which stops at awards has answered only part of the problem.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies the **three academies with their years, objects, constituent institutions and full award sets** - a dense block of matching-item material. The **22-plus-2 language formula** and the **1959-to-1975 drama-school sequence** are the two highest-value single facts. The **three-failure-modes comparison** is what converts an institutional list into an argument about why living heritage needs a different instrument from fabric. And the **mandate-precision rule** applies with full force here: a performing-arts safeguarding question routes to the performing-arts academy, a literature question to the letters academy, a visual-art question to the art academy, and naming the wrong one is a substantive error.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating the three academies as interchangeable "cultural bodies".
- Giving 1954 for the performing-arts academy; it is **1952**, and it is the **first** national academy.
- Saying "24 languages" without the 22-plus-English-and-Rajasthani breakdown.
- Attributing the drama school's founding to the Ministry of Culture; it was set up **by the academy** in 1959 and became independent in **1975**.
- Confusing the National Exhibition of Art with the Triennale-India.
- Treating awards as ceremony rather than as transmission instruments in a field with no fabric.
- Claiming that the academies address practitioner livelihood at scale.

### Revision notes

1. **Sangeet Natak Akademi, 1952:** India's **first** national academy for the performing arts - music, dance and drama. Collaborates with UNESCO on safeguarding.
2. Its four aims: promote research and performance; encourage **regional theatre** and document literature including handbooks and illustrated dictionaries; **revive, promote and preserve folk** dance, music and drama; foster international cultural contact.
3. Constituent units include the **Jawaharlal Nehru Manipur Dance Academy, Imphal** and **Kathak Kendra, Delhi**; it has established a **Sattriya Centre**, **Ravindra Rangshala** and a **Centre for Kutiyattam, Thiruvananthapuram**.
4. Its awards: **Sangeet Natak Akademi Award** (highest recognition to practising artists, gurus and scholars); **Fellowship / Ratna Sadsya**; **Ustad Bismillah Khan Yuva Puraskar** (**below 40**); **Tagore Ratna** and **Tagore Puraskar** (Tagore's **150th** birth anniversary); **Samman for Theatre Practitioners**; **Award for Folk and Tribal Arts**; **Award for Puppetry Arts**; **Certificate of Honour**.
5. **National School of Drama: set up by the academy in 1959; independent in 1975** under the Ministry of Culture. Wings: **Repertory Company** and **Theatre-in-Education Company (Sanskaar Rang Toli)**. **Bharat Rang Mahotsav from 1999**, second week of January, with a satellite festival in a showcase city.
6. **Sahitya Akademi, 1954:** National Academy of Letters; autonomous; **over 24 languages = 22 constitutional + English + Rajasthani**.
7. **Sahitya Akademi Award:** first given **1955**; first Hindi awardee **Makhanlal Chaturvedi** for **Him Tarangini**; plaque designed by **Satyajit Ray**; has been returned or declined in protest.
8. **Bhasha Samman, instituted 1996**, for languages **outside** the 24 and for **classical and medieval** literature. Also the **Dr. Ananda Coomaraswamy Fellowship** (ancient India) and the **Premchand Fellowship**.
9. Sahitya Akademi publishes the **Encyclopaedia of Indian Literature** and the journals **Indian Literature** (English) and **Samkaleen Bharatiya Sahitya** (Hindi); its gatherings include **Samvad, Meet the Author, Kathasandhi, Mulakat, Asmita**; its recent focus has shifted to **oral and tribal literature**.
10. **Lalit Kala Akademi, 1954:** National Academy of Art; autonomous, funded by the Ministry of Culture; main centre **Delhi**; regional centres **Chennai, Kolkata, Lucknow, Shimla, Shillong, Bhubaneswar**.
11. Its activities: **National Exhibition of Art** (most prestigious annual event); **Triennale-India from 1968** at **three-to-four-year** intervals, with **Rashtriya Kala Mela** as a complementary feature; **Garhi Studios**; permanent collection of modern and contemporary Indian art. The **Lalit Kala Akademi Award** is the highest Government of India award for visual arts.
12. Three objects, three failure modes, three instruments: **practitioner support** for performance, **publication and translation** for literature, **studio, exhibition and collection** for visual art.
13. Every recognition system creates an outside; the **Bhasha Samman**, the **folk and tribal award** and the **puppetry award** are the devices that reach past it.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Recognition is not safeguarding." Examine this proposition with reference to India's national academies. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is largely correct and needs one qualification.

**Why it is correct.** An award changes a practitioner's standing; it does not change the economics that make transmission fail. The **Sangeet Natak Akademi**, set up in **1952** as India's first national academy for the performing arts, confers a graded set of honours - its Award, its Fellowship or Ratna Sadsya, the **Ustad Bismillah Khan Yuva Puraskar** for artists below forty, and separate awards for **folk and tribal arts** and for **puppetry**. Each reaches a handful of individuals annually. Neither it nor the **Sahitya Akademi** (**1954**, literature in the twenty-two constitutional languages plus English and Rajasthani) nor the **Lalit Kala Akademi** (**1954**, fine arts) reaches practitioner livelihood at scale.

**The qualification.** In a field with no fabric, recognition is one of the few levers that moves **income, students and legitimacy** together, and the academies also run teaching institutions - **Kathak Kendra**, the **Jawaharlal Nehru Manipur Dance Academy**, a **Centre for Kutiyattam** - and support regional theatre and documentation.

Recognition is therefore a genuine but partial instrument; safeguarding requires transmission and livelihood measures that no academy currently supplies at scale.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the proposition assessed rather than merely asserted 2 + three academies named with correct year and object 3 + a graded award architecture shown to be aimed at transmission 2 + named teaching institutions as the non-award instrument 2 + the livelihood-at-scale limit stated as the residue 1.** Listing awards without connecting any of them to a failure mode caps the answer at four.

### Concept check

**Question:** A puppetry tradition has three ageing performers, no apprentices and no paying audience. Which national body is the correct route, which of its instruments actually bear on the problem, and what does the case reveal about the limits of the academy model?

**Model answer:** The correct route is the **Sangeet Natak Akademi**, set up in **1952** as India's first national academy for the performing arts, with music, dance and drama as its object; puppetry falls within its performing-arts mandate and the academy maintains a dedicated **Award for Puppetry Arts**, which confirms the routing. **Three of its instruments bear on the problem and they bear unequally.** The **Award for Puppetry Arts** and, for any younger performer, the **Ustad Bismillah Khan Yuva Puraskar** for artists below forty, supply recognition, a measure of income and public visibility - which addresses legitimacy and may attract a student, but reaches only an individual. The academy's stated aim to **revive, promote and preserve folk dance, music and drama** and to encourage **regional theatre** is the closest thing to a structural instrument, and its practice of establishing **dedicated centres for particular forms** - as it has done for Kutiyattam at Thiruvananthapuram - is the model that would actually address transmission, because a centre creates a place where apprentices can be taught. **Documentation** is the third, and it is the weakest and most honest: recording the repertoire, the technique and the construction of the puppets preserves the information even if the practice stops, which is a real but reduced outcome. **What the case reveals** is the limit of the model. The academy can confer recognition, create a teaching centre and document; it cannot create an audience, and it cannot supply a livelihood to three performers over the years that an apprenticeship takes. The two failures driving this case - no apprentices and no paying audience - are a transmission failure and a market failure, and the academy's instruments reach the first only partially and the second not at all.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the existence of a relevant award as evidence that the problem has an institutional solution. An award is aimed at an individual; transmission failure is a structural problem about apprentices, time and income, and only an instrument that operates over years - a teaching centre, a stipendiary apprenticeship, a stable venue - addresses it.

## Lesson 11 - How a place becomes a World Heritage property

### Visual: the route from a State's own inventory to an inscription

```text
   STATE PARTY IDENTIFIES A PROPERTY ON ITS OWN TERRITORY
                          |
                          v
   +--------------------------------------------------+

                          |

   +--------------------------------------------------+

   |   ICOMOS   -> cultural properties                |
   |   IUCN     -> natural properties                 |
   |   (a mixed property is evaluated by both)        |
   |   ICCROM   -> advises on conservation and        |
   |               training                           |
   +--------------------------------------------------+
                          |
                          v
   +--------------------------------------------------+
   | WORLD HERITAGE COMMITTEE DECIDES                 |
   | 21 States Parties, elected, SIX-YEAR term,       |
   | generally meets ONCE A YEAR                      |
   | outcomes: inscribe | refer back | defer |        |
   |           do not inscribe                        |
   +--------------------------------------------------+
                          |
                          v
   +--------------------------------------------------+
   | INSCRIPTION ON THE WORLD HERITAGE LIST           |
   | the property remains OWNED, MANAGED and          |
   | GOVERNED by the State Party under its own law    |
   +--------------------------------------------------+

   THE COMMITTEE'S THREE FUNCTIONS, AS THE SOURCE STATES THEM:
   1. selects the sites to be listed - the World Heritage List AND the
      List of World Heritage in Danger
   2. defines the use of the World Heritage Fund
   3. allocates financial assistance upon requests from States Parties
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

"UNESCO declared it a World Heritage Site" is the sentence every candidate writes, and almost every word in it is loose. UNESCO does not declare; a **Committee of twenty-one States Parties** decides. The decision is not arbitrary; it turns on **outstanding universal value** established against **numbered criteria**. And the decision is the end of a long route that begins with the State Party's own inventory.

The practical consequence is that a question about World Heritage can be asked at four different points on that route - the Convention, the Committee, the criteria, the process - and a candidate who has only memorised a list of Indian sites cannot answer at any of them.

### 2. The Convention and the Committee

**The Convention.** The list of World Heritage Sites is compiled by UNESCO through the **World Heritage Committee**, according to the provisions of the **World Heritage Convention, which was established in 1972**. The source describes the Convention as **one of the most important global conservation instruments**, whose **primary mission is to identify and protect the world's natural and cultural heritage considered to be of Outstanding Universal Value**. The Convention is **governed by the World Heritage Committee**.

The phrase to hold is **"natural and cultural"**. The 1972 Convention's distinguishing feature is that it covers both in a single instrument, which is why the criteria come in two groups and why the mixed category exists at all.

**The Committee.** It is **the governing body on World Heritage** and is **responsible for the implementation of the Convention**. It is an **inter-governmental committee comprising representatives from 21 States Parties selected through election for a six-year term**, and it **generally meets once a year**.

Four numbers and one adjective are examinable there: **21** members, **six-year** term, **once a year**, and the fact that it is **inter-governmental** - the decision-makers are States, not experts. That last point is the source of the most interesting criticism of the system, examined in section 7.

**Its three functions**, as the source lists them: it **selects the sites to be listed as UNESCO World Heritage Sites, including the World Heritage List and the List of World Heritage in Danger**; it **defines the use of the World Heritage Fund**; and it **allocates financial assistance upon requests from States Parties**.

Read those three together and the shape of the system appears. The Committee holds the **list**, the **danger list** and the **money**. That combination is what gives an international recommendation its practical force, and it is the mechanism behind the conditionality examined in Lesson 12.

**The advisory bodies.** The Committee **is aided by three advisory bodies, namely the IUCN, ICOMOS and ICCROM**. Their division of labour is the examinable content: **ICOMOS**, the international council on monuments and sites, evaluates **cultural** nominations; **IUCN**, the international union for conservation of nature, evaluates **natural** nominations; and **ICCROM**, the international centre for the study of the preservation and restoration of cultural property, advises on **conservation practice and training**. A **mixed** property is evaluated by both ICOMOS and IUCN, which is the structural consequence of the mixed category.

### 3. The ten criteria, in full

A nominated site **must be of outstanding universal value and should meet at least one of the ten criteria**. The source prints them, and they are worth having in full because items are built on individual criteria.

1. **Represents a masterpiece of human creative genius.**
2. **Exhibits an important interchange of human values over a span of time, or within a cultural area of the world, on developments in architecture or technology, monumental art, town planning or landscape design.**
3. **Bears a unique or exceptional testimony to a cultural tradition or to a civilisation which is living or which has disappeared.**
4. **An outstanding example of a type of building, architectural or technological ensemble or landscape which illustrates a significant stage in human history.**
5. **An outstanding example of a traditional human settlement, land use or sea-use which is representative of a culture or human interaction with the environment, especially when it has become vulnerable under the impact of irreversible change.**
6. **Is directly or tangibly associated with events or living traditions, with ideas, or with beliefs, with artistic and literary works of outstanding universal significance.**
7. **Contains superlative natural phenomena or areas of exceptional natural beauty and aesthetic importance.**
8. **An outstanding example representing major stages of the Earth's history, including the record of life, significant ongoing geological processes in the development of landforms, or significant geomorphic or physiographic features.**
9. **An outstanding example representing significant ongoing ecological and biological processes in the evolution and development of terrestrial, freshwater, coastal and marine ecosystems, and communities of plants and animals.**
10. **Contains the most important and significant natural habitats for in situ conservation of biological diversity, including those containing threatened species of outstanding universal value from the point of view of science or conservation.**

**The split is the rule to remember: the first six criteria relate to cultural sites and the remaining four to natural sites.** A property satisfying at least one from each group is a **mixed** property.

Two of the ten deserve a comment because they behave differently from the rest.

**Criterion (vi)** - association with events, living traditions, ideas, beliefs or artistic and literary works - is the only criterion that is essentially **associative** rather than physical. A place can satisfy it because of what happened there or what is believed about it. It is therefore the criterion that most often appears alongside another, because association alone is a fragile basis for recognising a place.

**Criterion (v)** contains a built-in vulnerability clause - "especially when it has become vulnerable under the impact of irreversible change" - which is the only criterion that explicitly anticipates the threat that makes protection urgent.

### 4. What a nomination must actually contain

The criteria establish *why* a property matters. A nomination must also establish that the property can be **kept**, and that is where the elements introduced in Lesson 3 and Lesson 7 return.

- **Boundaries.** The property must be defined on the ground. A vague significance over a general area is not a property.
- **A buffer zone where appropriate.** Mapped through the nomination and management documents - and, as Lesson 7 insists, an entirely different object from the domestic statutory zones.
- **Authenticity and integrity.** The property must be truthfully what it claims to be and must be whole enough to carry the value. Cultural properties are assessed principally on authenticity; natural properties on integrity; many properties on both.
- **Protection already in force.** This is the point at which the domestic law taught in Lessons 4 to 7 becomes internationally relevant. A State Party nominating a property must show the legal protection that already applies to it. Recognition therefore **presupposes** domestic protection; it does not create it.
- **A management plan or system.** How the property will be looked after: who is responsible, what the pressures are, and what is planned.

The fourth of these is the most analytically useful. It is the formal proof, inside the system's own procedure, that the recognition-protection distinction taught in Lesson 1 is not an academic invention: the Convention itself requires the State to have protected the property **before** it is inscribed.

### 5. The legal status attached by inscription

The source is precise here and the passage is quotable. **Once a site is declared a World Heritage Site by UNESCO, it is accepted as prima facie evidence that such a site is culturally sensitive and warrants legal protection pursuant to the Law of War under the Geneva Convention, its Articles, Protocols and Customs, together with other treaties including the Hague Convention for the Protection of Cultural Property in the Event of Armed Conflict, and international law.**

The source then quotes the operative prohibition. **Article 53 of the Geneva Convention** provides that, without prejudice to the provisions of the **Hague Convention for the Protection of Cultural Property in the Event of Armed Conflict of 14 May 1954** and of other relevant international instruments, it is prohibited:

- **to commit any act of hostility directed against the historic monuments, works of art or places of worship which constitute the cultural or spiritual heritage of peoples;**
- **to use such objects in support of the military effort;**
- **to make such objects the object of reprisals.**

Three observations turn this into usable content. First, the legal protection attached by inscription is **wartime** protection, under the law of armed conflict - it is not a general legal shield against neglect, development or decay in peacetime. Second, the second prohibition - **using such objects in support of the military effort** - is an obligation on the State that *holds* the property, not only on an attacker; a party that fortifies a monument forfeits much of its protection. Third, the phrase **prima facie evidence** is doing careful work: inscription does not create the protection, it **evidences** the cultural sensitivity that triggers protection already existing in the law of war.

### 6. The dated global picture, and the discipline for using it

The source gives a dated global figure, and it must be used with its date.

**As of July 2025**, a total of **1,248 World Heritage Sites - 972 cultural, 235 natural and 41 mixed properties - exist across 170 countries**.

**No country ranking is carried here, and the omission is deliberate.** The single-country leader figure that circulates in printed summaries was found on checking to be several inscription cycles out of date, and no replacement national total has been confirmed against an authoritative dated source. The global totals above are therefore used alone, and the distribution argument in section 7 is made **without any national ranking**.

The discipline is the same as for the Indian estate figure in Lesson 8 and it is worth restating because it is where careless answers die. **A total is a photograph, not a fact about the world.** Write "as of July 2025" or do not write the number. The safest usable form in an answer is a comparative statement anchored to the date - that the Convention had, as of July 2025, inscribed over twelve hundred properties across a hundred and seventy countries, of which the great majority were cultural - because the structure of that sentence survives an update even if the digits change.

**One further discipline, stated because it is the easiest one to breach.** These figures carry the date **July 2025 and nothing later**. The **48th session** of the World Heritage Committee, at which the most recent Indian property was inscribed in July 2026, necessarily altered the global totals; **no post-session global aggregate is asserted anywhere in this session**, and the July 2025 figures must not be quietly updated to a newer number that has not been checked. Where a global figure is needed for a date after that session, read it from the Convention's own list and cite the date on which it was read.

### 7. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** The system is political, not expert. The decision-maker is a committee of twenty-one **governments**, elected by other governments, and the expert bodies only advise. Inscriptions are therefore influenced by diplomacy, regional balance and national prestige, and the list is skewed - heavily towards monumental cultural properties and heavily towards certain regions.

**Reply.** The objection's description of the machinery is accurate, and the concentration it complains of is real: a relatively small number of States Parties account for a disproportionate share of the list, while whole regions are thinly represented. **No national total is cited in support of that observation here**, for the reason given in section 6 - the leader figures in circulation are stale and no authoritative dated replacement was confirmed - and the argument does not need one. Three replies stand. First, the **intergovernmental character is the price of the enforcement mechanism**: only States can undertake obligations about territory they govern, and a committee of independent experts with no State membership could recommend but could never bind, fund or monitor. Second, the **advisory-body evaluation is a real constraint** - nominations are regularly referred back or deferred on technical grounds - so the system is not a pure political auction. Third, and most usefully for an answer, the skew has a **non-political cause** as well: nominating a property is expensive, requiring dossiers, boundary definition, management plans and sustained administrative capacity, so States with more capacity nominate more successfully, and the distribution partly measures **administrative capacity rather than heritage value**. The defensible position is that the list is a product of value, diplomacy and capacity together, and that reading it as a ranking of the world's heritage is a category error.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies the **Committee's composition, term, meeting frequency and three functions**, the **three advisory bodies with their division of labour**, and the **ten criteria with the six-four split** - between them the densest block of directly examinable World Heritage content anywhere. The **route diagram** answers any process question. The **nomination requirements** supply the argument that recognition presupposes protection. The **Article 53 prohibitions** are quotable. And the **capacity explanation for the skewed list** is a genuinely analytical point that most answers will not contain.

### Traps introduced here

- Saying "UNESCO declared" rather than naming the Committee's decision.
- Giving the Committee a different size or term; it is **21** States Parties for **six** years.
- Assigning cultural evaluation to IUCN or natural evaluation to ICOMOS.
- Forgetting ICCROM, or treating it as an evaluator rather than an adviser on conservation and training.
- Reversing the criteria split; the **first six** are cultural and the **last four** natural.
- Treating tentative-list presence as a status.
- Presenting the wartime protection attached by inscription as general peacetime legal protection.
- Giving a global or national total without its date.

### Revision notes

1. The **World Heritage Convention was established in 1972**; its mission is to **identify and protect the world's natural and cultural heritage of Outstanding Universal Value**; it is **governed by the World Heritage Committee**.
2. The **Committee** is inter-governmental, **21 States Parties**, elected, **six-year term**, **generally meets once a year**.
3. Its **three functions**: selects sites for the **World Heritage List and the List of World Heritage in Danger**; **defines the use of the World Heritage Fund**; **allocates financial assistance upon requests from States Parties**.
4. **Three advisory bodies: IUCN, ICOMOS and ICCROM** - natural evaluation, cultural evaluation, and conservation and training advice respectively.
5. A nominated site must be of outstanding universal value and **meet at least one of ten criteria**.
6. **The first six criteria are cultural; the remaining four are natural.** A **mixed** property meets at least one from each group.
7. **Criterion (i)** masterpiece of human creative genius; **(ii)** interchange of human values; **(iii)** unique or exceptional testimony to a living or disappeared tradition or civilisation; **(iv)** outstanding example of a building, ensemble or landscape illustrating a significant stage in human history; **(v)** traditional settlement, land use or sea-use, especially when vulnerable to irreversible change; **(vi)** associated with events, living traditions, ideas, beliefs, or artistic and literary works.
8. **Criterion (vii)** superlative natural phenomena or exceptional natural beauty; **(viii)** major stages of Earth's history and geological processes; **(ix)** ongoing ecological and biological processes; **(x)** most important natural habitats for in situ conservation of biological diversity.
9. **Criterion (vi) is associative**, not physical, and usually appears with another; **criterion (v)** carries an express vulnerability clause.
10. A **nomination** requires boundaries, a buffer zone where appropriate, authenticity and integrity, **the legal protection already in force**, and a **management plan**. Recognition therefore **presupposes** domestic protection.
11. Inscription is **prima facie evidence** that a site is culturally sensitive and warrants protection under the **law of war**, alongside the **Hague Convention of 14 May 1954**.
12. **Article 53 of the Geneva Convention** prohibits **acts of hostility** against historic monuments, works of art and places of worship; **using such objects in support of the military effort**; and **making such objects the object of reprisals**.
13. **As of July 2025: 1,248** properties - **972 cultural, 235 natural, 41 mixed** - across **170** countries. Cite with the date. **No country ranking is carried**: the leader figures in circulation are stale, and none was confirmed from an authoritative dated source.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Describe the process by which a property is inscribed on the World Heritage List, and explain what the inscription does and does not confer. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

**The process runs in five stages.** A State Party first places the property on its own **Tentative List**, an inventory of properties it intends to consider for nomination; this is a queue and confers no status. It then prepares a **nomination dossier** establishing **Outstanding Universal Value** against at least one of the **ten criteria** - the first six cultural, the last four natural - and setting out boundaries, a buffer zone where appropriate, authenticity and integrity, **the legal protection already in force**, and a **management plan**. The dossier is **evaluated by the advisory bodies**: **ICOMOS** for cultural properties, **IUCN** for natural properties, both for a mixed property, with **ICCROM** advising on conservation and training. The **World Heritage Committee** - twenty-one States Parties, elected for a six-year term, generally meeting once a year - then decides, and may inscribe, refer back, defer or decline. Inscription follows.

**What it confers.** International recognition of outstanding universal value; obligations on the State Party under the 1972 Convention; access to the **World Heritage Fund**, whose use the Committee defines and from which it allocates assistance on request; advisory and monitoring engagement; and, as the Convention's own position records, **prima facie evidence** that the site warrants protection under the law of war, alongside the Hague Convention of **14 May 1954** and Article 53 of the Geneva Convention.

**What it does not confer.** It transfers no ownership, creates no domestic offence, and does not make UNESCO the manager. Legal protection, custody and enforcement remain entirely with India and its own institutions - which is why the nomination had to prove that protection already existed.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the five stages in correct order 6 + the ten criteria with the six-four split 3 + the four nomination requirements beyond criteria, with protection-already-in-force named 4 + the Committee's composition, term and decision options 3 + the wartime legal status with Article 53 2 + the three negatives - no ownership, no domestic offence, no UNESCO management 2.** Omitting the "protection already in force" requirement costs four marks, because it is the element that proves the final paragraph.

### Concept check

**Question:** A State Party nominates a spectacular ruined city for which no boundary has been fixed, which has no management plan, and which is protected by no national law. The advisory body's evaluation is negative even though the site's significance is undisputed. Explain why significance alone is not enough.

**Model answer:** Significance answers only the **first** of the questions a nomination must answer. Outstanding universal value, established against at least one of the ten criteria, establishes *why the property matters*. The remainder of the dossier must establish *that the property can be kept*, and it is on those elements that this nomination fails on three counts. Without **fixed boundaries**, there is no property in the required sense: it is not possible to say what is inscribed, what lies in a buffer zone, or where any obligation applies, and the Committee cannot later assess whether the property's condition has deteriorated because there is nothing defined to assess. Without a **management plan or system**, there is no answer to who is responsible, what the pressures on the site are and what is to be done about them; a plan is the Convention's mechanism for turning recognition into continuing care. Without **legal protection already in force**, the whole logic of the Convention fails, because the Convention does not itself protect the property in domestic law: it recognises value and places obligations on the State Party, which discharges them through its own statute and institutions. The absence of national protection therefore means that inscription would produce recognition standing on nothing, which is precisely the condition the danger-list machinery was designed to detect later. The case also illustrates the deeper point: the Convention's own procedure embodies the distinction between recognition and protection, by requiring protection to exist **first** - so a candidate who thinks inscription creates protection is contradicted by the procedure itself.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the ten criteria as the whole test. They are the threshold of value, not the whole nomination; authenticity, integrity, boundaries, protection and management carry equal weight, and a nomination can fail on any of them while the site's significance is entirely accepted.

## Lesson 12 - After inscription: monitoring, danger listing, and the loss of status

### Visual: inscription is the start of an obligation, not the end of a process

```text
                |                    |      | HERITAGE SITES         |

                +--------------------+

  THE INDIAN RECORD, AS THE SOURCE STATES IT:
  MANAS WILDLIFE SANCTUARY   previously inscribed on the danger list
                             owing to ETHNIC UNREST
  GROUP OF MONUMENTS AT      previously inscribed owing to the
  HAMPI                      CONSTRUCTION OF CABLE BRIDGES
  BOTH have since been successfully REMOVED after improvements in
  their conservation and preservation.

  AS OF JULY 2025: 53 properties worldwide on the danger list, and
  NO INDIAN SITE on it.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Candidates treat inscription as a finish line. The whole of this lesson is the demonstration that it is a starting line, and that the system is built around the possibility of failure.

The reason this matters for an answer is structural. A candidate who believes inscription is permanent can write only one kind of sentence about it - a celebratory one. A candidate who understands the danger-list machinery can write four: what the status confers, what obligation it creates, what happens when that obligation is not met, and what India's own record shows about both halves. The second candidate has an argument; the first has a press release.

### 2. The List of World Heritage in Danger, stated exactly

The source gives the purpose in two limbs, and the wording is worth keeping. The List of World Heritage in Danger is designed **to inform people of conditions which threaten the very characteristics for which a property was inscribed on the World Heritage List**, and **to encourage corrective action in that regard**.

Both limbs repay attention. The first is a **publicity** mechanism: the list works by making a problem visible. The second is a **corrective** mechanism: the object is not punishment but repair. Together they explain why danger listing is experienced by States as a reputational event - the system has no police, so visibility is its principal instrument.

**The machinery attached to it.** The Committee **can allocate immediate assistance from the World Heritage Fund to the endangered property**. Danger listing therefore combines the reputational cost with an offer of resources, which is a deliberate design: a State that accepts the listing gains access to assistance, while a State that resists it merely delays the visibility.

**The dated global figure.** **As of July 2025**, the Committee had **included 53 properties from across the globe** in the List of World Heritage in Danger, in accordance with the provisions of the Convention of 1972. Cite it with the date.

**The ultimate consequence.** A World Heritage Site **may lose its designation when the Committee determines that it is not properly managed or protected even after its inclusion in the danger list**; thereafter **it is included in the list of Former World Heritage Sites**. That is the end of the ladder, and its existence is what makes the status genuinely conditional rather than rhetorically conditional.

### 3. The Indian record: both halves of the story

The source records the Indian position with unusual precision, and it is the single most useful piece of evidence in this lesson because it contains both a failure and a recovery.

**Manas Wildlife Sanctuary** and the **Group of Monuments at Hampi** were **both previously inscribed on the List of World Heritage in Danger** - the first **due to ethnic unrest**, the second **due to the construction of cable bridges**. **Both have since been successfully removed after improvements in their conservation and preservation.** And **there are currently no Indian sites on the List of World Heritage in Danger as of July 2025**, a position rechecked on **4 September 2026**.

Three features of this record make it worth memorising exactly.

**The two causes are of completely different kinds.** Manas was endangered by **conflict** - a security and political condition over which a conservation agency has no control at all. Hampi was endangered by **construction** - an act of the State's own development machinery. One is an external shock; the other is a self-inflicted wound. Any answer that uses these cases should say so, because it shows that danger listing responds to two quite different classes of failure.

**Both removals happened.** This is the half candidates forget. The system is not only a mechanism for naming failure; it has a demonstrated route back, and India has travelled it twice. That fact is the strongest available evidence that the conditionality is real in both directions.

**The current position is a negative, and negatives are examinable.** "No Indian property is currently on the danger list" is a clean, checkable, high-value statement - and it carries a date, without which it is worthless.

### 4. The benefits of World Heritage status, as the source lists them

Seven benefits are commonly listed, and they should be learned as a list because the analysis in section 5 depends on having them.

1. **Brings international attention to the need for the preservation and conservation of the site.**
2. **Brings tourism to the site, ensuring economic benefits.**
3. **UNESCO provides funds for restoration, preservation and training, if required.**
4. **Promotes close ties with the United Nations and provides prestige and support.**
5. **Enables access to global project-management resources.**
6. **Facilitates creation of partnerships between the government, private sector and non-governmental organisations to achieve conservation goals.**
7. **The site becomes protected under the Geneva Convention against destruction or misuse during wartime.**

Note that the list as printed is **uncritical**: it is a list of benefits with no corresponding costs. A candidate who reproduces it as it stands has written a one-sided answer, which is exactly what the next section exists to prevent.

### 5. Comparative treatment: every benefit with its corresponding cost

| Benefit as listed | The corresponding cost or condition |
|---|---|
| International attention to preservation and conservation | attention is **reciprocal**: the same visibility that mobilises support also exposes failure, and the danger list is the instrument through which that exposure operates |
| Tourism and economic benefit | increased **visitor footfall** stresses fragile and unenclosed structures; the sites most attractive to visitors are frequently the least able to absorb them |
| Funds for restoration, preservation and training | assistance is **on request and conditional**, defined by the Committee; it supplements rather than replaces national expenditure, and absorbing it requires domestic institutional capacity |
| Prestige, United Nations ties and support | prestige raises the **standard against which performance is measured**; failure at an inscribed property is judged more harshly than failure at an unlisted one |
| Access to global project-management resources | access is only useful to a State Party with the **capacity to apply it**; a resource that cannot be absorbed is not a benefit |
| Partnerships across government, private sector and non-governmental organisations | partnership brings **commercial interest** to the site, and conservation-versus-commercial-development tension is a direct consequence rather than an accident |
| Wartime protection under the law of war | it is **wartime** protection; it does nothing against neglect, slow deterioration or development in peacetime, and the obligation not to use the property in support of the military effort runs against the holding State too |

The right-hand column is the whole analytical content of this lesson. An answer that pairs each benefit with its cost produces a balanced assessment automatically, and a balanced assessment is what an *evaluate* or *examine* directive is asking for.

### 6. Why the system works at all, given that it cannot compel

This is the question underneath the whole lesson and it deserves a direct answer. The Convention has no enforcement agency, no inspectorate with powers in national territory, and no sanction beyond listing and delisting. Why does it have any effect?

Four mechanisms, in ascending order of force.

1. **Definition.** Inscription requires the State to define boundaries, protection and management. Even if nothing else followed, the act of producing a management plan for a site that had none is a real change.
2. **Reporting.** The State undertakes to report on the property's condition, which creates an internal administrative obligation to know the condition.
3. **Visibility.** Danger listing publicises a failure that the State would prefer not to publicise. In a system without sanctions, reputational cost is the sanction.
4. **Conditionality.** The possibility of delisting - and the existence of a list of former sites - means that status can be lost, so the State has a continuing interest in the property's condition rather than a one-off interest at the moment of nomination.

The honest summary is that the system converts a permanent national responsibility into a **periodically reviewed international one**, and that its force is informational rather than coercive. Stating that explicitly is a much better conclusion than asserting that UNESCO "ensures" protection.

### 7. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** Danger listing punishes the wrong States. A country facing armed conflict or a natural disaster is listed for conditions it did not choose and cannot fix, while a wealthy country quietly permitting incremental development around its properties is not. The mechanism therefore measures misfortune rather than negligence.

**Reply.** The Indian record shows both sides of this and is the best available evidence. **Manas** was listed for **ethnic unrest**, which is close to the objector's paradigm case of a condition the conservation authority did not choose. **Hampi** was listed for the **construction of cable bridges**, which is the opposite - an avoidable act of the State's own development machinery. So the mechanism does reach negligence, and it reached it in India. Two further replies complete the answer. First, the list's stated purpose is **to inform and to encourage corrective action**, not to blame; and the accompanying power to allocate **immediate assistance from the World Heritage Fund** means that listing a conflict-affected property is a route to resources rather than only to embarrassment. Second, the objection's real force is about **asymmetry of scrutiny**, and that is best answered by the capacity argument from Lesson 11: States with strong administrative capacity are better at both nominating and at managing the reporting process, so the system's attention is unevenly distributed for reasons that have little to do with the properties themselves. The defensible position is that danger listing is a genuine and demonstrated instrument, that it reaches both misfortune and negligence, and that its attention is distributed unevenly because capacity is distributed unevenly.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies the **danger list's two-limb purpose in quotable form**, the **dated global figure**, the **immediate-assistance power**, the **former-sites consequence**, and the **two Indian cases with their two distinct causes and both removals** - which together constitute the single best evidence base in the topic for an *evaluate* directive. It supplies the **seven benefits** and, more importantly, the **paired-cost table** that prevents a one-sided answer. And it supplies the **four mechanisms** explanation, which is the right answer to any question asking how an international instrument with no enforcement power can matter.

### Traps introduced here

- Presenting World Heritage status as permanent and unconditional.
- Reproducing the benefits list without any corresponding cost.
- Naming an Indian property as currently on the danger list.
- Forgetting that both Indian danger listings ended in removal.
- Confusing the danger list with the list of former World Heritage Sites; the second is what follows loss of designation.
- Giving the global danger-list figure without its date.
- Describing the wartime protection as general peacetime legal protection.

### Revision notes

1. The **List of World Heritage in Danger** exists **to inform people of conditions which threaten the very characteristics for which a property was inscribed**, and **to encourage corrective action**.
2. The Committee **can allocate immediate assistance from the World Heritage Fund** to an endangered property.
3. **As of July 2025, 53 properties** worldwide were on the danger list.
4. A site **may lose its designation** if it is not properly managed or protected even after danger listing, and is then included in the list of **Former World Heritage Sites**.
5. **Manas Wildlife Sanctuary** was previously danger-listed owing to **ethnic unrest**; the **Group of Monuments at Hampi** owing to the **construction of cable bridges**.
6. **Both were subsequently removed** after improvements in conservation and preservation.
7. **No Indian site was on the danger list as of July 2025**, a position rechecked on **4 September 2026**. Always carry the date.
8. The two Indian causes are of different kinds: **conflict** (external shock) and **construction** (self-inflicted). Say so when using them.
9. **Seven benefits** as listed: international attention; tourism and economic benefit; UNESCO funds for restoration, preservation and training if required; close ties with the United Nations, prestige and support; access to global project-management resources; partnerships across government, private sector and non-governmental organisations; protection under the Geneva Convention in wartime.
10. **Every benefit has a cost**: attention exposes failure; tourism stresses fabric; funds are conditional and need absorptive capacity; prestige raises the standard of judgement; resources need capacity; partnership brings commercial pressure; wartime protection does nothing in peacetime.
11. The system works through **definition, reporting, visibility and conditionality** - informational force, not coercive force.
12. The usable thesis: **inscription is the start of an obligation, not the end of a process.**

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Evaluate World Heritage status as an instrument for the protection of India's heritage. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The status is best judged as a **conditional obligation with reputational leverage**.

**What it supplies.** International attention, tourism and its economic benefit, UNESCO funds for restoration, preservation and training where required, prestige and United Nations ties, access to global project-management resources, partnership facilitation across government, private sector and non-governmental bodies, and protection under the law of war.

**What each of those costs.** Attention is reciprocal and exposes failure as readily as it mobilises support. Tourism raises footfall at precisely the fragile, unenclosed structures least able to absorb it. Funds are conditional and require domestic capacity to absorb. Prestige raises the standard against which performance is judged. Partnership imports commercial interest and with it a conservation-versus-development tension. Wartime protection does nothing against peacetime neglect.

**The conditionality is real, and India has proved it twice.** The **Manas Wildlife Sanctuary** was placed on the List of World Heritage in Danger owing to **ethnic unrest**, and the **Group of Monuments at Hampi** owing to the **construction of cable bridges** - one an external shock, one an act of the State's own development machinery. **Both were subsequently removed** after documented improvement, and **no Indian property was on the danger list as of July 2025**, rechecked in September 2026. A property that is not properly managed even after danger listing may lose its designation and pass to the list of **Former World Heritage Sites**.

The verdict follows: inscription is the **beginning of a measurable obligation**, its force is informational rather than coercive, and it works only through Indian law and Indian institutions.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the paired benefit-and-cost structure rather than a benefits list 5 + the two Indian danger-list cases with their distinct causes 3 + both removals and the dated current negative 3 + the loss-of-designation and former-sites consequence 2 + the closing thesis that force is informational and operates through domestic institutions 2.** Reproducing the seven benefits with no costs caps the answer at six, because the directive is *evaluate*.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two properties are placed on the List of World Heritage in Danger in the same year - one because of an armed insurgency, the other because a State agency built a road through its buffer area. Does the mechanism treat them identically, and what should a candidate say about the comparison?

**Model answer:** **The mechanism treats them identically in form and differently in substance, and both halves of that must be said.** In form, the listing is the same act: the Committee records that conditions threaten the very characteristics for which the property was inscribed, the listing publicises those conditions, it encourages corrective action, and it opens the possibility of **immediate assistance from the World Heritage Fund**. In both cases the property remains inscribed, remains owned and managed by the State Party, and may be removed from the danger list on documented improvement - or may ultimately lose its designation and pass to the list of **Former World Heritage Sites** if it is still not properly managed or protected. In substance the two cases are quite different and the difference is exactly what a good answer exploits. The **insurgency** is an external condition that the heritage authority did not create and cannot resolve; for that property, danger listing functions principally as a route to attention and assistance, and the realistic corrective action lies with security and political processes outside the conservation system altogether. The **road through the buffer area** is an act of the State's own machinery; for that property, danger listing functions as an accountability instrument, because the corrective action is entirely within the State's control and consists of not doing, or undoing, what it did. India's own record contains one of each - **Manas**, listed owing to ethnic unrest, and **Hampi**, listed owing to the construction of cable bridges - and **both were subsequently removed** after improvement. A candidate should therefore say that the instrument is formally uniform, that it addresses two quite different classes of failure, and that its efficacy is highest precisely where the cause lies inside the State's own decisions.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading danger listing as a verdict on a country's competence. It is a statement about a property's condition and an invitation to act, and the Indian record shows that the invitation is sometimes accepted and the listing reversed - which is the outcome the mechanism was designed to produce.

## Lesson 13 - India's World Heritage record, stated with dates

Progress: 13/17 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the dated Indian inscription ledger, the cultural-natural-mixed split, serial properties and extensions, tentative-list discipline, and the session's single current linkage

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the dated Indian totals and the statement that one Indian property alone is mixed; the tentative-list table naming five cultural entries; and the dated inscription ledger for 2016 to 2026 with component names, criteria and cross-links
CA search: "Ancient Buddhist Site of Sarnath UNESCO World Heritage inscription July 2026 session of the World Heritage Committee India forty-fifth property"
CA found: Yes - the Ancient Buddhist Site of Sarnath was inscribed on the World Heritage List on 25 July 2026, at the 48th session of the World Heritage Committee held at Busan, Republic of Korea, as India's 45th World Heritage property. This is the single current linkage claimed anywhere in this session
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the dated ledger, and the three things it must never become

```text

                                                                    c. 3rd century BCE; monastic-
                                                                    scholastic establishment from
                                                                    the 5th to the 13th century CE;

                                                                    besides smaller shrines and
                                                                    votive structures

       of Mumbai                                                    monument
 2021  Dholavira: a Harappan City                 Gujarat
 2021  Kakatiya Rudreshwara (Ramappa) Temple      Telangana

 2023  Sacred Ensembles of the Hoysalas           Karnataka         SERIAL: three temple complexes
                                                                    - Channakeshava, Belur;
                                                                    Hoysalesvara, Halebidu;
                                                                    Keshava, Somanathapura.
                                                                    Criteria (i), (ii) and (iv)
 2024  Moidams - the Mound-Burial System of       Assam             inscribed 26 July 2024;
       the Ahom Dynasty                           (Charaideo)       criteria (iii) and (iv)
```
