```text
                                                  Tamil Nadu
 2026  Ancient Buddhist Site of Sarnath           Uttar Pradesh     SERIAL: two archaeological
                                                                    zones; inscribed 25 July 2026

 THE THREE THINGS THIS LEDGER MUST NEVER BECOME:

 (1) A TOTAL.  The ledger covers 2016-2026 only. It lists NINE
     inscriptions in that window, which is NOT India's total. Several
     years carry more than one property and many Indian properties fall
     outside the window entirely.

 (2) A LIST OF EXTENSION YEARS.  The Great Living Chola Temples were
     inscribed in 1987 and EXTENDED in 2004 - one property, two dates,
     three complexes. An extension is not a new inscription.

 (3) A TENTATIVE LIST.  Nothing from a tentative list belongs here.
     Pattadakal alone is inscribed (1987) out of the Aihole-Badami-
     Pattadakal grouping that appears together on the tentative list.

 THE DATED CURRENT POSITION, WITH ITS VERIFICATION DATE:
 India has 45 World Heritage properties - 37 cultural, 7 natural and
 1 mixed - verified 4 September 2026. The mixed property is
 KHANGCHENDZONGA NATIONAL PARK, the only Indian mixed-category site.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

This is the lesson where candidates lose the most marks for the least reason. They know many Indian World Heritage properties; they cannot attach a year to most of them; and they carry a total in their heads that was true at some point and is asserted as though it were permanent. The result is an answer full of recognisable names and unusable claims.

The fix has three parts and each is a discipline rather than a fact. Hold **years**, because inscription years are stable. Hold **the total separately**, with its verification date, because totals move. And never let the two mix - a ledger of years is not a total, and a total is not a list.

### 2. The dated ledger, property by property

**2016 - Archaeological Site of Nalanda Mahavihara (Nalanda University) at Nalanda, Bihar.** The property's own statement of value describes a **23-hectare** site with remains from about the **third century BCE**, functioning as a monastic and scholastic establishment from the **fifth to the thirteenth century CE**, comprising **eleven viharas and fourteen temples** besides smaller shrines and votive structures, inscribed under criteria **(iv)** and **(vi)**. The substance of Nalanda as an institution belongs to the ancient-history and rock-cut-heritage topics; what is owned here is the property, its date, its components and its criteria.

**2018 - Victorian Gothic and Art Deco Ensembles of Mumbai, Maharashtra.** The examinable feature is the word **ensemble**: this is an **urban ensemble property**, not a single monument. An item asking "which of the following is a World Heritage property" can use that distinction, because candidates expect properties to be buildings.

**2021 - Dholavira: a Harappan City, Gujarat.** The site is known for its elaborate system of **water harvesting and management**, built through a series of dams channelling water into connected reservoirs, and for an inscription of ten large-sized Harappan signs. The Harappan substance belongs to the architecture-foundations topic; the inscription year belongs here.

**2021 - Kakatiya Rudreshwara (Ramappa) Temple, Telangana.** A thirteenth-century Kakatiya temple dedicated to Shiva, built in the interest of **Recharla Rudra**, a general of **Ganapati Deva**, and noted for its **sandbox foundation technique** and **floating stone**. Recognised as a World Heritage Site in **2021**. The architectural substance belongs to the temple-architecture topic.

**2023 - Santiniketan, West Bengal.** Inscribed in 2023; the artistic and educational substance routes to the painting, colonial-architecture and literature topics.

**2023 - Sacred Ensembles of the Hoysalas, Karnataka.** A **serial** property of three twelfth- and thirteenth-century temple complexes: the **Channakeshava Temple at Belur**, the **Hoysalesvara Temple at Halebidu** and the **Keshava Temple at Somanathapura**. The statement of value cites criteria **(i)**, **(ii)** and **(iv)**, and identifies the Hoysala style by its **stellate sanctum**, a **circumambulatory platform following the sanctum's shape**, a **multi-tiered frieze**, a thematically arranged sculptural gallery and sculpture over the entire exterior surface, together with the **legend of Sala killing a tiger**; it also records that the **artists' signatures** on these temples are an unusual practice in the Indian subcontinent.

**A naming variance recorded rather than resolved.** The international property record names the Somanathapura temple **Keshava**, while a widely used printed table labels it **Chennakeshava**. Both forms are recorded here; neither is silently preferred.

**2024 - Moidams, the Mound-Burial System of the Ahom Dynasty, Charaideo, Assam.** Inscribed **26 July 2024**, a Tai-Ahom royal necropolis of mound-covered vaults created from the **thirteenth to the nineteenth centuries**, recognised under criteria **(iii)** and **(iv)**. The property is not a field of ordinary earthen tumuli: the record describes a **mound over a hollow vault, with a shrine and an octagonal wall**, within a **continuing funerary and ancestor-worship landscape**. The property's own record also identifies **rainfall, erosion and vegetation growth** as management pressures - a conservation particular rather than a changing count, and one used again in Lesson 16. Ahom political and administrative history belongs to medieval history.

**2025 - Maratha Military Landscapes of India, Maharashtra and Tamil Nadu.** A **serial** inscription of **twelve forts**, and India's **44th** property. The component fort names are the examinable detail for a serial property, and the two-State spread is the feature most often missed.

**2026 - Ancient Buddhist Site of Sarnath, Uttar Pradesh.** A **serial** property comprising **two archaeological zones**, inscribed on **25 July 2026** as India's **45th** property. Its former tentative-list status is now obsolete. Sarnath's Buddhist substance belongs to the Mauryan, Buddhist and rock-cut topic.

### 3. The single current linkage in this session

This is the one place in the entire session where a current record is claimed, and it is claimed here because this is the lesson where India's own World Heritage record is taught.

**The Ancient Buddhist Site of Sarnath was inscribed on the UNESCO World Heritage List on 25 July 2026, at the 48th session of the World Heritage Committee held at Busan, Republic of Korea. It is a serial property and it is India's 45th World Heritage property.**

Four points of discipline travel with that record.

1. **What it changes.** It supersedes Sarnath's former **tentative-list-only** status, which a widely used printed tentative-list table still carries. Anyone revising from that table alone would carry an obsolete status, and would answer a "which of these is still on the tentative list" item wrongly.
2. **What it changes about the count.** It makes the **Maratha Military Landscapes of India (2025)** the forty-fourth rather than the latest. Do not call the Maratha Landscapes the most recent Indian inscription after July 2026.
3. **What is not asserted.** The **criteria** under which Sarnath was inscribed are **not stated anywhere in this session**, because they have not been established from the property's own record. Where a criteria set is given in this lesson - Nalanda, the Hoysala ensembles, the Moidams - it comes from the property record; where it is absent, its absence is deliberate.
4. **What must be rechecked.** The inscription is recent, the annual cycle continues, and the count is dynamic. Cite the number with its verification date, always.

### 4. The dated current position, and the discipline of citing a total

**India has 45 World Heritage properties - 37 cultural, 7 natural and 1 mixed - verified 4 September 2026.** The mixed property is **Khangchendzonga National Park**, the only Indian site in that category.

**A discrepancy recorded rather than resolved.** A widely used printed account states, **as of September 2025**, that India had **44** World Heritage Sites, comprising **36 cultural, 7 natural and 1 mixed**. That figure is internally consistent and it predates the July 2026 Sarnath inscription. That printed number is therefore not an error; it is a **photograph taken at an earlier moment**, and it is recorded here precisely so that the mechanism by which totals become stale is visible rather than hidden. The same account also gives, as of July 2025, a global total of **1,248** properties - **972 cultural, 235 natural, 41 mixed** - across **170** countries. **No country ranking is carried**, here or anywhere else in this session: the single-country leader figure that circulates in printed summaries is stale, and no authoritative dated replacement was confirmed. **No global aggregate for any date after the 48th session is asserted either**; the July 2025 figures stand with their date and are not quietly updated.

The rule that follows is the most important single sentence in this lesson. **A total is only as good as its date. Write the date or do not write the number.**

### 5. The tentative list, and what it is not

India's cultural tentative-list entries are printed as on **August 2025**, and five are legible in the copy consulted: **Temples at Bishnupur** (West Bengal); **Mattancherry Palace, Ernakulam** (Kerala); **Group of Monuments at Mandu** (Madhya Pradesh); **Ancient Buddhist Site, Sarnath** (Uttar Pradesh); and **Sri Harimandir Sahib, Amritsar** (Punjab).

Three disciplines attach.

**A tentative list is a queue.** Nothing on it is inscribed by virtue of being on it, and an item that offers a tentative-list entry among inscribed properties is testing exactly this.

**A tentative list changes as entries are inscribed.** Sarnath's presence in that printed table and its inscription in July 2026 are the clearest possible demonstration. The table is not wrong; it is dated.

**A grouping on the tentative list is not an inscription of its members.** **Aihole, Badami and Pattadakal** appear together on the tentative list under an evolution-of-temple-architecture grouping, but **Pattadakal alone is inscribed, in 1987**. Similarly, the **Thousand Pillar Temple**, **Warangal Fort** and the **Kakatiya Kala Thoranam** are tentative-list entries only. A candidate who reads a grouping as an inscription will get all three wrong at once.

### 6. Serial properties, extensions and the component-name rule

Three features of the ledger repay separate attention because each generates its own kind of item.

**Serial properties** are single inscriptions with multiple components. India's ledger contains three in the recent window: the **Sacred Ensembles of the Hoysalas** (three temple complexes), the **Maratha Military Landscapes of India** (twelve forts) and the **Ancient Buddhist Site of Sarnath** (two archaeological zones). **The component names are the examinable detail**, because a serial property is precisely where a candidate can name the property and still fail to identify what it contains.

**Extensions are not new inscriptions.** The **Great Living Chola Temples** were inscribed in **1987 and extended in 2004** - one property, two dates, three temple complexes. An answer that treats 2004 as an inscription year has created a property that does not exist.

**Ensembles are not single monuments.** The Mumbai property is an urban ensemble; the Hoysala property is a set of temple complexes. The word in the official property name is a reliable signal and should be read.

### 7. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** Learning a ledger of inscription years is rote work of the lowest kind. It tests memory, not understanding, and an answer built on it is a list.

**Reply.** The objection is right about the ledger used alone and wrong about what the ledger is for. Three points. First, the years are the **only stable facts in a topic full of moving numbers**; everything else - totals, counts, statuses - requires a verification date, so the ledger is what allows a candidate to be specific without being reckless. Second, the ledger is **not** the answer; it is the evidence base for arguments made elsewhere, and each row carries an analytical hook: Mumbai carries the ensemble concept, the Hoysalas and the Maratha Landscapes carry the serial concept, the Moidams carry a documented conservation pressure, Sarnath carries the tentative-list-to-inscription transition, and the Chola temples carry the extension distinction. Third, the discipline the ledger teaches is transferable: **hold the stable fact, date the moving one, and never convert a list into a total**. That is a habit of argument, not a feat of memory. The defensible position is that the ledger is a tool for precision, and that a candidate who recites it without using its hooks has indeed produced a list - which is a failure of use, not of the ledger.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies the **dated inscription ledger**, which is what allows an answer to name a property *with* a year rather than gesturing at it. It supplies the **45 = 37 + 7 + 1 split with its verification date** and the **Khangchendzonga mixed-category fact**. It supplies **three serial properties with their components**, the **Chola extension distinction** and the **Pattadakal-alone rule**, which between them cover the standard traps. And it supplies the **single current linkage** - Sarnath, 25 July 2026, the 48th session at Busan, India's 45th property - which is the most recent dated anchor available in this topic and the one that supersedes several older printed statuses.

### Traps introduced here

- Converting the 2016-2026 ledger into India's total.
- Citing any total without its verification date.
- Calling the Maratha Military Landscapes the latest Indian inscription after July 2026.
- Treating Sarnath as tentative-list-only, following an older printed table.
- Treating 2004 as an inscription year for the Chola temples; it is an **extension** year.
- Reading the Aihole-Badami-Pattadakal tentative-list grouping as three inscriptions; only **Pattadakal (1987)** is inscribed.
- Naming a serial property without its components.
- Naming a second Indian mixed-category property.
- Asserting criteria for a property where none has been established.

### Revision notes

1. **2016 Nalanda Mahavihara**, Bihar: criteria **(iv)** and **(vi)**; **23 hectares**; remains from **c. 3rd century BCE**; monastic-scholastic from the **5th to 13th century CE**; **11 viharas and 14 temples**.
2. **2018 Victorian Gothic and Art Deco Ensembles of Mumbai**, Maharashtra: an **urban ensemble**, not a single monument.
3. **2021 Dholavira: a Harappan City**, Gujarat - water harvesting through dams and connected reservoirs. **2021 Kakatiya Rudreshwara (Ramappa) Temple**, Telangana - Recharla Rudra, general of Ganapati Deva; sandbox foundation; floating stone.
4. **2023 Santiniketan**, West Bengal. **2023 Sacred Ensembles of the Hoysalas**, Karnataka - **serial**, three complexes: **Channakeshava, Belur; Hoysalesvara, Halebidu; Keshava, Somanathapura**; criteria **(i), (ii), (iv)**; stellate sanctum, shaped circumambulatory platform, multi-tiered frieze, artists' signatures.
5. **2024 Moidams**, Charaideo, Assam: inscribed **26 July 2024**; **13th to 19th** centuries; criteria **(iii)** and **(iv)**; mound over a hollow vault with shrine and octagonal wall; **rainfall, erosion and vegetation growth** recorded as management pressures.
6. **2025 Maratha Military Landscapes of India**: **serial**, **twelve forts**, **Maharashtra and Tamil Nadu**; India's **44th** property.
7. **2026 Ancient Buddhist Site of Sarnath**, Uttar Pradesh: **serial**, **two archaeological zones**, inscribed **25 July 2026** at the **48th session** of the World Heritage Committee at **Busan, Republic of Korea**; India's **45th** property. **Criteria not asserted here.**
8. **India: 45 properties - 37 cultural, 7 natural, 1 mixed - verified 4 September 2026.** The mixed property is **Khangchendzonga National Park**.
9. A widely used printed account states **44 (36 + 7 + 1) as of September 2025** - a pre-Sarnath photograph, recorded as such.
10. Global, **as of July 2025**: **1,248** properties - **972 cultural, 235 natural, 41 mixed** - across **170** countries. **No country ranking is carried**, and **no later global aggregate is asserted**.
11. Tentative-list cultural entries printed **as on August 2025** include **Temples at Bishnupur; Mattancherry Palace, Ernakulam; Group of Monuments at Mandu; Ancient Buddhist Site, Sarnath; Sri Harimandir Sahib, Amritsar** - and Sarnath's inscription in 2026 makes that entry obsolete.
12. **Pattadakal alone (1987)** is inscribed from the Aihole-Badami-Pattadakal tentative grouping; the **Thousand Pillar Temple, Warangal Fort** and **Kakatiya Kala Thoranam** are tentative-list only.
13. **Great Living Chola Temples: inscribed 1987, extended 2004** - one property, two dates, three complexes.
14. **Somanathapura naming variance:** the property record says **Keshava**; a widely used printed table says **Chennakeshava**. Recorded, not resolved.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"India's World Heritage record should be described with dates rather than totals." Justify this proposition with examples. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The proposition follows from a simple asymmetry: inscription years are stable, totals are not.

**Years are fixed.** Nalanda Mahavihara was inscribed in **2016** under criteria (iv) and (vi); the Victorian Gothic and Art Deco Ensembles of Mumbai in **2018**; Dholavira and the Ramappa Temple in **2021**; Santiniketan and the Sacred Ensembles of the Hoysalas in **2023**; the Moidams of Charaideo on **26 July 2024**; the Maratha Military Landscapes in **2025**; and the **Ancient Buddhist Site of Sarnath on 25 July 2026**, at the 48th session of the World Heritage Committee at Busan, as India's forty-fifth property. None of those dates will change.

**Totals move, and they move quickly.** A standard printed source records India as having **44** properties - 36 cultural, 7 natural and 1 mixed - **as of September 2025**. That figure was accurate and is now superseded: the position verified on **4 September 2026** is **45 properties, 37 cultural, 7 natural and 1 mixed**, the mixed property being **Khangchendzonga National Park**.

That same account's tentative-list table still prints **Sarnath**, which the 2026 inscription has made obsolete.

Dates survive the annual cycle; undated totals do not.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = at least five properties named with correct years 3 + the Sarnath inscription with its exact date and session 2 + the superseded 44 figure identified with its own date rather than called wrong 2 + the current split with its verification date 2 + the obsolete tentative-list entry as the clinching illustration 1.** Writing any total without a date forfeits two marks regardless of whether the number is right.

### Concept check

**Question:** A revision note copied from a standard textbook says: "India has 44 World Heritage Sites (36 cultural, 7 natural, 1 mixed). Sarnath is on India's tentative list. The Maratha Military Landscapes are India's latest inscription." Assess each sentence.

**Model answer:** **The first sentence is not wrong; it is dated.** The figure of 44 properties, comprising 36 cultural, 7 natural and 1 mixed, is a correct record of the position **as of September 2025**, and it is internally consistent. What makes it unusable is the absence of the date: written without one, it asserts a present state of affairs that has since changed. The position verified on **4 September 2026** is **45 properties - 37 cultural, 7 natural and 1 mixed** - the mixed property being Khangchendzonga National Park, and the single additional cultural property accounting for the difference. **The second sentence is now false, and it is false for a reason that illustrates the mechanism.** Sarnath did appear on India's tentative list, and a tentative-list table printed as on August 2025 correctly carried it; but the **Ancient Buddhist Site of Sarnath was inscribed on the World Heritage List on 25 July 2026**, at the 48th session of the World Heritage Committee at Busan, and a property cannot be both inscribed and awaiting nomination. The entry is obsolete rather than mistaken - which is exactly how tentative lists behave, since their purpose is to be worked through. **The third sentence was true and is no longer.** The Maratha Military Landscapes of India, a serial inscription of twelve forts across Maharashtra and Tamil Nadu, were India's **44th** property in 2025 and were correctly described as the latest until July 2026; Sarnath is now the **45th** and the most recent. The general lesson is that all three sentences failed in the same way - not by containing a false fact, but by **omitting the date that made each of them true** - and the remedy in every case is the same.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a superseded figure as an error by the source. A printed total is a photograph taken on a stated date, and the mistake belongs to the reader who copies it without its date, not to the account that printed it. This distinction matters because it changes what the candidate does: not distrust the source, but carry its date.

## Lesson 14 - Safeguarding what has no fabric: the 2003 Convention

```text
      |    allows States Parties, communities and stakeholders to    |
      |    SHARE successful safeguarding experiences and models      |
      |    GLOBAL: more than 40 practices selected since 2009        |
      |    INDIA: *** NONE ***                                       |
      +-------------------------------------------------------------+
                                |
      A CATEGORY THAT IS NONE OF THE ABOVE:
      UNESCO CREATIVE CITIES NETWORK - created 2004, seven creative
      fields, eight Indian cities as of August 2025. It is a CITY
      NETWORK, not an ICH list and not a World Heritage list. Never
      add its numbers to either.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Intangible heritage is where the "one UNESCO list" illusion does the most damage. There are **three** mechanisms under the 2003 Convention, they do three different things, and two of them are much more interesting for an answer than the one everybody names. Candidates know the Representative List; they rarely know the Urgent Safeguarding List; almost nobody knows the Register of Good Safeguarding Practices - and India's position on the second and third is a pair of clean, checkable negatives that most answers will not contain.

There is a second and deeper problem. Candidates apply conservation vocabulary to living practice. A practice cannot be stabilised, consolidated or restored, because there is nothing to stabilise. The only thing that keeps it alive is that somebody still performs it and somebody else is learning to. That is why the Convention's verb is **safeguarding** and why every instrument under it is ultimately about **transmission**.

### 2. The definition, and what follows from each clause

The definition was introduced in Lesson 2 and is repeated here because this lesson depends on its detail. Intangible cultural heritage means **the practices, representations, expressions, knowledge, skills - as well as the instruments, objects and artefacts associated therewith - that communities, groups and in some cases individuals recognise as part of their cultural heritage.**

**Five kinds of thing are named.** Practices, representations, expressions, knowledge, skills. This is deliberately wider than "art forms": it takes in a technique of utensil-making, a system of recitation, a body of knowledge about nature and a festival calendar with equal standing.

**Associated objects are included.** The instruments, objects and artefacts associated with a practice come in with it. This is why the mask, the costume, the loom and the ritual vessel are part of the intangible heritage even though they have fabric.

**Recognition is by the community.** Communities, groups and in some cases individuals must recognise the thing as part of *their* heritage. Two consequences follow. A practice cannot be inscribed over the objection of the community that carries it - consent is structural, not procedural. And the community, not the scholar, is the authority on what the practice is, which is why safeguarding plans are supposed to be built with practitioners rather than for them.

### 3. The three mechanisms, and why the differences are examinable

**The Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity** comprises **cultural practices and expressions that help demonstrate the diversity of this heritage and raise awareness about its importance**. Read the stated object carefully: **diversity and awareness**. It is not a list of the most endangered practices, nor of the most excellent ones. It is a showcase, and its purpose is representational.

**The List of Intangible Cultural Heritage in Need of Urgent Safeguarding** is **composed of those cultural elements that concern communities and countries considered vulnerable and require urgent measures to keep them alive**. This is the endangerment list, and it is the structural analogue of the World Heritage danger list examined in Lesson 12 - with one important difference. A World Heritage property is moved *to* the danger list after inscription; an intangible element is nominated *to* the urgent-safeguarding list as its own listing route. **No verified dated global total for this list is carried in this session; what is carried, and is safe, is that no Indian element appears on it.**

**The Register of Good Safeguarding Practices** allows **States Parties, communities and other stakeholders to share successful safeguarding experiences and examples of how they surmounted challenges faced in the transmission of their living heritage, its practice and knowledge to the future generation**; these methods should be **useful as lessons and models that can be adapted to other circumstances, including those in developing countries**. **Since 2009, more than 40 good safeguarding practices from various countries have been selected for the register, and no practice from India has been selected for it till now.**

The third mechanism is the one worth dwelling on, because it is the only one of the three that is not about an **element** at all. It recognises a **method**. That difference produces the most interesting sentence available about India's position in this whole topic, and it is developed in section 6.

### 4. India's dated element ledger

The ledger below carries **inscription years**, which are stable. The **total** belongs to section 5, with its verification date.

| Year | Element | Domain |
|---:|---|---|
| **2008** | **Kutiyattam, Sanskrit theatre** | performing arts - Kerala |
| **2008** | **Tradition of Vedic chanting** | oral traditions and expressions - pan-Indian |
| **2008** | **Ramlila, the traditional performance of the Ramayana** | performing arts and social practice - north India |
| **2009** | **Ramman, religious festival and ritual theatre of the Garhwal Himalayas** | social practices, rituals and festive events - Uttarakhand |
| **2010** | **Chhau dance** | performing arts - Odisha, Jharkhand, West Bengal |
| **2010** | **Kalbelia folk songs and dances of Rajasthan** | performing arts - Rajasthan |
| **2010** | **Mudiyettu, ritual theatre and dance drama of Kerala** | performing arts - Kerala |
| **2012** | **Buddhist chanting of Ladakh** | oral traditions - Ladakh |
| **2013** | **Sankirtana, ritual singing, drumming and dancing of Manipur** | performing arts - Manipur |
| **2014** | **Traditional brass and copper craft of utensil making among the Thatheras of Jandiala Guru, Punjab** | traditional craftsmanship - Punjab |
| **2016** | **Yoga** | knowledge and practices concerning nature and the universe - pan-Indian |
| **2016** | **Nowruz / Novruz** | social practices and festive events - a **multinational** inscription shared with other States Parties |
| **2017** | **Kumbh Mela** | social practices, rituals and festive events |
| **2021** | **Durga Puja in Kolkata** | social practices and festive events - West Bengal |
| **2023** | **Garba of Gujarat** | social practices and performing arts - Gujarat |
| **2025** | **Deepavali** | social practices, rituals and festive events |

Four features of this ledger are directly examinable.

**The 2008 group is the oldest.** Kutiyattam, Vedic chanting and Ramlila are the earliest Indian inscriptions, and Kutiyattam is the one most often named first.

**Only one element is in the craftsmanship domain.** The Thatheras' brass and copper utensil craft (2014) is India's single traditional-craftsmanship element, which makes it a reliable answer to any item asking for a craft element.

**Nowruz is multinational.** It is an inscription **shared with other States Parties**, not an India-only element. Any item asking which elements were inscribed **for India** or **from India alone** is testing this row.

**Deepavali is the most recent.** The inscription was made on **10 December 2025**, at the **20th session of the Intergovernmental Committee**, held at the **Red Fort, Delhi** - **the first time India hosted the session**. The nomination was prepared by the **Sangeet Natak Akademi** under the **Ministry of Culture**, and Deepavali was among **67 global nominations** assessed in that cycle.

That last row connects three lessons at once. The nominating body is the performing-arts academy from Lesson 10 - a direct illustration of the mandate-precision rule, since an intangible-heritage nomination is prepared by the body whose object is living practice. The session was hosted by India, which is a statement about diplomatic engagement with the Convention machinery. And the date is exact, which is what makes the claim usable.

### 5. The total, its date, and the superseded figure

**India has 16 elements on the Representative List, verified 4 September 2026.**

**A discrepancy recorded rather than resolved.** A widely used printed account states, **as on August 2025**, that India has **15** items on the Representative List. That figure predates the **Deepavali** inscription of **10 December 2025** and is therefore a photograph taken before the most recent addition. It is recorded here as dated rather than wrong, for exactly the reason given in Lesson 13: the reader who copies a printed total without its date has made the error, not the account that printed it.

The same discipline applies to everything in this lesson, and in one place it is applied by **refusing a number altogether**. A global total for the **urgent-safeguarding list** circulates in printed summaries; on checking, the figures in circulation disagree with one another and none could be confirmed against an authoritative dated source, so **no global total for that list is carried in this session**. The **more than 40** good safeguarding practices is a **since 2009** cumulative statement. The two Indian negatives were rechecked on **4 September 2026** and are safe to state, because a negative of this kind does not depend on the size of the list it is absent from.

### 6. The two standing negatives, and the argument they support

**India has no element on the List of Intangible Cultural Heritage in Need of Urgent Safeguarding, and no practice on the Register of Good Safeguarding Practices.** Both negatives are sourced and both are safe to state.

They are worth more than they look, because together they support the single sharpest analytical claim available in this lesson.

**The first negative** means that India has never nominated an element as being in urgent need of safeguarding - while simultaneously building a substantial presence on the showcase list. Two readings are possible and an honest answer gives both. It may mean that no Indian element meets the vulnerability threshold, which is an implausible reading given the documented pressure on several traditional performance and craft forms. Or it may mean that **recognition has been pursued through the representative route and not through the endangerment route**, which is a statement about nomination strategy rather than about the state of India's living heritage. The second reading is the more defensible, and it generates the sentence: **recognition is running ahead of any acknowledged safeguarding crisis, and recognition is not safeguarding.**

**The second negative** is sharper still. The Register of Good Safeguarding Practices recognises a **method** that worked. India, with three national academies, a training centre, a manuscripts mission, a cultural-resources scheme for schoolchildren and sixteen inscribed elements, has not placed a single safeguarding method on that register. That is not a criticism of the institutions; it is an observation about what has been **documented and offered as a transferable model**. The argument it supports is that India's engagement with the Convention has been strong on **elements** and thin on **methods** - and that the gap is one a policy answer can legitimately propose closing.

### 7. The creative cities network: a category that belongs to none of the above

Standard accounts record it separately, and the separation is the point. The **UNESCO Creative Cities Network was created in 2004** to promote cooperation among cities **that have identified creativity as a strategic factor for sustainable urban development**. The network covers **seven creative fields: Crafts and Folk Arts, Media Arts, Film, Design, Gastronomy, Literature and Music**. **As of August 2025, eight Indian cities have been incorporated**: **Gwalior - Music (2023); Kozhikode - Literature (2023); Srinagar - Crafts and Folk Arts (2021); Hyderabad - Gastronomy (2019); Mumbai - Film (2019); Chennai - Music (2017); Jaipur - Crafts and Folk Arts (2015); and Varanasi - Music (2015).**

**Why this appears in this lesson.** It is a **city network**, not a heritage list. Membership is a designation about a city's creative economy and urban development strategy; it is not an inscription of any element, it is not a World Heritage property, and it confers nothing of the kind. The trap it generates is arithmetical: a candidate who has absorbed "eight Indian cities" alongside "sixteen elements" and "forty-five properties" may combine them, and any combination is nonsense. **Network membership counts change independently and must never be mixed with World Heritage or intangible-heritage totals.**

### 8. Comparative treatment: safeguarding a practice against conserving a monument

| | Monument | Living practice |
|---|---|---|
| What exists | fabric, continuously | only while it is performed |
| Failure mode | material decay, damage, encroachment | nobody learns it; nobody performs it; nobody watches |
| The instrument | conservation, restoration, zoning, permission | transmission: teaching, practitioner support, documentation, recognition |
| Who must consent | the owner, under statute | the **community**, structurally, under the definition itself |
| What freezing does | preservation is a legitimate aim | freezing kills it; a practice that stops changing has usually stopped living |
| What documentation achieves | a record for future conservation decisions | a record that permits reconstruction if transmission breaks - a **second-best** outcome, not a substitute |
| The international list that flags danger | the **World Heritage in Danger** list, entered after inscription | the **Urgent Safeguarding** list, a separate nomination route |

The row on freezing is the one that carries the most weight in an answer. Conservation of fabric and safeguarding of practice differ not just in technique but in **aim**: the first tries to prevent change, the second tries to sustain a continuing, changing tradition. An answer that applies the first aim to the second object has produced a museum, not a safeguarding plan.

### 9. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** Inscribing a festival with hundreds of millions of participants - or a practice as globally diffused as yoga - on a heritage list is meaningless. These are not endangered; the list is being used for national prestige rather than for safeguarding, and the two Indian negatives prove it.

**Reply.** The objection identifies a real tension and overstates the conclusion. Three replies. First, the objection misreads the mechanism it is criticising: the **Representative List's own stated object is to demonstrate diversity and raise awareness**, not to protect the endangered - so inscribing a widely practised element is the list working as designed, and the criticism should be aimed at the **absence of urgent-safeguarding nominations**, not at the presence of representative ones. Second, inscription does real institutional work even for a flourishing element: it requires an inventory, a safeguarding plan and community consent, and it routes national attention through a body with a living-heritage mandate - the Deepavali nomination was prepared by the performing-arts academy under the Ministry of Culture, which is a concrete administrative consequence rather than a ceremonial one. Third, the concession is the one the objector should press hardest and did not: the meaningful gap is **methodological**, not representational. India has sixteen elements and **no practice on the Register of Good Safeguarding Practices**, which means it has documented many traditions and offered no transferable safeguarding model. The defensible position is that representative inscription is legitimate and limited, that the absence of urgent-safeguarding nominations is a strategy question rather than evidence of health, and that the register's second negative is where the real institutional argument lies.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies the **three mechanisms with their stated objects**, which is the distinction most answers miss entirely. It supplies the **dated sixteen-element ledger** with domains, the **craftsmanship singleton**, the **multinational Nowruz row** and the **Deepavali particulars** - date, session, venue, first Indian hosting, nominating academy, sixty-seven nominations. It supplies the **two standing negatives** and the **recognition-is-not-safeguarding** argument they generate. It supplies the **creative-cities panel** with the arithmetic trap it prevents. And the **monument-versus-practice table** is the fastest route to a high-value paragraph on why living heritage needs a different instrument.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating the Representative List as the only intangible-heritage mechanism.
- Saying an Indian element is on the Urgent Safeguarding List; there is none.
- Saying an Indian practice is on the Register of Good Safeguarding Practices; there is none.
- Treating Nowruz as an India-only element.
- Carrying a superseded element total without its date.
- Adding creative-cities memberships to World Heritage or intangible-heritage counts.
- Describing an intangible element as conserved or restored.
- Assuming the Representative List is a list of endangered practices.

### Revision notes

1. **Intangible cultural heritage** = practices, representations, expressions, knowledge, skills - **and the instruments, objects and artefacts associated therewith** - that communities, groups and in some cases individuals **recognise** as part of their cultural heritage.
2. **Five domains:** oral traditions and expressions; performing arts; social practices, rituals and festive events; knowledge and practices concerning nature and the universe; traditional craftsmanship.
3. **Three mechanisms.** Representative List - **demonstrate diversity, raise awareness**. Urgent Safeguarding List - elements of **vulnerable** communities and countries **requiring urgent measures**. Register of Good Safeguarding Practices - **share successful safeguarding experiences** as transferable models.
4. **Global figures:** **no verified dated total is carried for the urgent-safeguarding list**, because the figures in circulation disagree and none was confirmed from an authoritative dated source; **more than 40** good safeguarding practices selected **since 2009**.
5. **India has none on the Urgent Safeguarding List and none on the Register of Good Safeguarding Practices.** Both negatives are examinable.
6. **India's ledger:** **2008** Kutiyattam, Vedic chanting, Ramlila; **2009** Ramman; **2010** Chhau, Kalbelia, Mudiyettu; **2012** Buddhist chanting of Ladakh; **2013** Sankirtana; **2014** Thatheras of Jandiala Guru; **2016** Yoga and Nowruz; **2017** Kumbh Mela; **2021** Durga Puja in Kolkata; **2023** Garba of Gujarat; **2025** Deepavali.
7. The **Thatheras' brass and copper utensil craft (2014)** is India's only **traditional craftsmanship** element.
8. **Nowruz is multinational**, shared with other States Parties.
9. **Deepavali: inscribed 10 December 2025**, at the **20th session of the Intergovernmental Committee**, at the **Red Fort, Delhi** - **India's first hosting**; nomination prepared by the **Sangeet Natak Akademi** under the **Ministry of Culture**; among **67** global nominations.
10. **India has 16 Representative List elements, verified 4 September 2026.** The printed figure of **15** items **as on August 2025** predates Deepavali and is dated, not wrong.
11. **UNESCO Creative Cities Network: created 2004**; **seven fields** - Crafts and Folk Arts, Media Arts, Film, Design, Gastronomy, Literature, Music; **eight Indian cities as of August 2025**: **Gwalior-Music (2023), Kozhikode-Literature (2023), Srinagar-Crafts and Folk Arts (2021), Hyderabad-Gastronomy (2019), Mumbai-Film (2019), Chennai-Music (2017), Jaipur-Crafts and Folk Arts (2015), Varanasi-Music (2015)**. **Never add these to heritage counts.**
12. A practice is safeguarded by **transmission**, never by freezing; a practice that stops changing has usually stopped living.
13. The usable argument: **India is strong on elements and absent on methods** - sixteen inscriptions and nothing on the good-practices register.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"India's engagement with intangible cultural heritage has been strong on recognition and weak on safeguarding." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is largely sustainable, and the evidence for it is the structure of the Convention's own mechanisms.

**The recognition record is substantial.** India holds **16** elements on the Representative List, verified **4 September 2026**, across all five domains: oral traditions such as **Vedic chanting** (2008) and **Buddhist chanting of Ladakh** (2012); performing arts including **Kutiyattam** (2008), **Chhau**, **Kalbelia** and **Mudiyettu** (2010) and **Sankirtana** (2013); knowledge practices in **Yoga** (2016); traditional craftsmanship in the **Thatheras of Jandiala Guru** (2014); and social practices from **Ramman** (2009) through **Kumbh Mela** (2017), **Durga Puja in Kolkata** (2021) and **Garba of Gujarat** (2023) to **Deepavali**, inscribed on **10 December 2025** at the 20th session of the Intergovernmental Committee held at the Red Fort, Delhi - India's first hosting - on a nomination prepared by the Sangeet Natak Akademi.

**The safeguarding record is where the gap shows.** India has **no element** on the List of Intangible Cultural Heritage in Need of Urgent Safeguarding - a list whose global total is not carried here, because the figures in circulation disagree and none was confirmed from an authoritative dated source - and **no practice** on the Register of Good Safeguarding Practices, of which **more than 40** have been selected since 2009.

**The critical qualification.** The Representative List's own object is to demonstrate diversity and raise awareness, so inscribing flourishing elements is the mechanism working correctly. The real deficit is **methodological**: India has documented many traditions and offered no transferable safeguarding model.

Recognition is therefore genuine, and it is not safeguarding.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = at least eight elements with correct years spanning all five domains 6 + the Deepavali particulars including date, session, venue and nominating academy 3 + both Indian negatives, with the good-practices comparator dated and the urgent-safeguarding total correctly withheld 5 + the qualification that representative inscription is the list working as designed 3 + the methodological-deficit conclusion 3.** An answer that treats the two negatives as proof that India's living heritage is healthy loses five marks, because it mistakes the absence of a nomination for the absence of a problem; supplying an unverified global total for the urgent-safeguarding list costs two.

### Concept check

**Question:** A commentator writes: "Since no Indian tradition appears on UNESCO's urgent safeguarding list, India's living heritage is evidently in good health." Assess the inference.

**Model answer:** The premise is accurate and the inference does not follow. **The premise:** **no element from India** appears on the List of Intangible Cultural Heritage in Need of Urgent Safeguarding, a position rechecked on 4 September 2026. The size of that list is deliberately not stated here, because the global figures in circulation disagree and none was confirmed from an authoritative dated source - and, usefully for this question, the size is irrelevant to the inference being tested. **Why the inference fails:** the urgent-safeguarding list is not a survey of the world's endangered traditions but a **register of nominations made and accepted**. An element appears on it because a State Party nominated it as requiring urgent measures, with a safeguarding plan and community consent; an element is absent from it either because no such element exists or because **no such nomination has been made**. Absence therefore records a **nomination decision**, not a condition. The second reading is the more plausible here for two reasons. First, the documented pressure on several traditional performance and craft forms - ageing practitioners, loss of apprentices, failing audiences and failing incomes - is exactly the condition the list exists to address, and it is implausible that no Indian element meets that threshold. Second, India's engagement has demonstrably run through the **representative** route, with sixteen elements inscribed on a list whose own stated purpose is to demonstrate diversity and raise awareness rather than to protect the endangered. The sharper corroboration is the second negative: **no Indian practice appears on the Register of Good Safeguarding Practices**, of which more than forty have been selected since 2009 - so India has neither declared an element in danger nor offered a method that rescued one. The correct statement is therefore: **India's record shows recognition without a corresponding safeguarding declaration, and that tells us about nomination strategy rather than about the health of the traditions.**

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading an absence from a nomination-based list as a measurement of reality. Nomination-based lists record what States have chosen to put forward; they are evidence about behaviour, not about condition, and treating the two as interchangeable produces confident conclusions from no data at all.

## Lesson 15 - Documentary heritage: archives, manuscripts and the Memory of the World

Progress: 15/17 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the documentary category, the register's levels, India's inscription ledger with its internal count discrepancy, and digitisation as a conservation and claim-enabling technique

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the literature chapter for the Memory of the World register's description and its table of India's inscriptions with years and significance; its institutions chapter for the archives, their laboratory and their portal; and its law chapter for the records statute's appraisal cycle
CA search: "Not sought - documentary-heritage lesson; the single current record in this session is in Lesson 13"
CA found: None sought
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one object, two values, three register levels

```text
        A DOCUMENT HAS TWO SEPARABLE VALUES
        +----------------------------+  +---------------------------+
        | THE CARRIER                |  | THE INFORMATION           |
        | palm leaf, birch bark,     |  | the text, the image, the  |
        | paper, parchment, film     |  | data, the record          |
        | - decays                   |  | - is reproducible         |
        | - burns, floods, is eaten  |  | - survives copying        |
        | - is unique                |  | - can exist in many places|
        +----------------------------+  +---------------------------+
                    |                              |
        CONSERVATION of the carrier      DIGITISATION / COPYING of
        (a Conservation Research          the information
         Laboratory was added to the      (a RISK TRANSFER: the
         national archives in 1940        information survives even
         for exactly this purpose)        if the carrier does not)
                    |                              |
                    +--------------+---------------+
                                   |
              THIS SEPARATION IS WHAT MAKES DOCUMENTARY
              HERITAGE DIFFERENT FROM EVERY OTHER CATEGORY

        THE MEMORY OF THE WORLD: A PROGRAMME, NOT A CONVENTION
        +-------------------------------------------------------+
        | INTERNATIONAL REGISTER   documentary heritage of world |
        |                          significance                  |
        +-------------------------------------------------------+
        | REGIONAL REGISTERS       significance within a region  |
        |                          (for example, an Asia-Pacific |
        |                          register)                     |
        +-------------------------------------------------------+
        | NATIONAL REGISTERS       significance within a country |
        +-------------------------------------------------------+
        THREE LEVELS. A NATIONAL OR REGIONAL ENTRY IS NOT AN
        INTERNATIONAL INSCRIPTION, AND THE THREE ARE NOT ADDED
        TOGETHER.

        THE DATE TRAP THAT THIS CATEGORY GENERATES:
        an INSCRIPTION YEAR is the year the document was entered on a
        register. It says NOTHING about when the text was composed.
        "Rigveda - 2007" means the manuscript material was inscribed
        in 2007. It does not date the Rigveda.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Documentary heritage is the category candidates skip, and it is the category where a single confusion produces an instantly recognisable error: reading an inscription year as a date of composition. "Natyashastra, 2025" written without care reads as an absurdity, and an examiner setting an item on this register is often testing exactly that reflex.

The deeper problem is conceptual. A document is the only heritage object whose **value can be separated from its body**. A monument's value is in its fabric; a practice has no fabric; but a manuscript's value lies partly in the physical object - its script, its material, its marginalia, its history of ownership - and partly in the **text**, which can be copied without loss. That separation is what makes digitisation a genuine conservation technique here and merely an aid elsewhere, and it is the idea the whole lesson is built on.

### 2. The programme and its register levels

**Memory of the World is a programme, not a convention.** It has no States Parties, no treaty obligations and no committee of the kind the 1972 and 2003 instruments have. It has **registers**. The register is described as including **many significant manuscripts from around the world that are recognised for their outstanding value to humanity**, reflecting the **diverse cultural, religious, scientific and historical heritage of different civilisations**.

**The register operates at three levels: international, regional and national.** An entry on a regional register - for example, one covering the Asia-Pacific - records significance within that region; a national register records significance within a country; the international register records world significance. The levels are separate, an entry at one level is not an entry at another, and **their numbers are not added together**.

This is the single most important structural point in the lesson, and it generalises. Every recognition system in this topic has levels or lists that look similar and count differently: World Heritage and its danger list; the three intangible-heritage mechanisms; the three documentary-register levels. A candidate who has learnt to ask **"which list, at which level?"** will avoid the majority of errors this topic can generate.

### 3. India's inscriptions, with their years

India's entries are printed with years and a note of significance. The ledger below carries the years, which are stable.

| Year | Entry | What the source records |
|---:|---|---|
| **2025** | **Manuscripts of the Bhagavad Gita** | a central philosophical text of the Indian tradition with wide readership |
| **2025** | **Natyashastra** | a foundational text on Indian poetics, dance, theatre and aesthetics, by Bharata Muni |
| **2024** | **Ramcharitmanas** | the Awadhi retelling of the Ramayana by Tulsidas |
| **2024** | **Panchatantra** | a collection of ancient Indian animal fables and moral tales |
| **2024** | **Sahrdayaloka-Locana** | a commentary concerned with aesthetics and tantra |
| **2023** | **Abhinavagupta Manuscripts** | tenth-century Kashmiri texts on aesthetics and tantra |
| **2023** | **Saang Lok Collection** | a collection of materials on traditional folk art and performance |
| **2017** | **Gilgit Manuscript** | one of the oldest Buddhist manuscript collections from ancient India |
| **2017** | **Maitreyavyakarana** | a Buddhist text |
| **2013** | **Shantinatha Charitra** | a Jain text narrating the life of Shantinatha, the sixteenth Tirthankara |
| **2011** | **Tarikh-e-Khandan-e-Timuriyah** | a Persian chronicle on the Timurid dynasty in India |
| **2011** | **Laghukalachakratantrarajatika Vimalaprabha** | a Buddhist tantric commentary associated with the Kalachakra Tantra tradition |
| **2007** | **Rigveda** | the oldest sacred text of the Vedic tradition |
| **2005** | **Shaiva Manuscripts in Pondicherry** | an important collection of Shaiva Siddhanta texts preserved in Tamil and Sanskrit |
| **2003** | **Archives of the Dutch East India Company** | a collection of colonial records and correspondence relevant to South Asian history |
| **1997** | **The I.A.S. Tamil Medical Manuscript Collection** | a compendium of traditional Tamil medical knowledge |

**A discrepancy recorded rather than resolved, and it is an internal one.** One widely used printed account states in its prose that **India has 14 entries** on the Memory of the World Register, while the **table printed immediately below that sentence contains sixteen rows**. The two statements in the same account do not agree. A separate dated check recorded **14 International Register inscriptions on 4 September 2026**. This session therefore does two things and refuses a third: it **prints the ledger as it is given**, because the years and titles are the examinable content; it **records the numerical disagreement openly**; and it **asserts no total of its own**. A candidate who needs a number must verify it against the register itself and cite it with a date - which is exactly the discipline the whole topic imposes.

**Two title-rendering notes, recorded so that nothing is silently corrected.** First, the ninth row above is printed in the copy consulted as **Maitreyayvarakarana**; the form used in the ledger, **Maitreyavyakarana**, is the standard rendering of that Buddhist title, and both forms are recorded here rather than one being adopted without notice. Second, several of these titles carry **diacritical marks** in print - for example on the Shantinatha and Laghukalachakratantrarajatika entries - which are rendered here **without diacritics** for consistency with the rest of this session. That is a presentational normalisation, not a correction, and it is declared rather than assumed.

One row deserves a specific caution. The **Archives of the Dutch East India Company (2003)** appears in the source's India list and is described as a collection of colonial records relevant to South Asian history; its title indicates records of a trading company that operated across several territories. Whether it is a joint or shared inscription involving more than one country is **not established here** and is not asserted. The parallel with the multinational intangible-heritage element in Lesson 14 is noted as a possibility to check, not as a finding.

### 4. The institutions that hold documentary heritage in India

Lesson 9 mapped these bodies; here they are read for what they contribute to this category specifically.

**The National Archives of India** is the custodian of public records. Its **Conservation Research Laboratory**, added in **1940**, exists expressly for conserving and preserving important documents on Indian history - which is to say that India built a scientific capability for carrier conservation eighty-odd years ago, and that capability is the institutional expression of the two-values distinction opening this lesson. Its portal **Abhilekh-Patal** - **Portal for Access To Archives & Learning** - provides access to reference media and digitised collections, which is the institutional expression of the other half: information made reproducible and accessible.

**The National Mission for Manuscripts** carries documentation, conservation and digitisation for manuscripts, which are **not** public records and therefore fall outside the archives' statutory remit.

**The Public Records Act, 1993** supplies the statutory machinery for the records side: scope over documents, files, manuscripts, microfilms and images of the Central Government, its Ministries and Departments; a **Records Officer** and a **Record Room** in every such agency; and an appraisal **every twenty-five years in consultation with the National Archives of India**, with documents of value preserved.

**The Central Archaeological Library**, within the Archaeological Survey of India, holds **more than 100,000 rare books and journals, plates and original drawings** - a documentary collection whose original drawings are primary evidence for conservation decisions, as Lesson 8 records.

**The Asiatic Society** holds a library and museum with **more than 117,000 books, 79,000 journals**, maps, microfiche, microfilm, paintings and **2,500 photographs** - and the movement of the **Bairat Minor Rock Edict** from its museum to the Indian Museum, Kolkata, is a small but useful illustration that custody of objects changes over time and must be stated as of a date.

### 5. Digitisation, and exactly what it does and does not do

Digitisation is the technique this category makes possible, and it is habitually overclaimed. Four statements keep it accurate.

**What it does.** It **transfers risk**. The information survives a fire, a flood, an insect attack or a slow chemical decay that destroys the carrier. In a country with a vast quantity of organic-carrier material - palm leaf, birch bark, paper - held in conditions that are frequently uncontrolled, this is not a minor benefit.

**What it also does.** It **reduces handling**, which is itself a conservation gain: a digitised manuscript need not be opened for every reader, and handling is one of the standard agents of deterioration named in Lesson 3.

**What it enables.** It makes the information **citable, searchable and comparable**, and it is the precondition of a provenance claim. Lesson 6 established that repatriation turns on documentation; a digitised, catalogued record is what allows an object or a text to be identified later.

**What it does not do.** It does not conserve the **carrier**. A digitised manuscript whose palm leaves are crumbling is a manuscript that is still crumbling, and treating digitisation as a substitute for physical conservation is the characteristic error here. Nor does it preserve everything: the physical object carries information a scan does not - the material, the binding, the wear, the marginal annotations in a different hand, the pigments - and a digitisation programme that is understood as making the original disposable has destroyed evidence.

The honest formulation for an answer is therefore: **digitisation is a risk-transfer and access technique that preserves the information and reduces handling; it is not a substitute for conserving the carrier, and the two must be funded together.**

### 6. Comparative treatment: the three UNESCO-linked recognitions, side by side

| | World Heritage | Intangible Cultural Heritage | Memory of the World |
|---|---|---|---|
| Instrument type | **Convention**, 1972 | **Convention**, 2003 | **Programme**, with registers |
| Object | a place | a living practice | a document or archival collection |
| Decision body | World Heritage Committee, 21 States Parties | the Convention's intergovernmental committee | the programme's register machinery |
| Levels or lists | World Heritage List; List of World Heritage in Danger | Representative List; Urgent Safeguarding List; Register of Good Safeguarding Practices | International; regional; national registers |
| Advisory or evaluation | ICOMOS, IUCN, ICCROM | evaluation under the Convention's own procedure | register-specific evaluation |
| Can status be lost? | yes - danger listing and, ultimately, the list of Former World Heritage Sites | - | - |
| The counting trap | totals change annually; extensions are not new inscriptions | three mechanisms are not one list; a multinational element is not India-only | three register levels are not added together; inscription year is not composition date |

This table is the consolidation of everything taught since Lesson 11, and it is the single most useful revision artefact in the session for the four-category firewall introduced in Lesson 1. Note that the fourth category - the domestic origin-linked product right - does not appear here at all, because it is not a UNESCO instrument and belongs to a different legal system entirely.

### 7. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** Inscribing famous texts on an international register is a vanity exercise. The Rigveda, the Panchatantra and the Ramcharitmanas are among the best-known texts in the world; they are in no danger of being lost; and the register does nothing for the thousands of uncatalogued manuscripts decaying in private and institutional collections across India.

**Reply.** The objection is strong on the second half and weak on the first. Three replies. First, the register inscribes **manuscript material and collections**, not texts in the abstract: the Rigveda's inscription concerns specific manuscript holdings, and those manuscripts are as perishable as any others - a widely printed text and a fragile physical witness to it are different objects, and the register recognises the second. Second, inscription has a **documentary discipline attached**: a nomination requires the collection to be identified, described, located and assessed, which is cataloguing work that would not otherwise have been done, and cataloguing is precisely what the objection says the uncatalogued material lacks. Third - and this concession should be made explicitly - the objection is right that the register reaches only what is nominated, and that the great mass of undocumented manuscript material in India is reached not by an international register but by a **national documentation and digitisation effort**, which is what the manuscripts mission exists to do. The defensible position is that the register recognises specific perishable witnesses and imposes useful documentary discipline, that its reach is inherently narrow, and that the argument an answer should make is for **coverage through national documentation** rather than for more international inscriptions.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies the **three register levels**, which is the distinction that prevents the commonest error in this category. It supplies the **dated Indian ledger** with sixteen printed rows, and the **honest treatment of the source's own count discrepancy** - which is itself a model of how to handle a conflicting figure in an answer. It supplies the **inscription-year-is-not-composition-date** rule. It supplies the **two-values analysis** and the precise statement of what digitisation does and does not achieve. And section 6's table is the consolidated comparison of the three UNESCO-linked instruments.

### Traps introduced here

- Calling Memory of the World a convention.
- Adding international, regional and national register entries together.
- Reading an inscription year as a date of composition.
- Asserting a total for India's entries when the source's own prose and table disagree.
- Treating manuscripts and public records as the same class of document.
- Presenting digitisation as a substitute for conserving the carrier.
- Assuming an entry in an India list is necessarily an India-only inscription.

### Revision notes

1. **Memory of the World is a programme with registers**, not a convention: no States Parties, no treaty obligations.
2. **Three register levels: international, regional and national.** They are separate and are **never added together**.
3. A document has **two separable values**: the **carrier**, which decays and is unique, and the **information**, which is reproducible.
4. **India's ledger (years stable):** **2025** Bhagavad Gita manuscripts, Natyashastra; **2024** Ramcharitmanas, Panchatantra, Sahrdayaloka-Locana; **2023** Abhinavagupta Manuscripts, Saang Lok Collection; **2017** Gilgit Manuscript, Maitreyavyakarana; **2013** Shantinatha Charitra; **2011** Tarikh-e-Khandan-e-Timuriyah, Laghukalachakratantrarajatika Vimalaprabha; **2007** Rigveda; **2005** Shaiva Manuscripts in Pondicherry; **2003** Archives of the Dutch East India Company; **1997** I.A.S. Tamil Medical Manuscript Collection.
5. **The count discrepancy:** the printed prose says **14** entries while the table beside it prints **sixteen** rows; a separate dated check recorded **14** on 4 September 2026. **No total is asserted in this session.**
6. **An inscription year is not a composition date.** Natyashastra's 2025 entry dates the inscription of manuscript material, not the text.
7. **National Archives of India:** custodian of public records; **Conservation Research Laboratory, 1940**; portal **Abhilekh-Patal = Portal for Access To Archives & Learning**.
8. **Public Records Act, 1993:** Records Officer, Record Room, appraisal **every 25 years in consultation with the National Archives**.
9. **National Mission for Manuscripts** handles manuscripts, which are **not** public records.
10. **Central Archaeological Library:** **100,000+** rare books and journals, plates and **original drawings** - primary evidence for conservation decisions. **Asiatic Society:** **117,000+** books, **79,000** journals, **2,500** photographs.
11. **Digitisation** transfers risk, reduces handling and enables citation, comparison and provenance claims. It **does not conserve the carrier**, and it does not capture material, binding, wear, annotation or pigment.
12. The consolidated firewall: **1972 Convention - place; 2003 Convention - practice; Memory of the World programme - document; and the domestic origin-linked product right, which is not a UNESCO instrument at all.**

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Assess digitisation as a strategy for safeguarding India's documentary heritage. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Digitisation works because a document has two separable values: a **carrier** that decays and is unique, and **information** that is reproducible.

**What it achieves.** It **transfers risk** - the information survives fire, flood, pests and chemical decay that destroy the carrier, which matters greatly for palm-leaf, birch-bark and paper material held in uncontrolled conditions. It **reduces handling**, itself a standard agent of deterioration. And it makes holdings **citable, searchable and comparable**, which is the precondition of any provenance or claim work. India's institutional expression of this is the archives' portal, **Abhilekh-Patal**, and the documentation and digitisation mandate of the **National Mission for Manuscripts**.

**What it does not achieve.** It does not conserve the carrier: a scanned manuscript whose leaves are crumbling is still crumbling, which is why the archives maintain a **Conservation Research Laboratory**, established in **1940**, alongside access work. Nor does a scan capture material, binding, wear, annotation or pigment - evidence that exists only in the object.

Digitisation is therefore a **risk-transfer and access technique that must be funded alongside carrier conservation**, not a substitute for it.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the two-values distinction stated as the basis 3 + three achievements named, including reduced handling 3 + two named Indian instruments 2 + the explicit statement that the carrier is not conserved 1 + the evidence a scan cannot capture 1.** An answer that presents digitisation as preservation without the carrier caveat cannot exceed five, because the caveat is the assessment the question asks for.

### Concept check

**Question:** A note reads: "The Natyashastra was inscribed in the Memory of the World Register in 2025; India now has 14 such inscriptions across its international, regional and national registers." Identify every problem with the second half of that sentence.

**Model answer:** The first clause is accurate: **Natyashastra**, described as a foundational text on Indian poetics, dance, theatre and aesthetics by Bharata Muni, is recorded with an inscription year of **2025**, alongside the **Manuscripts of the Bhagavad Gita** in the same year. The second half contains three distinct problems. **First, it aggregates across register levels.** Memory of the World operates at **three levels - international, regional and national** - which are separate recognitions with separate evaluation, and their entries are **not added together**. A figure described as covering all three at once is not a figure that the programme's own structure produces. **Second, it asserts a total that the available statements do not agree on.** One widely used printed account states in its prose that India has **14** entries, while the table printed immediately below it lists **sixteen** rows; a separate dated check recorded fourteen international-register inscriptions on 4 September 2026. Where a source's own prose and table disagree, the honest course is to record the disagreement and assert no total - which is what this session does. **Third, it carries no verification date.** Register entries are added in cycles, so any total is a photograph; a number without the date on which it was checked is unusable regardless of whether it happens to be right. A corrected sentence would read: the Natyashastra was inscribed in 2025; India's entries on the international register are recorded in the ledger by year, and any cumulative total should be taken from the register itself and cited with the date on which it was checked. **A fourth problem lurks in the first clause** and should be noted even though the clause is accurate: the year records **when the manuscript material was inscribed**, not when the text was composed, and the two must never be run together.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "a UNESCO register" as a single tier. Almost every recognition system in this topic has levels or parallel lists that look alike and count differently, and the habit that protects an answer is to ask, every time, **which list and at which level** - and then to write the answer in those words.

## Lesson 16 - Threats, mechanisms and the instrument that actually reaches each one

### Visual: the threat-response matrix

```text

                    transmitted through ground;    (next 200 m); competent

                                                   conservation laboratory

                    modern materials that damage   minimum intervention;
                    historic fabric                compatible materials

                    excavation destroying context  Article 49's export
                                                   prohibition; inventory
```
