The most probable future framings are the safeguarding family, the conservation-versus-restoration family, the evaluate-World-Heritage-status family and the institutional-architecture family - all four of which are prepared in Lesson 17.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

Four blocks, taken at the points where the session's dependencies close. Each is harder than the lesson-local checks because it requires material from several lessons at once.

## Block A - foundations: categories, vocabulary and conservation ethics (Lessons 1-3)

### Concept check

**Question:** A single claim reads: "This UNESCO-listed craft tradition has been restored to its authentic form and is now permanently protected." Using the six-component test, identify how many separate errors the sentence contains and correct each.

**Model answer:** **Five errors, one in almost every component.** **Category:** a craft tradition is a living practice, and if it has any UNESCO recognition it is as **intangible cultural heritage** under the 2003 Convention - but the sentence's phrasing suggests World Heritage, and if the recognition in question is in fact a **Geographical Indication**, that is a domestic intellectual-property right and not a UNESCO instrument at all. The category must be named before anything else can be assessed. **Instrument:** "UNESCO-listed" names no instrument; the accurate form specifies which convention or programme, and at which list or register level. **Legal effect, first error:** "restored to its authentic form" applies fabric vocabulary to a practice. A practice cannot be restored, because there is nothing material to return to an earlier state; it is **safeguarded by transmission**. Worse, "authentic form" imports the idea that a living practice has a correct frozen version - and a practice that has stopped changing has usually stopped living. **Legal effect, second error:** "protected" confuses recognition with protection. Recognition changes the State Party's obligations and the tradition's visibility; protection changes what some person may lawfully do, and no listing under the 2003 Convention does that. **Date and owner:** "permanently" is false of every status in this topic - World Heritage status is conditional and can be lost through the danger-list route to the list of Former World Heritage Sites - and the sentence implies that some external body now holds the tradition, when a living practice is held by the community whose recognition of it as their own heritage is structural to the definition. **A corrected sentence:** the tradition is inscribed on a named list under a named instrument, in a stated year; the inscription confers international recognition and obligations on India; the practice continues to be carried by its community, is safeguarded by supporting transmission rather than by restoration, and no legal protection against any third party follows from the inscription itself.

## Block B - the domestic legal and institutional machinery (Lessons 4-10)

### Concept check

**Question:** Arrange the following in the order in which each first entered Indian law or administration, and state in one clause what each added: the National Monuments Authority; the duty to report a treasure trove; the Archaeological Survey of India; the prohibition on unlicensed export of antiquities; the Ancient Monuments Preservation Act; the National Academy of Letters; the consolidating monuments statute.

**Model answer:** **The order is 1861, 1878, 1904, 1947, 1954, 1958, 2010.** **1861 - the Archaeological Survey of India**, established under Alexander Cunningham as its first Director-General: it added a permanent state capability for survey, excavation, documentation and epigraphy, which is the institutional precondition of everything that follows. **1878 - the duty to report a treasure trove**, under the Indian Treasure Trove Act: it added the principle that **discovery creates a legal obligation to tell the State**, with declaration to the District Collector or the nearest Government treasury and a penalty on an owner who withholds the prescribed share. **1904 - the Ancient Monuments Preservation Act**: it added the principle that the State may **limit what an owner does** with a monument in private custody, through a conservation agreement barring addition, demolition, alteration or disfigurement, together with a Government first right to purchase on sale; it was amended in 1932. **1947 - the prohibition on unlicensed export of antiquities**, under the Antiquities (Export Control) Act enacted under Sir Mortimer Wheeler as Director-General: it added **export licensing** and the Director-General's **binding determination** of whether an object is an antiquity, and it was replaced by the Antiquities and Art Treasures Act, 1972. **1954 - the National Academy of Letters**, the Sahitya Akademi: it added recognition and support for **literature** across the twenty-two constitutional languages plus English and Rajasthani, a wholly different safeguarding technique from anything in the monuments chain. **1958 - the consolidating monuments statute**, the AMASR Act: it repealed the 1904 Act, the 1951 Declaration of National Importance Act and Section 126 of the States Reorganization Act, 1956, made the Survey **custodian**, broadened coverage to urban and rural archaeological settlements, and introduced the **spatial prohibition** around protected monuments. **2010 - the National Monuments Authority**, created by the AMASR (Amendment and Validation) Act: it added the **machinery** the 1958 Act lacked - a statutory authority, a permission architecture through the competent authority, and monument-specific heritage bye-laws - together with the power to declare monuments of ancient **and medieval** times and to acquire antiquities and control their movement. **The thread through all seven** is a steady widening of the class of persons whose freedom is constrained in the heritage interest: finder, owner, builder, dealer.

## Block C - the international instruments and India's record (Lessons 11-15)

### Concept check

**Question:** Three Indian recognitions are announced in the same month: a place, a practice and a manuscript collection. For each, state the instrument, the decision body, the list or register concerned, what the State Party had to establish beforehand, and whether the status can subsequently be lost.

**Model answer:** **The place.** The instrument is the **1972 World Heritage Convention**; the decision body is the **World Heritage Committee**, twenty-one States Parties elected for a six-year term, generally meeting once a year, advised by **ICOMOS** for cultural properties, **IUCN** for natural properties and **ICCROM** on conservation and training; the list is the **World Heritage List**. Beforehand, India had to place the property on its **Tentative List**, then establish **outstanding universal value** against at least one of the ten criteria - the first six cultural, the last four natural - together with **boundaries**, a **buffer zone** where appropriate, **authenticity and integrity**, **the legal protection already in force**, and a **management plan**. The status **can be lost**: a property may be placed on the **List of World Heritage in Danger** and, if still not properly managed or protected, may lose its designation and pass to the list of **Former World Heritage Sites**. India has twice been on the danger list - **Manas** for ethnic unrest and **Hampi** for cable-bridge construction - and both properties were subsequently removed. **The practice.** The instrument is the **2003 Convention**; the decision body is its **intergovernmental committee**; the list is presumptively the **Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity**, whose stated object is to demonstrate diversity and raise awareness. Beforehand, the element had to be inventoried, a safeguarding plan prepared, and - structurally, from the Convention's own definition - the **community's recognition and consent** obtained, since intangible heritage is what communities, groups and in some cases individuals recognise as their own. Two other mechanisms exist and are distinct: the **List of Intangible Cultural Heritage in Need of Urgent Safeguarding**, on which **India has no element**, and the **Register of Good Safeguarding Practices**, on which **India has no practice**. **The manuscript collection.** The instrument is the **Memory of the World programme** - not a convention, with no States Parties and no treaty obligations - and the recognition is an entry on a **register**, which exists at **international, regional and national** levels that are separate and are never added together. Beforehand, the collection had to be identified, described, located and assessed for significance. **The decisive contrast:** only the first of the three carries an explicit, exercised mechanism for loss of status, and that mechanism is what makes World Heritage recognition a conditional obligation rather than a permanent award.

## Block D - threats, instruments and answer construction (Lessons 16-17)

### Concept check

**Question:** A heritage policy note proposes: a corporate adoption programme for monument upkeep; a national digitisation drive for manuscripts; an increase in the prohibited area around monuments from one hundred to two hundred metres; and a national award scheme for folk performers. Rank these by how much of the threat taxonomy each actually reaches, and identify the threat that none of them addresses.

**Model answer:** **The ranking, from widest to narrowest reach, is the digitisation drive, the zoning increase, the award scheme, and the adoption programme - and the reasons matter more than the order.** The **national digitisation drive** reaches the most, because it transfers risk against **disaster** (fire, flood, seismic loss), against **neglect and material decay** of carriers, and against **dissociation**, the loss of the link between an object and its information; it also reduces **handling**, itself an agent of deterioration, and it creates the documentary base on which any future **provenance or repatriation** claim depends. Its declared limit is that it does not conserve the carrier, so it must be funded alongside physical conservation. The **increase in the prohibited area** reaches the **urbanisation and construction** row comprehensively - encroachment, vibration, altered drainage and loss of setting - which is already the best-covered row in the matrix; its limits are that it addresses one threat only, that it imposes real hardship in dense historic settlements, and that a wider zone that is not mapped, published and enforced creates cost without protection, which is why monument-specific **heritage bye-laws** are the intended correction rather than a larger uniform circle. The **award scheme for folk performers** reaches the **loss of transmission** row, which is the weakest-covered row in the matrix, and that is its value; but it reaches individuals rather than structures, and by itself it addresses neither audience loss nor income, so it is a partial instrument for the problem it targets. The **corporate adoption programme** reaches least: adoption covers **amenities**, the monument remains under the Survey and the monuments statute, and no structural or restorative work passes to the partner - so it improves the visitor's experience without limiting **visitor load**, which is the threat it is most often assumed to address. **The threat none of them addresses is environment and climate action on fabric** - rainfall, erosion and vegetation growth of the kind recorded at the Moidams, and damp, biological growth and salt action on murals and masonry. That threat is continuous rather than discrete, so no permission regime, award or partnership reaches it; only **site-specific conservation planning, documented monitoring and trained conservation manpower** do, and those are capacity inputs that no scheme creates. **The general lesson** is the diagnostic from Lesson 16: India's instruments are strongest against discrete acts that can be permitted or prohibited and weakest against continuous processes.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

Six original questions, none of which reproduces or closely paraphrases a verified examination item. Each is followed by a complete model answer within its stated ceiling and a unique quantified scoring note.

## Original 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

**"Designation is not protection." Examine this statement in the context of India's heritage regime. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The statement is accurate and its force is legal rather than rhetorical.

**Designation** is an act of recognition. Inscription on the World Heritage List under the 1972 Convention changes India's obligations and the property's visibility; it creates no offence enforceable against a private person and transfers no ownership, management or jurisdiction. Inclusion on a **Tentative List** confers even less - it is a queue. Identification under an amenities scheme such as **Adarsh Smarak**, which covers a hundred monuments, changes visitor facilities and nothing about legal status.

**Protection** is a permission regime with offences. It comes from the **Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains Act, 1958**, which makes the Archaeological Survey of India custodian, and from its **2010** amending Act, which created the prohibited and regulated areas, the competent authority and heritage bye-laws; and, for movable heritage, from the **Antiquities and Art Treasures Act, 1972**.

The Convention itself proves the distinction: a nomination must demonstrate that legal protection **already exists**.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the recognition-against-permission distinction stated as the organising idea 3 + three designations correctly characterised, including the tentative list and an amenities scheme 3 + two protective statutes with their distinctive content 3 + the nomination requirement used as internal proof 1.** Any sentence stating that UNESCO protects, manages or maintains an Indian property forfeits three marks, because it is the precise error the question is testing.

## Original 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Distinguish between conservation, restoration and reconstruction, and explain why the choice among them is an interpretive act. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The three differ in **how much is done to the object**.

**Conservation** is the whole set of actions taken to safeguard an object and prolong its life - examination, documentation, environmental control, stabilisation and compatible repair. **Preventive conservation** sits inside it and acts on the environment rather than the object.

**Restoration** returns an object to a **known earlier state** on the basis of evidence, which may mean removing later additions or replacing lost members.

**Reconstruction** rebuilds what no longer exists, and is defensible only with comprehensive documentation and an exceptional, recent loss.

**The choice is interpretive** because fabric records its own history - earlier repairs, weathering, successive surfaces - and returning it to one period erases the evidence of the others. At the great Chola temple at Thanjavur, **Rajaraja-period** paintings carry **Nayaka-period superimpositions**; to restore "the original" is to decide which century the building belongs to.

Hence the defensible default: **documented, reversible, minimum intervention**, with reconstruction as an argued exception.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the three terms placed on a single intervention axis 3 + preventive conservation correctly placed inside conservation 1 + the evidential reason that fabric records its own history 2 + the Thanjavur superimposition case named 2 + minimum intervention and reversibility as the default with reconstruction as the exception 2.** Using conservation and restoration as synonyms anywhere in the answer costs three marks.

## Original 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

**"India's heritage instruments are strongest where the threat is a discrete act and weakest where it is a continuous process." Critically examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is a diagnosis rather than a complaint, and the threat taxonomy supports it.

**Where the threat is a discrete act, the instruments are strong.** Construction near a monument is met by the **prohibited area** of the first hundred metres and the **regulated area** of the next two hundred, with permission through the **competent authority**, oversight by the **National Monuments Authority** created in **2010**, and monument-specific **heritage bye-laws**. Export of an antiquity is met by the **Antiquities and Art Treasures Act, 1972** - a hundred-year threshold, a general export prohibition, dealer licensing, registration and certification, and imprisonment from three months to three years with fine - resting on **Article 49**'s requirement that a monument of national importance be saved from removal, disposal or export. Each is an identifiable act that can be permitted, refused or punished.

**Where the threat is a continuous process, the instruments thin out.** Rainfall, erosion and vegetation growth - recorded as management pressures at the **Moidams** - and damp, biological growth and salt action on murals and masonry cannot be prohibited. Loss of traditional repair skills cannot be legislated. Failing transmission in living traditions is reached by recognition and teaching centres and by nothing at scale: India has **sixteen** intangible-heritage elements and **no element on the Urgent Safeguarding List** and **no practice on the Register of Good Safeguarding Practices**.

**The critical qualification.** Continuous threats are not unaddressable; they are addressed by **capacity** - trained conservators, compatible materials, monitoring, documentation - which statutes do not create. Against an estate recorded at **3,679** centrally protected monuments and sites, that is the binding constraint.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the discrete-against-continuous distinction stated first and used as the spine 4 + two discrete threats with their full instruments, including the zoning bands and the 1972 Act's particulars 4 + two continuous threats with named mechanisms 3 + the two intangible-heritage negatives as corroboration 2 + the capacity qualification with the dated estate figure 2.** Listing instruments without sorting them into the two classes caps the answer at seven, because the sorting is the examination demanded.

## Original 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Evaluate the proposition that community rights and heritage conservation are in fundamental conflict in India. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition overstates a real tension.

**The tension is genuine.** A fabric-centred conservation regime is oriented to arresting decay, and living use damages fabric: footfall abrades, lamps deposit soot, offerings introduce moisture, and continuous occupation limits the access conservators need. Where a site is inhabited, regulation of construction under the prohibited and regulated areas bears directly on residents' homes and livelihoods.

**But "fundamental conflict" is wrong for three reasons.** **First, living use is often part of the recognised value.** The **Moidams** were recognised within a **continuing Tai-Ahom funerary and ancestor-worship landscape**, and **Matangeshvara at Khajuraho** remains an active site of worship within a monument group. Removing the practice would diminish the very significance being conserved. **Second, the community holds conservation knowledge.** Carriers of a practice know a building's seasonal behaviour, its traditional maintenance and its meanings - the "associated traditions, techniques and management systems" that are among the attributes of **authenticity**. **Third, the Constitution does not permit the conflict to be resolved by fiat.** Article 49 is a **Directive Principle** creating an obligation on the State; **Article 29** is a **Fundamental Right** of any section of citizens with a distinct language, script or culture to conserve it; and a Part IV directive does not override a Part III right.

**The correct framing** is instrument choice, not opposition: exhaust fabric-directed measures - drainage, vegetation control, visitor routing, load limits, protection of vulnerable surfaces - and negotiate a management plan with practitioners, before contemplating restriction or displacement.

Conservation without consent is fragile; consent without conservation is loss. Neither is the necessary price of the other.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the tension conceded with named physical mechanisms 3 + two Indian cases where living use is part of the recognised value 3 + the authenticity attribute covering associated traditions and management systems 2 + the Article 29 against Article 49 constitutional structure stated correctly 4 + the instrument-choice reframing with named fabric-directed measures 3.** Treating the community as an obstacle anywhere in the answer costs four marks.

## Original 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

**"Recognition is not safeguarding." Critically examine this proposition with reference to India's engagement with UNESCO's heritage instruments. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is largely sustained, and the evidence is structural rather than anecdotal.

**Recognition is substantial and well distributed.** Under the **1972 Convention**, India holds **45** World Heritage properties - **37 cultural, 7 natural and 1 mixed**, verified **4 September 2026** - the mixed property being **Khangchendzonga National Park**, with the **Ancient Buddhist Site of Sarnath** inscribed on **25 July 2026** as the forty-fifth. Under the **2003 Convention**, India holds **16** Representative List elements across all five domains, most recently **Deepavali**, inscribed **10 December 2025** at the twentieth session of the Intergovernmental Committee at the Red Fort, Delhi, on a nomination prepared by the **Sangeet Natak Akademi**.

**Safeguarding is where the record thins.** India has **no element** on the **List of Intangible Cultural Heritage in Need of Urgent Safeguarding**, whose global total is not carried here for want of an authoritative dated source, and **no practice** on the **Register of Good Safeguarding Practices**, of which more than **40** have been selected since 2009. India has therefore neither declared an element endangered nor offered a method that rescued one.

**Three qualifications keep the judgement fair.** The Representative List's own object is to **demonstrate diversity and raise awareness**, so inscribing flourishing elements is the mechanism working as designed. Inscription imposes real discipline - inventory, safeguarding plan, community consent, and a management plan for a World Heritage nomination. And the **danger-list** mechanism shows recognition can bite: **Manas** and **Hampi** were both listed and both removed after documented improvement.

Recognition is therefore genuine, disciplined and partial; the deficit is **methodological**, not representational.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = both counts with their verification dates and the two most recent inscriptions with exact dates 5 + the two Indian negatives, with the good-practices comparator dated and the urgent-safeguarding total correctly withheld 5 + the Representative List's stated object used as the first qualification 3 + the nomination disciplines named 3 + the danger-list conditionality with both Indian cases and both removals 3 + the methodological-deficit conclusion 1.** Any undated total forfeits two marks; treating the two negatives as evidence of health forfeits five.

## Original 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

**Assess the adequacy of India's legal and institutional framework for protecting movable cultural property, and suggest what a credible reform agenda would address. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The framework is coherent, correctly designed and aimed at the wrong link in the chain.

**What exists.** The **Antiquities and Art Treasures Act, 1972** defines an antiquity in **two limbs** - works of art and craftsmanship, things detached from buildings or caves, things illustrative of past life and things of historical interest, **in existence for not less than one hundred years**; and any **manuscript, record or other document of scientific, historical, literary or aesthetic value, in existence for not less than seventy-five years**; prohibits export except by the Central Government or a person authorised by it; requires dealers to hold a **licence**, **register** and obtain a **certificate**; and attaches imprisonment of **three months to three years** with fine. It replaced the **Antiquities (Export Control) Act, 1947**, which had vested export licensing and the **binding determination** of what is an antiquity in the Director-General of the Archaeological Survey of India. The **2010** amending Act added powers to acquire antiquities and control their movement. **Article 49** requires that a monument of national importance be saved from **removal, disposal or export**. The **Public Records Act, 1993** governs public records through a **Records Officer**, a **Record Room** and appraisal **every twenty-five years in consultation with the National Archives**.

**Where it fails.** Every control operates at **sale** or at the **border**. The decisive loss occurs earlier, when an object leaves an undocumented context: the object survives, its **provenance and evidential value** do not, and without provenance no future claim for return is even formulable.

**The reform agenda** is therefore upstream: **inventory** of holdings in temples, monasteries and collections; **custody and security** at source; **community stake** in objects remaining where they are; and publication practice that distinguishes the record that must exist from the record that may safely be public.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the 1972 Act's particulars - the two-limb definition with both the hundred-year object threshold and the seventy-five-year manuscript, record and document threshold, the export prohibition, the dealer triad and the penalty range 6 + the 1947 predecessor with the Director-General's two powers 3 + Article 49's three taking-away verbs 2 + the Public Records Act's machinery 2 + locating the failure at the first link with the provenance reason 4 + a four-part upstream reform agenda including the publication caution 3.** Proposing only "stricter enforcement" as the reform earns none of the final three marks, since it repeats the layer already shown to be correctly designed; stating a single age threshold costs two.

# REMEDIATION

Five error sets, each stating the error, why it is made, and the correction that must replace it.

## Error set A - category and instrument confusion

| # | The error | Why it happens | The correction |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | Calling any recognition a "UNESCO tag" | the four instruments are announced in similar language | name the instrument: the 1972 Convention, the 2003 Convention, the Memory of the World programme, or a domestic statute |
| 2 | Placing a Geographical Indication among UNESCO categories | both feel like official recognitions of Indian tradition | a Geographical Indication arises under the Indian Act of 1999, is granted by the Registry at Chennai, and belongs to intellectual-property law |
| 3 | Writing "the Memory of the World Convention" | the other two instruments are conventions | it is a **programme with registers**; there are no States Parties and no treaty obligations |
| 4 | Treating the Representative List as the only intangible-heritage mechanism | it is the only one commonly reported | there are **three**: the Representative List, the Urgent Safeguarding List and the Register of Good Safeguarding Practices |
| 5 | Adding creative-cities memberships, geopark designations or awards to heritage totals | all are announced as UNESCO recognitions | each is a separate designation with its own purpose; they are never combined |
| 6 | Applying the cultural-natural-mixed split outside the 1972 Convention | it is the most familiar classification | that three-way split belongs to that Convention alone |

## Error set B - date and number failures

| # | The error | Why it happens | The correction |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 7 | Citing India's World Heritage total without a date | totals are memorised as facts | every total is a photograph; the position verified **4 September 2026** is **45 = 37 + 7 + 1** |
| 8 | Carrying a superseded printed total | textbooks print totals without prominent dates | a widely used printed account's **44 (36 + 7 + 1) as of September 2025** and **15** intangible elements **as on August 2025** both predate later inscriptions; they are dated, not wrong |
| 9 | Treating an extension year as an inscription year | both are years attached to a property | the **Great Living Chola Temples: inscribed 1987, extended 2004** - one property, two dates |
| 10 | Reading a documentary-register inscription year as a composition date | the entry names a famous text | the year records when **manuscript material** was inscribed; it says nothing about the text's age |
| 11 | Converting a dated ledger into a total | a list of years looks like a count | the 2016-2026 ledger covers a window only; totals live separately with their verification date |
| 12 | Asserting a documentary-register total | candidates expect a number | the printed prose and the table beside it disagree; **no total is asserted in this session** |
| 13 | Calling the Maratha Military Landscapes the latest Indian inscription | it was, until July 2026 | **Sarnath (25 July 2026)** is India's forty-fifth and most recent property |

## Error set C - legal and institutional errors

| # | The error | Why it happens | The correction |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 14 | Saying the Survey "owns" monuments | custodianship looks like ownership | it is **custodian** under the 1958 Act; the 2010 Act separately distinguishes **guardianship, purchase and lease** |
| 15 | Reading the regulated area as ending at 200 metres from the monument | the two numbers are stored as one | the regulated area is the **next** 200 metres, so the regime reaches **300** metres |
| 16 | Equating the statutory zones with a World Heritage buffer zone | both are protective rings | different source, different method of fixing, different legal effect; they need not coincide |
| 17 | Forgetting the 1958 Act's three repeals | only the two dedicated Acts are expected | it repealed the **1904 Act**, the **1951 Act** and **Section 126 of the States Reorganization Act, 1956** |
| 18 | Describing a scheme as a statute or a statute as a scheme | both are government measures | a **statute** creates offences and permissions; a **scheme** is executive and can be renamed, moved or discontinued |
| 19 | Saying monuments were "handed over" under the participation scheme | corporate involvement suggests control | adoption covers **amenities**; the monument stays under the Survey and the 1958 Act |
| 20 | Saying individuals may receive assistance from the National Culture Fund | it invites individuals as partners | it enables individuals to **support** art and culture; **individuals are not entitled to financial assistance** |
| 21 | Treating the three academies as interchangeable | all are national cultural bodies | performing arts to the **Sangeet Natak Akademi (1952)**, literature to the **Sahitya Akademi (1954)**, visual art to the **Lalit Kala Akademi (1954)** |
| 22 | Placing the cultural-relations council under the Ministry of Culture | almost every other body here is | it was founded in **1950** and sits under the **Ministry of External Affairs** |

## Error set D - process and status errors

| # | The error | Why it happens | The correction |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 23 | Treating tentative-list presence as inscription | both are official listings | a tentative list is a **queue**; Sarnath's presence on it until July 2026 and its inscription in that month is the demonstration |
| 24 | Saying "UNESCO declared" | it is the reported shorthand | the **World Heritage Committee** decides: 21 States Parties, elected, six-year term, generally meeting once a year |
| 25 | Assigning cultural evaluation to IUCN or natural evaluation to ICOMOS | both are advisory bodies | **ICOMOS** evaluates cultural, **IUCN** natural, **ICCROM** advises on conservation and training |
| 26 | Reversing the criteria split | ten criteria are hard to hold | the **first six** are cultural, the **last four** natural; a **mixed** property meets at least one of each |
| 27 | Presenting World Heritage status as permanent | inscription is reported as an achievement | it is conditional: the **danger list**, and ultimately the list of **Former World Heritage Sites** |
| 28 | Saying an Indian property is currently danger-listed | the mechanism is known, the position is not | **no Indian property** was on the danger list as of July 2025, rechecked 4 September 2026; **Manas** and **Hampi** were listed and **both removed** |
| 29 | Treating Nowruz as an India-only element | it appears in India's list | it is a **multinational** inscription shared with other States Parties |
| 30 | Saying an Indian element is on the Urgent Safeguarding List or the good-practices register | the lists are assumed to be populated | **India has none on either**; both negatives are examinable and both are safe |

## Error set E - construction and discipline failures

| # | The error | Why it happens | The correction |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 31 | Naming threats without mechanisms | threat lists feel complete | name the physical or social process: abrasion, moisture and pollutant load, vibration, altered drainage, salt action, skill loss, loss of context, failed transmission |
| 32 | Describing a monument's architecture in a heritage answer | it is the more comfortable material | one clause for the monument; the rest for category, instrument, body, effect, date and owner |
| 33 | Closing with awareness, coordination or participation | it sounds like a conclusion | a verdict answers the proposition in the question and could not be pasted into another answer |
| 34 | Reproducing the benefits of status with no costs | the source lists benefits only | pair every benefit with its cost when the directive is *evaluate* |
| 35 | Asserting a repatriation | the claim is emotionally compelling | never, without a dated official source naming **object, receiving institution and provenance** |
| 36 | Asserting treaty ratification status from a secondary summary | it looks like a simple fact | it is a binary fact with a date; **not asserted anywhere in this session** |
| 37 | Inventing biography for unfamiliar named figures | an item names five people | reason to the **category**; a fabricated biography is far more damaging than a declared gap |
| 38 | Applying fabric vocabulary to living practice | conservation vocabulary is the default | a practice is **safeguarded by transmission**; it cannot be conserved, restored or authenticated to a frozen form |

## Drill sets

**Drill A - name the instrument.** For each, state the governing instrument, the deciding body and what is recognised: a temple complex; a festival; a palm-leaf manuscript collection; a place-linked textile name; a city recognised for its music. *(Expected: 1972 Convention and the World Heritage Committee, a place; 2003 Convention and its intergovernmental committee, a living practice; Memory of the World programme register machinery, a document; the Indian Act of 1999 and the Registry at Chennai, a product name; the creative cities network, a city - and the last is not a heritage list at all.)*

**Drill B - date or refuse.** Which of these may be written without a verification date: the AMASR Act's year; India's World Heritage total; Kutiyattam's inscription year; the centrally protected monument estate; the Sahitya Akademi's founding year; the number of Indian creative cities; the year of the Antiquities and Art Treasures Act. *(Expected: statute years and inscription and founding years may; every total may not.)*

**Drill C - zone or buffer.** For each phrase, say whether it names the domestic statutory regime or the international management document: the first hundred metres; mapped through the nomination dossier; the next two hundred metres; the competent authority's permission; heritage bye-laws; an area whose boundaries are agreed with the Committee.

**Drill D - list level.** For each, name the list or register level: an element recognised for demonstrating diversity; an element requiring urgent measures; a method that worked; a document of world significance; a document of regional significance; a property whose condition threatens the characteristics for which it was inscribed.

**Drill E - route the question.** Send each to the right body: conservation of a centrally protected monument; permission to build at 250 metres; national documentation of monuments and antiquities; digitisation of a monastery's manuscripts; conservation of a government file; a tribal community's social organisation; cultural education in schools; projection of Indian culture abroad; an unprotected 1930s building.

**Drill F - statute or scheme.** Classify each and state its consequence: the AMASR Act, 1958; Adarsh Smarak; the Antiquities and Art Treasures Act, 1972; Adopt a Heritage; the National Culture Fund; the Public Records Act, 1993.

## Answer spines

**Spine 1 - safeguarding demand (10 or 15 marks).** instrument-fit thesis -> four-category firewall -> three threats with mechanisms -> instruments matched -> one dated anchor -> the capacity and coverage gap -> verdict on currency and specificity.

**Spine 2 - evaluate World Heritage status (15 marks).** conditional-obligation thesis -> the benefits, named -> each benefit's cost -> the danger-list mechanism -> Manas and Hampi, listed and removed -> the current dated negative -> loss of designation and former sites -> verdict that force is informational and operates through domestic institutions.

**Spine 3 - distinguish the categories (10 marks).** the four-row firewall -> the two conventions against one programme against one domestic statute -> what each recognises -> what each does **not** confer -> the tentative-list and register-level cautions -> verdict that category selects instrument.

**Spine 4 - institutional architecture (15 marks).** capacity thesis -> the six jobs: document, regulate, research, train, collect, project -> named bodies with founding years and precise mandates -> the routing rule -> the coverage gap for unprotected heritage -> verdict that reform is alignment and coverage, not new bodies.

**Spine 5 - intangible heritage and safeguarding (15 or 20 marks).** recognition-is-not-safeguarding thesis -> the definition with community recognition -> the five domains -> the three mechanisms -> four or five dated elements across different domains -> the multinational discriminator -> the two Indian negatives -> verdict on the methodological deficit.

**Spine 6 - conservation ethics (10 marks).** the intervention ladder -> minimum intervention and reversibility with their justifications -> authenticity against integrity -> the Thanjavur superimposition case -> verdict that transparency replaces an unavailable neutrality.

## Map 3 - the four recognitions, side by side

| | World Heritage | Intangible Cultural Heritage | Memory of the World | Geographical Indication |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Instrument type | Convention, 1972 | Convention, 2003 | **programme**, with registers | **domestic statute**, 1999 |
| Object | a place | a living practice | a document or archival collection | an origin-linked product name |
| Decision body | World Heritage Committee, 21 States Parties | the Convention's intergovernmental committee | the programme's register machinery | the Registry at Chennai |
| Lists or levels | World Heritage List; List of World Heritage in Danger | Representative List; Urgent Safeguarding List; Register of Good Safeguarding Practices | international; regional; national registers | a register of registered goods |
| Evaluation | ICOMOS, IUCN, ICCROM | under the Convention's own procedure | register-specific | statutory examination |
| Legal effect for a private person | **none directly** | **none directly** | **none directly** | **an enforceable right** against other traders |
| Can it be lost? | yes - danger list, then former sites | - | - | it is a term-limited, renewable right |
| India's position | **45 = 37 + 7 + 1**, verified 4 September 2026 | **16**, verified 4 September 2026; **none** on the other two mechanisms | **no total asserted here**; the source's prose and table disagree | taught in the craft topic |

## Map 4 - the threat-to-instrument matrix, with the coverage verdict

| Threat | Mechanism | Instrument | Coverage |
|---|---|---|---|
| visitor load | abrasion; moisture and pollutant load; informal commerce; worst at open unwalled sites | carrying capacity; routing; amenity provision | **moderate** - amenities do not limit numbers |
| construction and urbanisation | encroachment; vibration; altered drainage; lost setting | 100 m prohibited, next 200 m regulated; competent authority; heritage bye-laws | **strong** |
| environment and climate | rainfall, erosion, vegetation growth (recorded at the Moidams); damp, biological growth, salt action | site-specific planning; documented monitoring; preventive conservation | **weak to moderate** |
| disaster | seismic, flood, fire, to fabric and to collections | documentation and digitisation as risk transfer; structural assessment | **moderate for information, weak for fabric** |
| neglect and skill loss | loss of traditional repair skills; unsuitable modern materials | trained manpower; compatible materials; reversible minimum intervention | **weak** |
| illicit removal | theft and illicit excavation destroying context; unlicensed sale and export | the 1972 Act; Article 49's export prohibition; **inventory** | **moderate**, and downstream of the real loss |
| failed transmission | practitioner ageing; no apprentices; no audience; no income | academy recognition and awards; teaching centres; safeguarding plans | **weakest** |

**The verdict this table produces:** India's heritage instruments are strongest where the threat is a **discrete act that can be permitted or prohibited**, and weakest where it is a **continuous process** that no permission regime reaches.

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## The six-component test and the four-category firewall

- Every heritage claim has six components: **category, instrument, body, legal effect, date, owner**.
- **World Heritage Site** - 1972 Convention - World Heritage Committee - a place.
- **Intangible Cultural Heritage** - 2003 Convention - its intergovernmental committee - a living practice.
- **Memory of the World** - a UNESCO **programme with registers**, not a convention - a document.
- **Geographical Indication** - the Indian Act of **1999** - the Registry at **Chennai** - a product name. **Not a UNESCO category.**
- **Recognition** changes the State Party's position; **protection** changes a private person's permissions; **funding** moves money.
- Inscription transfers **no ownership**; management and jurisdiction remain with India.

## Categories of heritage

- **Immovable tangible** is protected **spatially**; **movable tangible** is protected **transactionally**.
- **Intangible** heritage = practices, representations, expressions, knowledge, skills - **and associated instruments, objects and artefacts** - that communities, groups and in some cases individuals **recognise** as their heritage.
- **Cultural, natural and mixed** is the 1972 Convention's own split; **Khangchendzonga National Park** is India's only mixed property.
- **Archaeological** value lies in **context**; **underwater** heritage needs specialised capability, and the Survey maintains an **Underwater Archaeology Wing**; **documentary** heritage separates a decaying **carrier** from reproducible **information**.

## Conservation vocabulary and ethics

- Intervention ladder: **preventive conservation, preservation, conservation, restoration, reconstruction**; **adaptive reuse** cuts across it by changing function.
- **Preventive conservation acts on the environment, not the object.**
- **Minimum intervention** - every intervention destroys part of the object's own record. **Reversibility** - a later generation will know more.
- Default: **documented, reversible, minimum intervention**; reconstruction is an argued exception.
- **Authenticity** = truthfulness across form and design, materials and substance, use and function, setting, associated traditions. Fails through **falsification**.
- **Integrity** = wholeness: all value-carrying elements present, adequate size, no adverse development. Fails through **fragmentation**.
- **Agents of deterioration** include physical forces, theft, fire, water, pests, pollutants, light, incorrect temperature and relative humidity, and **dissociation** - loss of the object's information.
- **Thanjavur**: Rajaraja-period paintings with **Nayaka-period superimpositions** - restoring "the original" chooses a century.

## The constitutional frame

- **Article 29, Part III, Fundamental Right:** any section of citizens with a distinct **language, script or culture** has the right to **conserve** it; also prohibits denial of assistance from a State-maintained institution on grounds of religion, caste, language or race.
- **Article 49, Part IV, Directive Principle:** the State must protect every monument, place or object of artistic or historic interest; a monument **declared by or under law made by Parliament to be of national importance** must be saved from **spoliation, disfigurement, destruction, removal, disposal or export**.
- The six verbs split into harm **in place** and harm **by taking away** - the constitutional basis for the two statutory techniques.
- **Article 51A(f), Part IVA, Fundamental Duty:** every citizen is to **value and preserve the rich heritage of our composite culture**.
- A widely used printed account prints "spoilation"; the standard text is "**spoliation**".

## The statutory chain

- **1878 Indian Treasure Trove Act:** declare to the **District Collector or nearest Government treasury**; an owner withholding the prescribed share is convicted before a **Magistrate** - **six months**, or fine, or both.
- **1904 Ancient Monuments Preservation Act (amended 1932):** conservation **agreement** with the owner; no **addition, demolition, alteration or disfigurement**; Government's **first right to purchase** on sale.
- **1947 Antiquities (Export Control) Act**, under **Sir Mortimer Wheeler** as Director-General: the **Director-General licenses export** and **decides bindingly** what is an antiquity. **Replaced by the 1972 Act.**
- **1951 Declaration of National Importance Act:** around **450** monuments and sites added to the 1904 list.
- **1958 AMASR Act:** repealed **(i)** the 1904 Act, **(ii)** the 1951 Act, **(iii)** **Section 126 of the States Reorganization Act, 1956**; Survey made **custodian**; coverage extended to urban and rural archaeological settlements; **construction prohibited within 100 metres**, extendable by Government.
- **1972 Antiquities and Art Treasures Act:** antiquity has **two limbs** - works of art and craftsmanship, things detached from a building or cave, things illustrative of past science, art, crafts, literature, religion, customs, morals or politics, things of historical interest and things so declared by notification, **not less than 100 years**; and any **manuscript, record or other document of scientific, historical, literary or aesthetic value, not less than 75 years**. Export prohibited save by or under Central Government authority; dealers need **licence, registration and certificate**; penalty **three months to three years** plus fine.
- **1993 Public Records Act:** **Records Officer**, **Record Room**, appraisal **every 25 years in consultation with the National Archives**.
- **2010 AMASR (Amendment and Validation) Act:** declare monuments of **ancient and medieval** times of national importance; Director-General may assume **guardianship, purchase or lease**; power to **acquire antiquities, control their movement** and levy penalties; **National Monuments Authority** created.
- The thread: the constrained class widens from **finder** to **owner** to **builder** to **dealer**.

## Spatial protection

- **First 100 metres = prohibited area**, construction generally not permitted; **next 200 metres = regulated area**, to **300 metres**, permission required.
- **Competent authority** processes and enforces; **National Monuments Authority** is the statutory body; **heritage bye-laws** are monument-specific.
- The regime is spatial because the threats are: **vibration, altered drainage, encroachment, lost setting, fire and load**.
- **A statutory zone is not a World Heritage buffer zone** - different source, different method, different effect, and they need not coincide.

## The Archaeological Survey of India

- **1861**, under **Alexander Cunningham**, father of Indian archaeology and **first Director-General**; under the **Ministry of Culture**; functions under the **1958** and **1972** Acts.
- Headquarters **New Delhi**; **Central Archaeological Library** with **100,000+** rare books and journals, plates and **original drawings**; **Underwater Archaeology Wing**.
- **Five functions:** survey and excavation; protection and conservation; research and documentation; restoration and management; public awareness.
- **Adarsh Smarak:** a Ministry of Culture scheme, **100** monuments identified for **enhanced visitor amenities** - **Bhimbetka, Hampi, Konark, Elephanta, the Shore Temple, the Taj Mahal, Humayun's Tomb, the Red Fort, Hazarduari Palace, Adina Masjid**. **Amenities, not protection.**
- Estate: **3,679** centrally protected monuments and archaeological sites, **accessed 4 September 2026**.
- **Alexander Rea, A. H. Longhurst, Robert Sewell, James Burgess and Walter Elliot** belong to the colonial archaeological record; **no biography is asserted here**.

## The institutional map, by job

- **Document and record:** **National Archives of India** (Imperial Records Department **1891**, Calcutta, under **Prof. G. W. Forrest**; Delhi **1911**; public research **1939**; **Conservation Research Laboratory 1940**; centres at **Jaipur, Bhubaneswar, Puducherry**; portal **Abhilekh-Patal = Portal for Access To Archives & Learning**); **NMMA, launched 2007**; **National Mission for Manuscripts**.
- **Regulate and permit:** **National Monuments Authority** (2010); **competent authority**.
- **Research and study:** **Anthropological Survey of India** (**1945** Varanasi; **1948** to the Indian Museum, Calcutta; headquarters **Kolkata**; branches **Port Blair, Shillong, Dehra Dun, Udaipur, Nagpur, Mysore**; **Journal since 1952**); **Asiatic Society** (**1784**, **Sir William Jones**; Asiatic Society of Bengal **1832-1935**; Royal Asiatic Society of Bengal **1936-1951**; Asiatic Society from **1952**; **Institution of National Importance 1984**; **117,000+** books, **79,000** journals, **2,500** photographs; the **Bairat/Bhabru Minor Rock Edict** moved to the **Indian Museum, Kolkata**); **IGNCA**; **ICHR**.
- **Teach and train:** **CCRT, 1979**, at the behest of **Dr. Kapila Vatsyayan and Smt. Kamaladevi Chattopadhyay**; New Delhi with **Udaipur, Hyderabad, Guwahati**; **Cultural Talent Search Scholarship Scheme since 1982, ages 10-14**.
- **Collect and display:** **National Gallery of Modern Art**, main museum at **Jaipur House, New Delhi**; **Indian Museum, Kolkata**.
- **Project and publish:** **ICCR, 1950**, founded by **Maulana Abul Kalam Azad**, under **External Affairs**, **Bharat Mitra Samman**; **National Book Trust**; **All India Radio / Akashvani since 1957** under **Prasar Bharati**, **99.19 per cent** reach, **23 languages, 179 dialects**, **Prasar Bharati Act amended 1990**; **Directorate of Film Festivals, 1973**.
- **Outside government:** **INTACH**, non-governmental, associated with the consultative framing of **heritage bye-laws**.
- **Routing rule:** monument to the Survey; manuscript to the manuscripts mission; public record to the archives; community to the anthropological survey; school curriculum to the training centre; projection abroad to the cultural-relations council.

## The three academies

- **Sangeet Natak Akademi, 1952** - India's **first** national academy for the performing arts; collaborates with UNESCO on safeguarding; constituent units include **Jawaharlal Nehru Manipur Dance Academy, Imphal** and **Kathak Kendra, Delhi**; established a **Sattriya Centre**, **Ravindra Rangshala** and a **Centre for Kutiyattam, Thiruvananthapuram**.
- Its awards: **SNA Award**; **Fellowship / Ratna Sadsya**; **Ustad Bismillah Khan Yuva Puraskar** (**below 40**); **Tagore Ratna** and **Tagore Puraskar** (Tagore's **150th** anniversary); **Samman for Theatre Practitioners**; **Award for Folk and Tribal Arts**; **Award for Puppetry Arts**; **Certificate of Honour**.
- **National School of Drama: set up by the academy in 1959; independent 1975** under the Ministry of Culture; **Repertory Company** and **Theatre-in-Education Company (Sanskaar Rang Toli)**; **Bharat Rang Mahotsav from 1999**, second week of January, with a satellite festival in a showcase city.
- **Sahitya Akademi, 1954** - National Academy of Letters; **over 24 languages = 22 constitutional + English + Rajasthani**; **Award** first given **1955**, first Hindi awardee **Makhanlal Chaturvedi** for **Him Tarangini**, plaque designed by **Satyajit Ray**; **Bhasha Samman, 1996**, for languages outside the 24 and for classical and medieval literature; **Dr. Ananda Coomaraswamy** and **Premchand** Fellowships; **Encyclopaedia of Indian Literature**; journals **Indian Literature** and **Samkaleen Bharatiya Sahitya**; gatherings **Samvad, Meet the Author, Kathasandhi, Mulakat, Asmita**; focus shifted to **oral and tribal literature**.
- **Lalit Kala Akademi, 1954** - National Academy of Art; main centre **Delhi**; regional centres **Chennai, Kolkata, Lucknow, Shimla, Shillong, Bhubaneswar**; **National Exhibition of Art**; **Triennale-India from 1968** at **three-to-four-year** intervals with **Rashtriya Kala Mela**; **Garhi Studios**; the **LKA Award** is the highest Government of India award for visual arts.
- Three objects, three failure modes, three instruments: **practitioner support**, **publication and translation**, **studio, exhibition and collection**.

## The 1972 Convention and the World Heritage process

- Convention **established 1972**; mission to **identify and protect natural and cultural heritage of Outstanding Universal Value**; **governed by the World Heritage Committee**.
- Committee: **inter-governmental, 21 States Parties, six-year term, generally meets once a year**; functions - selects for the **World Heritage List and the Danger List**, **defines use of the World Heritage Fund**, **allocates assistance on request**.
- Advisory bodies: **IUCN** (natural), **ICOMOS** (cultural), **ICCROM** (conservation and training).
- **Ten criteria; first six cultural, last four natural; mixed = at least one from each group.**
- Nomination requires: **tentative listing**, **outstanding universal value against a criterion**, **boundaries**, a **buffer zone** where appropriate, **authenticity and integrity**, **the legal protection already in force**, and a **management plan**.
- Inscription is **prima facie evidence** that the site warrants protection under the **law of war**, with the **Hague Convention of 14 May 1954**; **Article 53 of the Geneva Convention** prohibits acts of hostility against monuments, works of art and places of worship, **using such objects in support of the military effort**, and **making them the object of reprisals**.
- Global, **as of July 2025**: **1,248** properties - **972 cultural, 235 natural, 41 mixed** - in **170** countries. **No country ranking and no later global aggregate are carried.**

## Danger listing and conditionality

- Purpose: **to inform people of conditions which threaten the very characteristics for which a property was inscribed**, and **to encourage corrective action**.
- The Committee may allocate **immediate assistance from the World Heritage Fund**.
- **As of July 2025: 53** properties on the danger list worldwide.
- A property not properly managed even after listing **may lose its designation** and passes to the list of **Former World Heritage Sites**.
- **Manas** (ethnic unrest) and **Hampi** (cable-bridge construction) were both listed and **both removed**; **no Indian property was danger-listed as of July 2025**, rechecked **4 September 2026**.
- **Seven benefits, seven costs:** attention exposes as it mobilises; tourism stresses fabric; funds are conditional and need absorptive capacity; prestige raises the standard of judgement; resources need capacity; partnership brings commercial pressure; wartime protection is silent in peacetime.
- The system works through **definition, reporting, visibility and conditionality** - informational force, not coercive.

## India's World Heritage record

- **2016 Nalanda Mahavihara** (criteria iv, vi; 23 hectares; c. 3rd century BCE; 5th-13th century CE; **11 viharas and 14 temples**); **2018 Victorian Gothic and Art Deco Ensembles of Mumbai** (an **ensemble**); **2021 Dholavira** and **Ramappa**; **2023 Santiniketan** and the **Sacred Ensembles of the Hoysalas** (serial; **Channakeshava/Belur, Hoysalesvara/Halebidu, Keshava/Somanathapura**; criteria i, ii, iv); **2024 Moidams** (**26 July**; criteria iii, iv); **2025 Maratha Military Landscapes** (serial; **twelve forts**; Maharashtra and Tamil Nadu); **2026 Ancient Buddhist Site of Sarnath** (serial; **two archaeological zones**; **25 July 2026**; **48th session**, **Busan**).
- **India: 45 properties - 37 cultural, 7 natural, 1 mixed - verified 4 September 2026.** A widely used printed account's **44 (36+7+1) as of September 2025** is a pre-Sarnath photograph.
- **Great Living Chola Temples: inscribed 1987, extended 2004.**
- **Pattadakal alone (1987)** is inscribed from the Aihole-Badami-Pattadakal tentative grouping.
- Tentative-list entries printed **as on August 2025** include **Bishnupur, Mattancherry Palace, Mandu, Sarnath** (now obsolete) and **Sri Harimandir Sahib**.
- **Somanathapura naming variance:** property record **Keshava**, a widely used printed table **Chennakeshava**.

## The 2003 Convention and India's living heritage

- **Five domains:** oral traditions and expressions; performing arts; social practices, rituals and festive events; knowledge and practices concerning nature and the universe; traditional craftsmanship.
- **Three mechanisms:** Representative List (**diversity and awareness**); Urgent Safeguarding List (**vulnerable, urgent measures**; **no verified global total carried**); Register of Good Safeguarding Practices (**methods**; **more than 40** since 2009).
- **India has none on the Urgent Safeguarding List and none on the good-practices register.**
- **India's elements:** **2008** Kutiyattam, Vedic chanting, Ramlila; **2009** Ramman; **2010** Chhau, Kalbelia, Mudiyettu; **2012** Buddhist chanting of Ladakh; **2013** Sankirtana; **2014** Thatheras of Jandiala Guru (**the only craftsmanship element**); **2016** Yoga and **Nowruz** (**multinational**); **2017** Kumbh Mela; **2021** Durga Puja in Kolkata; **2023** Garba of Gujarat; **2025** Deepavali.
- **Deepavali: 10 December 2025**, **20th session** of the Intergovernmental Committee, **Red Fort, Delhi**, **India's first hosting**; nomination by the **Sangeet Natak Akademi** under the **Ministry of Culture**; among **67** global nominations.
- **India: 16 elements, verified 4 September 2026.** The printed figure of **15 as on August 2025** predates Deepavali.
- **UNESCO Creative Cities Network: created 2004**; **seven fields**; **eight Indian cities as of August 2025** - **Gwalior-Music (2023), Kozhikode-Literature (2023), Srinagar-Crafts and Folk Arts (2021), Hyderabad-Gastronomy (2019), Mumbai-Film (2019), Chennai-Music (2017), Jaipur-Crafts and Folk Arts (2015), Varanasi-Music (2015)**. **Never added to heritage counts.**

## Documentary heritage

- Memory of the World is a **programme**, with **international, regional and national** registers that are **never added together**.
- A document has a decaying **carrier** and reproducible **information**.
- **India's entries by year:** **2025** Bhagavad Gita manuscripts, Natyashastra; **2024** Ramcharitmanas, Panchatantra, Sahrdayaloka-Locana; **2023** Abhinavagupta Manuscripts, Saang Lok Collection; **2017** Gilgit Manuscript, Maitreyavyakarana; **2013** Shantinatha Charitra; **2011** Tarikh-e-Khandan-e-Timuriyah, Laghukalachakratantrarajatika Vimalaprabha; **2007** Rigveda; **2005** Shaiva Manuscripts in Pondicherry; **2003** Archives of the Dutch East India Company; **1997** I.A.S. Tamil Medical Manuscript Collection.
- **The count disagreement:** the printed prose says **14** while the table beside it prints **sixteen** rows. **No total is asserted in this session.**
- **An inscription year is not a composition date.**
- **Digitisation** transfers risk, reduces handling and enables citation and provenance claims; it **does not conserve the carrier**, and does not capture material, binding, wear, annotation or pigment.

## Movable heritage, illicit trade and repatriation

- The chain: **removal -> undocumented hands -> sale -> export -> foreign collection -> claim**. The decisive loss is at the **first** link, where **provenance** dies.
- Instruments bite at **sale** and **export**; the real loss is upstream, so the binding constraint is **inventory**.
- International instruments **by function only**: the **1954 Hague Convention** (armed conflict); **Article 53 of the Geneva Convention** (three prohibitions); the **1970 UNESCO Convention** (illicit import, export and transfer of ownership); the **1995 UNIDROIT Convention** (stolen or illegally exported objects); **ICOM Red Lists** (at-risk object categories); **Blue Shield** (cultural property in conflict and disaster).
- **India's ratification status for these instruments is not asserted anywhere in this session** and must be checked against the depositary's own list.
- **Repatriation discipline:** never assert a return without a dated official source naming **object, receiving institution and provenance**.

## Threats, schemes, funding and community

- Threats with mechanisms: **visitor load** (abrasion, moisture and pollutant load, informal commerce, worst at open unwalled sites); **construction** (encroachment, vibration, altered drainage, lost setting); **climate** (rainfall, erosion, vegetation growth at the **Moidams**; damp, biological growth, salt action); **disaster** (seismic, flood, fire); **neglect** (lost repair skills, unsuitable modern materials); **illicit trade** (removal, lost context); **failed transmission** (ageing practitioners, no apprentices, no audience, no income).
- **Management plan** = responsibility, pressures, condition, actions, monitoring; a **World Heritage nomination requirement**. **Carrying capacity** = site-specific and contested.
- **Adopt a Heritage - "Apni Dharohar, Apni Pehchaan":** a **Monument Mitra** is a **public or private sector company**; **originally launched by the Ministry of Tourism**, **revamped 2023**, **transferred to the Ministry of Culture**; **Corporate Social Responsibility** route; **amenities only**, with nothing structural or legal passing to the partner.
- **National Culture Fund: 1996**, under the **Charitable Endowment Act, 1890**; a **funding mechanism, not an authority**; **Council chaired by the Union Minister of Culture** plus an **Executive Committee**; eligible applicants are **State Governments and voluntary organisations registered under the Societies Registration Act or as a Public Trust for at least two years**; **individuals are not entitled**; **donations attract tax benefit**. Naming variance: **Charitable Endowment / Endowments Act, 1890**.
- **Two tourism-side heritage programmes are a declared bounded gap**; no particulars are asserted.
- **Community rights:** **Matangeshvara at Khajuraho** is an active site of worship; the **Moidams** sit in a **continuing funerary and ancestor-worship landscape**. Access, ritual use and livelihood are **conservation variables**, and **Article 29** is engaged.

## Answer construction

- **Five theses:** instrument-fit, conditionality, capacity, living heritage, authenticity. State one first.
- Body in rows: **pressure -> mechanism -> instrument -> what it does not reach**.
- **10 marks:** thesis, category or instrument, two named institutions or provisions, one limitation, verdict. **15:** add the firewall, the statutory and constitutional frame, two named cases. **20:** add the threat taxonomy, conservation versus restoration, community rights and the tourism trade-off, documentation and repatriation discipline, dated anchors.
- **Stable:** statute, institution, inscription and extension years; articles and Parts; criteria numbers for a named property. **Dynamic, always dated:** every total and every scheme's current version; the global World Heritage and danger-list figures carry **July 2025** and **no later aggregate is asserted**.
- **Never asserted here:** undated repatriations; budgets, staff, visitor and carrying-capacity figures; the particulars of two tourism-side programmes, which are a **named boundary with particulars withheld**; treaty ratification statuses; criteria not taken from a property record; **any national ranking of World Heritage properties**; **any global total for the Urgent Safeguarding List**.
- **Two prohibited narratives:** dying heritage must be saved; the UNESCO tag saved it. Replace each with mechanism, instrument and gap.
- **The strongest single move:** sixteen intangible-heritage elements, **none** on the Urgent Safeguarding List and **none** on the good-practices register - recognition running ahead of any acknowledged safeguarding crisis.

# COVERAGE MATRIX

## A. Lesson-level coverage ledger

Every accepted lesson is shown against the material it discharges. A lesson is complete only if its row shows core owner content, enrichment content where owned, book evidence, and its practice elements.

| Lesson | Core owner units discharged | Enrichment units discharged | Core art-source evidence used | Practice |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | snapshot and syllabus fit; essential vocabulary; the four-category firewall; the boundary statements with polity, economy and the other art-and-culture topics; the sovereignty rule | the recognition-against-trade-off framing opened; the institutional-mandate-precision principle introduced | the chapter openings on World Heritage, intangible heritage, cultural institutions and law and culture | concept check; lesson-local 10-mark model |
| 2 | the four categories restated as seven objects; tangible, intangible, natural, mixed, movable, archaeological, underwater and documentary distinctions; the mixed-category Indian anchor | the category-selects-instrument rule; the irreducible-limit analysis per category | the World Heritage chapter's cultural-natural-mixed statement and mixed-category note; the intangible definition; the antiquity definition; the underwater wing record | concept check; lesson-local 15-mark model |
| 3 | conservation against restoration; authenticity; the worked superimposition case; documentation as the least contested instrument | preservation, reconstruction, adaptive reuse, preventive and risk-based conservation; integrity as distinct from authenticity; the agents of deterioration including dissociation | the institutions chapter's five-function list distinguishing protection, conservation, restoration and management; the archives' conservation laboratory record | concept check; lesson-local 10-mark model |

## Bounded-draft and bounded-research declaration

This session was generated as a bounded draft under the bounded-research rule. The substantive base is the complete core owner, the complete advanced owner and one core OCR art and heritage source. External work was confined to three declared purposes.

**(a) PYQ verification and routing.** The routing ledgers and the locally held official papers named in the manifest above. The **official 2024 Set-A key was not consulted** and the **provisional 2026 Set-A key was not consulted**. No official Preliminary key for 2023 is held in this repository. The reproducing core source prints its own answer keys beneath each of its reproduced objective lists; those keys were **encountered during extraction and were not carried into this session**.

**(b) One bounded material-fact pass.** The list of items was fixed before searching and is reproduced here in full, with the outcome of each.

| Item | Why it was on the list | Outcome |
|---|---|---|
| The **statutory basis, function and zoning architecture of the National Monuments Authority** | the core OCR source states only that the 1958 Act prohibits construction within 100 metres and that the radius may be increased; without the authority, the competent authority and the regulated band, the topic's most frequently tested numerical pair could not be taught correctly | **Resolved.** The authority is statutory under the Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains (Amendment and Validation) Act, 2010; the first 100 metres form a prohibited area, the next 200 metres a regulated area in which construction, reconstruction, repair and renovation require prior permission through the competent authority, applying monument-specific heritage bye-laws. Recorded in Lesson 7, with the OCR source's compressed formulation printed beside it as a recorded discrepancy. **No form, fee, timeline or case particular is asserted** |
| **Founding year, character and mandate for four institutions whose entries in the core OCR source are sidebars carrying headings only** - the arts centre, the historical-research council, the heritage trust and the manuscripts mission | four institutions named in the syllabus scope are printed in the held copy as linked headings with no recoverable body text; omitting them would create a real coverage gap in the institutional map | **Partially resolved, and the limits declared.** The arts centre is recorded as an autonomous centre under the Ministry of Culture for research and dissemination across the arts; the historical-research council as an autonomous body for historical research; the heritage trust as a non-governmental heritage organisation associated with the consultative framing of heritage bye-laws; and the manuscripts mission by its documentation, conservation and digitisation function. **No founding year, office-holder, programme name, budget or scheme is asserted for the arts centre or the historical-research council**, and those remain declared gaps |
| The **register levels of the documentary-heritage programme** | the core OCR source describes only the international register, while the syllabus scope expressly requires register levels; without the levels, the commonest counting error in this category could not be prevented | **Resolved at the structural level only.** The programme operates registers at **international, regional and national** levels, which are separate and are not added together. Recorded in Lesson 15. **No regional or national Indian entry, count or name is asserted** |
| The **function of the international instruments against illicit trafficking and armed-conflict damage**, and **India's party status** for each | the syllabus scope requires the international bodies and the illicit-trafficking dimension, and the core OCR source carries only the 1954 Hague Convention and Article 53 of the Geneva Convention | **Split outcome, declared.** The **functions** of the 1970 UNESCO Convention, the 1995 UNIDROIT Convention, the published at-risk object classifications of the international museum body and the conflict-and-disaster role of the cultural-property protection committee were established and are taught in Lesson 6. **India's ratification or accession status for the 1954 Hague Convention, its 1999 Second Protocol, the 1970 Convention, the 1995 UNIDROIT Convention and the 2001 Underwater Heritage Convention was NOT resolved** from an official depositary list in this pass, and **no party-status claim appears anywhere in this session** |
| Whether the **most recent Indian World Heritage inscription** recorded by the canonical owner is confirmable, with its exact date and session | the core OCR source's Indian total and tentative-list table both predate it, and several of this session's status statements turn on it | **Resolved.** The Ancient Buddhist Site of Sarnath was inscribed on **25 July 2026**, at the **48th session** of the World Heritage Committee held at **Busan, Republic of Korea**, as India's **45th** property, a serial property comprising two archaeological zones. Recorded in Lesson 13 as this session's single current linkage. **The criteria under which it was inscribed were not established from the property's own record in this pass and are not asserted anywhere** |
| The **exact statutory definition of an antiquity** under the 1972 Act, and in particular whether a separate threshold applies to manuscripts, records and documents | the core OCR source compresses the definition into a single hundred-year rule and places manuscripts alongside paintings under it; if the statute distinguishes them, the compressed rendering would teach a wrong rule on a directly examinable point | **Resolved from the statutory text.** The definition has **two limbs**: the enumerated classes of object - coins, sculpture, painting, epigraphs and other works of art or craftsmanship; things detached from a building or cave; things illustrative of science, art, crafts, literature, religion, customs, morals or politics in bygone ages; things of historical interest; and things declared antiquities by notification - **in existence for not less than one hundred years**; and, separately, any **manuscript, record or other document of scientific, historical, literary or aesthetic value in existence for not less than seventy-five years**. Both limbs are taught in Lesson 6 and harmonised across the traps, revision notes, register notes, maps and Mains models; the compressed printed rendering is recorded as a discrepancy |
| A **current, authoritative, dated national total** for the State Party holding the most World Heritage properties | the widely reproduced leader figure carried by printed summaries was suspected of being several inscription cycles out of date, and a stale ranking in the teaching would be a plain factual error | **Not resolved, and the figure is therefore removed entirely.** The printed leader figure was confirmed to be stale, and no replacement national total could be confirmed from an authoritative dated source in this pass. **Every occurrence of the stale ranking was removed** - from the global-picture passage, the objection-and-reply, the revision notes, the register notes and the maps. The July 2025 global totals are retained with their date, the distribution and capacity argument is made **without any national ranking**, and **no later global aggregate is asserted** |
| A **current, authoritative, dated global total** for the List of Intangible Cultural Heritage in Need of Urgent Safeguarding | the printed figure was dated but uncorroborated, and the lesson's central argument turns on India's absence from that list | **Not resolved, and the figure is therefore removed entirely.** The global figures in circulation disagree with one another and none could be confirmed from an authoritative dated source. **No global total for that list is carried anywhere in this session**, in the visual, the teaching, the revision notes, the register notes, the models or the maps. India's absence from the list is retained and remains safe, because a negative of that kind does not depend on the size of the list it is absent from |

**(c) One bounded current linkage.** Exactly one is claimed, file-wide, in **Lesson 13**: the inscription of the **Ancient Buddhist Site of Sarnath on 25 July 2026**. Every other pre-teach checklist in this session records the absence of a sought linkage truthfully. The **Deepavali** inscription of 10 December 2025, treated in Lesson 14, is used as a **dated record carried by the canonical owner**, not as a second current linkage.
